Exotic traveling waves for a quasilinear Schrödinger equation with nonzero background
André de Laire, Erwan Le Quiniou

Abstract
We study a defocusing quasilinear Schrödinger equation with nonzero conditions at infinity in dimension one. This quasilinear model corresponds to a weakly nonlocal approximation of the nonlocal Gross–Pitaevskii equation, and can also be derived by considering the effects of surface tension in superfluids. When the quasilinear term is neglected, the resulting equation is the classical Gross–Pitaevskii equation, which possesses a well-known stable branch of subsonic traveling waves solution, given by dark solitons. Our goal is to investigate how the quasilinear term and the intensity-dependent dispersion affect the traveling-waves solutions. We provide a complete classification of finite energy traveling waves of the equation, in terms of two parameters: the speed and the strength of the quasilinear term. This classification leads to the existence of dark and antidark solitons, as well as more exotic localized solutions like dark cuspons, compactons, and composite waves, even for supersonic speeds. Depending on the parameters, these types of solutions can coexist, showing that finite energy solutions are not unique. Furthermore, we prove that some of these dark solitons can be obtained as minimizers of the energy, at fixed momentum, and that they are orbitally stable.



Keywords:

Quasilinear Schrödinger equation, Gross–Pitaevskii equation, traveling waves, dark solitons, dark cuspons, nonzero conditions at infinity, orbital stability.

2020
MSC
:
35Q55, 35J62, 35C07, 35C08, 34A05, 35J20, 35B35, 35D35, 35Q60, 82D50, 34A12.

1
Introduction

1.1
The quasilinear equation and related models

We consider the following defocusing quasilinear Gross–Pitaevskii equation in dimension one

i{\partial}_{t}\Psi={\partial}_{xx}\Psi+\Psi(1-|\Psi|^{2})+\kappa\Psi{\partial}_{xx}(1-|\Psi|^{2}),\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},

(QGP)

where
\kappa
is a real parameter, and
\Psi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, satisfies the nonzero conditions at infinity

\lim_{|x|\to\infty}|\Psi(x,\cdot)|=1,

(1.1)

representing a (normalized) nonzero background.

Let us notice that the equation is recast as in (
QGP
) for practical purposes. Indeed, we can write a more general Schrödinger equation as

i{\partial}_{t}\Phi={\partial}_{xx}\Phi+\mathfrak{s}\Phi\big(|\Phi|^{2}+\kappa{\partial}_{xx}|\Phi|^{2})).

(1.2)

When
\kappa=0,
it corresponds to the cubic NLS equation, a classical model for Bose–Einstein condensates, superfluidity, and nonlinear optical fibers, depending on the sign of
\mathfrak{s}
, and on the background conditions
[
27
,
36
]
.
For instance, in Bose–Einstein condensates, the term in
\mathfrak{s}
models attractive interatomic interactions if
\mathfrak{s}>0
,
and repulsive interactions if
\mathfrak{s}<0
. In nonlinear optics, it corresponds to the Kerr effect in a focusing fiber if
\mathfrak{s}>0
, and to ionization effects in a defocusing one if
\mathfrak{s}<0
. Equation (
1.2
) enables the description of significant and experimentally relevant nonlinear phenomena such as
solitons
. Solitons are particular types of solutions that travel with constant speed and with a profile that remains unchanged. They provide important information for the analysis of dispersive equations. Although the most common solitons are bright and dark solitons, there are more exotic solitons dispersive PDEs, that are not smooth, such as the cupsons and compactons
[
51
,
47
,
2
,
46
,
28
,
31
,
58
]
, which we will discuss below.

Bright solitons are characterized by having a localized amplitude peak, rapidly decaying to zero. The existence and properties of these solutions are a classical subject for the focusing NLS equation, that has received much attention
[
56
,
17
,
57
]
in the case
\kappa=0
. In the case
\kappa\geq 0
,
Krówlikowski and Bang obtained in
[
40
]
an explicit formula for the bright solitons to (
1.2
) with
\mathfrak{s}=1
, taking the form of a standing wave
\Phi(x,t)=v_{\omega,\kappa}(x)e^{-i\omega t},
for every
\omega>0
,
with
v_{\omega,\kappa}
a real-valued profile given by
v_{\omega,\kappa}(x)=F_{\omega,\kappa}^{-1}(|x|)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, where

F_{\omega,\kappa}(y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega}}\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega}}\sqrt{\frac{2\omega-y^{2}}{1+2\kappa y^{2}}}\Big)+2\sqrt{\kappa}\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{2\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2\omega-y^{2}}{1+2\kappa y^{2}}}\Big).

(1.3)

These bright solitons are unique for up to invariances (translation by a constant and multiplication by a phase shift).
Letting
\kappa\to 0
in (
1.3
), we recover the profile of the cubic NLS bright soliton

v_{\omega,0}(x)=\sqrt{2\omega}\operatorname{sech}(\sqrt{\omega}x).

On the one hand, Colin, Jeanjean, and Squassina
[
16
]
showed the existence of bright solitons for (
1.2
), with
\mathfrak{s}=1
and
\kappa>0
, in any dimension. Moreover, using formula (
1.3
) in the one-dimensional case, the results in
[
16
]
imply that these bright solitons are orbitally stable solutions to (
1.2
). We also refer to Iliev and Kirchev
[
34
]
who showed existence of bright solitons and a stability criterion for equations similar to (
1.2
) with
\mathfrak{s}=1
in one dimension, with more general nonlinearities.

On the other hand, in the defocusing case, there are no bright solitons, but the existence of dark solitons is expected
[
38
]
. Dark solitons have a localized amplitude dip or notch (of their absolute value) on a nonzero background density. Although these solutions are physically relevant, they have been less studied in the literature. They can be obtained explicitly for the cubic defocusing NLS equation (i.e. (
1.2
) with
\mathfrak{s}=-1
and
\kappa=0
), and they are given, up to invariances, by

\Phi(x,t)=u_{c}(x-ct)e^{it},

for
c\in[0,\sqrt{2}),
where

u_{c}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}}{2}}\tanh\Big(\frac{\sqrt{2-c^{2}}}{2}x\Big)-i\frac{c}{\sqrt{2}}.

(1.4)

Notice that this branch of dark solitons satisfies the nonzero background condition
|\Phi(x,\cdot)|\to 1
, as
|x|\to\infty
. We refer to
[
7
,
10
]
for more details and stability results for these dark solitons, and to
[
13
]
for some generalizations.

Since dark solitons can only exist in the defocusing case, from now on, we only consider the case
\mathfrak{s}=-1
. To avoid the dependence on
t
of the solitary waves, it is usual to perform the change of variables

\Psi(x,t)=\Phi(x,t)e^{-it},

which transforms equation (
1.2
) into (
QGP
), and so that the nontrivial condition at infinity appears more explicitly in the equation.

The study of generalizations of Schrödinger equations with intensity-dependent dispersion, has recently gained significant attention,
mostly in the context of solutions vanishing at infinity
[
52
,
37
,
49
]
.
We refer to the work of Pelinovsky and Plum
[
48
]
for the study of the stability of the black soliton for an intensity-dependent dispersion Schrödinger equation.

Given the increasing interest in intensity-dependent dispersion equations and quasilinear Gross-Pitaevskii equations, our aim is to provide a complete classification of finite energy traveling waves of the equation, and the stability of some of them.

We end this subsection by giving some physical motivations for the quasilinear model (
QGP
), with the nonzero condition (
1.1
). The evolution of a one-dimensional optical beam of intensity
|{\Psi}|^{2}
in a defocusing nonlocal Kerr-like medium is given by the nonlocal Gross–Pitaevskii equation

i\partial_{t}\Psi=\partial_{xx}\Psi+\Psi(\mathcal{W}*(1-|\Psi|^{2})),

(1.5)

where
\mathcal{W}
characterizes the nonlocal response of the medium
[
45
,
40
,
18
,
19
]
. As explained in
[
40
,
39
]
, in a weakly nonlocal medium we can replace
\mathcal{W}
by
\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)=\mathcal{W}(\cdot/\varepsilon)/\varepsilon
, for a small positive
\varepsilon
. Then,
performing a Taylor expansion of
\eta(x-y)=(1-|\Psi|^{2})(x-y,t)
near
x
, for fixed
t
, we have

\eta(x-y)=\eta(x)-y\eta^{\prime}(x)+\frac{y^{2}}{2}\eta^{\prime\prime}(x)+{O}(y^{3}),

hence, using that
\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}
is even, the convolution product in (
1.5
) can be formally computed as

(\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}*\eta)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon}(y)\eta(x-y)dy=\eta(x)+\kappa_{\varepsilon}\eta^{\prime\prime}(x)+O(\varepsilon^{3}),\text{ with }\kappa_{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}y^{2}\mathcal{W}(y).

(1.6)

Therefore, equation (
QGP
) follows from (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), in the regime
\varepsilon
small, neglecting the term
O(\varepsilon^{3})
.
For this reason, (
QGP
) is known as the weakly nonlocal Gross–Pitaevskii equation in the nonlinear optics literature.

Formally, we can also see a connection between (
1.5
) and (
QGP
)
by considering the potential given with its Fourier transform

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\kappa}(\xi)=1-\kappa\xi^{2},

(1.7)

that can be seen as a limit case for the results of the existence of dark solitons for (
1.5
) (see Remark
1.14
).

In addition, (
QGP
) was also obtained using the least action principle by
Kurihara in
[
41
]
, in order to describe a superfluid
{}^{4}\rm{He}
film, where
\kappa\leq 0
and

|\kappa|
measures the surface tension of the superfluid.

1.2
Classification of finite energy traveling waves

Equation (
QGP
) has a Hamiltonian structure, and its energy, given by

E_{\kappa}(\Psi(\cdot,t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|{\partial}_{x}\Psi(x,t)|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(1-|\Psi(x,t)|^{2}\Big)^{2}dx-\frac{\kappa}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big({\partial}_{x}|\Psi(x,t)|^{2}\big)^{2}dx,

(1.8)

is formally conserved, as well as the (renormalized) momentum

p(\Psi(\cdot,t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\langle i{\partial}_{x}\Psi(x,t),\Psi(x,t)\rangle}\Big(1-\frac{1}{|\Psi(x,t)|^{2}}\Big)dx,

(1.9)

whenever
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\Psi(x,t)|>0
, where we used the inner product
\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle=\operatorname{Re}(z_{1})\operatorname{Re}(z_{2})+\operatorname{Im}(z_{1})\operatorname{Im}(z_{2})
, for
z_{1}
,
z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
.

We are interested in solutions to (
QGP
) of the form

\Psi_{c}(x,t)=u(x-ct),

which represents a traveling wave with profile
u:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
propagating at speed
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence, the profile
u
satisfies

icu^{\prime}+u^{\prime\prime}+u(1-|u|^{2})+\kappa u\big(1-|u|^{2}\big)^{\prime\prime}=0.

(TW
(c,\kappa)
)

Notice that taking the complex conjugate of
u
in equation (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), we are reduced to the case
c\geq 0
. To study physically relevant solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in function of
\kappa
, we define
energy space

\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})=\{v\in H^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}):1-|v|^{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\ v^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}.

Let us recall that
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})
, and that any function in
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies the nontrivial condition at infinity (
1.1
)
(see Lemma
2.1
).
We will use extensively, as a new variable, the
intensity profile
of
u
, given by

\eta_{u}=1-|u|^{2},

so that the condition at infinity (
1.1
) becomes
\eta_{u}(x)\to 0
, as
|x|\to\infty
. Omitting the subscript of
\eta_{u}
for notational simplicity, we can recast the energy functional as

E_{\kappa}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta^{2}-\frac{\kappa}{4}(\eta^{\prime})^{2},\quad\text{ for all }u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}).

(1.10)

Moreover, the momentum is well-defined in the nonvanishing energy space defined by

\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})=\{v\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}):\inf_{\mathbb{R}}|v|>0\},

(1.11)

and writing the lifting
u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
(see Lemma
2.2
), we have

p(u)=-\frac{1}{2}\int\langle iu^{\prime},u\rangle\frac{\eta}{1-\eta}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta\theta^{\prime}.

(1.12)

Furthermore, the energy can be written in this case as

E_{\kappa}(u)=\frac{1}{8}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{(\eta^{\prime})^{2}(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)}{1-\eta}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1-\eta)(\theta^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta^{2}.

(1.13)

We can check that the energy space
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is equal to the domain of
E_{\kappa}
if
\kappa\leq 0
, and is strictly included in the domain of
E_{\kappa}
if
\kappa>0
.

To be more precise, we say that
\Psi\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};H^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}))
is a
weak solution
to (
QGP
) if, for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(\mathbb{R}))
,

\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\langle{i\Psi},{{\partial}_{t}\varphi}\rangle dxdt=\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\big(\langle\partial_{x}\Psi,{\partial_{x}\varphi}\rangle-(1-|\Psi|^{2})\langle\Psi,\varphi\rangle+\kappa\partial_{x}(1-|\Psi|^{2})\partial_{x}\langle\Psi,\varphi\rangle\big)dxdt.

(1.14)

Hence, we can check that
\Psi(x,t)=u(x-ct)
, with
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a weak solution to (
QGP
)
if and only if for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle icu^{\prime}+u(1-|u|^{2}),\phi\rangle-\langle u^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}\rangle+2\kappa\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle\langle u,\phi\rangle^{\prime}=0,

(1.15)

where we used that
(1-|u|^{2})^{\prime}=-2\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle
. Therefore, (
1.15
) corresponds to the weak formulation of (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

To obtain analytical solutions, we will show that if
u
is a finite energy solution to (
1.15
), then its intensity profile
\eta=1-|u|^{2}
satisfies the ODE

(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)(\eta^{\prime})^{2}=\eta^{2}(2-c^{2}-2\eta),

(1.16)

as long as
u
is smooth enough. Thus, the rest of the analysis relies on the study of possible singularities, combined with the computation of the primitives of

\displaystyle f(y)=-y\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}},

according to the parameters
c
and
\kappa
. As explained in Section
3
,
taking
x
large enough in (
1.16
), we expect that

0<(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa),

(1.17)

is a necessary condition for the existence of nontrivial solutions. From (
1.17
), we see that
c=\sqrt{2}
and
\kappa=1/2
are critical values, as shown below in our results.
In fact, the value
c=\sqrt{2}
corresponds to the speed of sound for (
1.2
) (see the explanations in
[
19
,
21
]
applied to (
1.7
)), while the value
\kappa=1/2
corresponds to the critical case, for which the linear dispersion and the nonlinear disperse
on (i.e. the quasilinear terms of order 2) cancel as the intensity
|\Psi|^{2}
approaches
1

[
2
,
46
,
42
]
.

To explain our results, we split the set of parameters according to the critical values, as represented in Figure
1
, by defining the following regions

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(c,\kappa):0\leq c<\sqrt{2}\text{ and }0<\kappa<1/2\},\ \mathcal{D}_{2}=\{(c,\kappa):0\leq c<\sqrt{2}\text{ and }\kappa\leq 0\},

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{(c,\kappa):c>\sqrt{2}\text{ and }\kappa>1/2\},\ \text{ and, }\ \mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3};

(1.19)

the boundary sets associated with the sonic speed

\mathcal{B}_{-}=\{(c,\kappa):c=\sqrt{2}\text{ and }0<\kappa<1/2\},\quad\mathcal{B}_{+}=\{(c,\kappa):c=\sqrt{2}\text{ and }\kappa>1/2\},

(1.20)

and the boundary set associated with the critical value for
\kappa

\mathcal{C}=\{(c,\kappa):c\geq 0\text{ and }\kappa=1/2\}.

(1.21)

\begin{overpic}[figures/domains]
\end{overpic}

Figure 1:
Sets of parameters to classify the solutions of (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

Our main classification result shows the existence of multiple branches of localized traveling waves indexed by
\kappa
and
c
, that can coexist at fixed parameters, showing the nonuniqueness of finite energy solutions. Before stating our results, we give some vague definitions used in the literature, to understand the nature of these different types of solitons
u
for (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). The adjective
dark
refers to the nonzero constant background condition
|u(x)|\to 1
, as
|x|\to\infty
. Moreover,
u
is a:

(i)

dark soliton
if
u
is smooth and
|u|^{2}
has a localized dip.

(ii)

antidark soliton
if
u
is smooth and
|u|^{2}
has a localized bump.

(iii)

black soliton
if
u
is smooth and
u
vanishes at some point.

(iv)

dark cuspon
if
u
is continuous and has a localized cusped dip,
u^{\prime}
is unbounded at the cusp but is smooth elsewhere.

(v)

antidark cuspon
if
u
is continuous and has a localized cusped bump,
u^{\prime}
is unbounded at the bump but is smooth elsewhere.

(vi)

compacton
if
u
is continuous and
1-|u|^{2}
is compactly supported.

(vii)

composite wave
if
u
is continuous,
built using pieces of the solitons mentioned above at different intervals, and the gluing is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}
.

With these formal definitions at hand, we can schematically summarize our results on the existence and uniqueness (up to invariances)
of nontrivial finite energy solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), in the following table.

In
\mathcal{D}_{1}

There exist a unique
dark soliton
, a unique
antidark cuspon
,

and
composite waves
.

In
\mathcal{D}_{2}

There exists a unique
dark soliton
.

In
\mathcal{D}_{3}

There exist a unique
antidark soliton
, a unique
dark cuspon
,

and
composite waves
.

In
\mathcal{B}_{-}

There exist a unique
antidark cuspon
, and
composite waves
.

In
\mathcal{B}_{+}

There exist a unique
dark cuspon
, and
composite waves
.

In
\mathcal{C}

There exist
composite waves
built from
compactons
.

Elsewhere

There is no nontrivial finite energy traveling wave.

To give explicit formulas for the dark and antidark soliton solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), which we will denote by
u_{c,\kappa}
,
we introduce the following intervals

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{c}=(0,1-c^{2}/2],\quad\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ}=(0,1-c^{2}/2)\quad\text{ if }c<\sqrt{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{c}=[1-c^{2}/2,0),\quad\mathcal{J}_{c}^{\circ}=(1-c^{2}/2,0)\quad\text{ if }c>\sqrt{2}.

Indeed, these intervals will correspond to the image set of
\eta_{c,\kappa}:=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
.
Then, we will check that the following functions are well-defined depending on the set of parameters (
1.18
)–(
1.19
) (see Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
).
(i) For
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, we set
F_{c,\kappa}:\mathcal{I}_{c}\to\mathbb{R}
the function defined, for all
y\in\mathcal{I}_{c}
, by

\displaystyle F_{c,\kappa}(y)=2\sqrt{\kappa}\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)+2\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa}{2-c^{2}}}\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2}-2y)}{(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}}\Big).

(1.22)

(ii)
For
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
, we set
G_{c,\kappa}:\mathcal{I}_{c}\to\mathbb{R}
the function defined, for all
y\in\mathcal{I}_{c}
, by

G_{c,\kappa}(y)=-2\sqrt{-\kappa}\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{-\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)+2\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa}{2-c^{2}}}\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2}-2y)}{(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}}\Big).

(1.23)

(iii)
For
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
, we set
H_{c,\kappa}:\mathcal{J}_{c}\to\mathbb{R}
the function defined, for all
y\in\mathcal{J}_{c}
, by

H_{c,\kappa}(y)=F_{c,\kappa}(y).

(1.24)

We will prove that the functions (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) are injective and take their values in
(0,\infty)
. Thus, we can define their inverse as follows

\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}=F^{-1}_{c,\kappa},\text{ for }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1};\ \mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}=G^{-1}_{c,\kappa},\text{ for }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2};\ \mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}=H^{-1}_{c,\kappa},\text{ for }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}.

(1.25)

Also, we can extend
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa},

\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}
to even
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-functions on
\mathbb{R}
, which we still denote by
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa},

\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}
.

We can now state our first classification result for
smooth
solutions.
Using the sets defined in (
1.18
)–(
1.19
), we first show that the only finite energy smooth solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in
\mathcal{D}^{c}
are the trivial ones, i.e. the constants of modulus one. Then, for
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
,
the description of dark solitons is explicitly provided in terms of
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}
, while antidark solitons are described by
\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}
. Moreover, we establish their uniqueness among smooth solutions, up to invariances, i.e. up to a translation and a phase shift.

Theorem 1.1
.

(i)

If
(c,\kappa)\notin\mathcal{D}
and
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), then there exists
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
such that
u_{c,\kappa}(x)=e^{i\phi}
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

For
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
, we set

\eta_{c,\kappa}=\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa},\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1};\ \eta_{c,\kappa}=\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa},\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2};\ \eta_{c,\kappa}=\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa},\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}.

(1.26)

If
c>0
, then
\eta_{c,\kappa}<1
on
\mathbb{R}
, and
u_{c,\kappa}=\rho_{c,\kappa}e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}}

is the unique nontrivial solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, up to invariances, where

\rho_{c,\kappa}(x)=\sqrt{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\theta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}dy.

(1.27)

If
c=0
, then the real-valued odd function

\displaystyle u_{0,\kappa}(x)=\pm\sqrt{1-\eta_{0,\kappa}(x)},\quad\text{ for all }\pm x\geq 0,

(1.28)

is the unique nontrivial solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, up to invariances.

In addition, for any
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
,
u_{c,\kappa}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}
is even, reaching a global extremum at the origin with

\eta_{c,\kappa}(0)=1-c^{2}/2.

(1.29)

Moreover, we have
\eta_{c,\kappa}>0
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}<0
on
(0,\infty)
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}
, while
\eta_{c,\kappa}<0
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}>0
on
(0,\infty)
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
. Finally, there exist constants
A,C>0
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the following exponential decay holds

\displaystyle|D^{k}u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x)|+|D^{k}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)|\leq Ae^{-C|x|},\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.30)

Notice that (
1.29
) implies that the solutions
u_{c,\kappa}
in Theorem
1.1
correspond to dark solitons that do not vanish if
c\neq 0
(gray solitons) and to black solitons if
c=0
.

\end{overpic}

\end{overpic}

\par\end{overpic}

Figure 2:
Plot of intensity profiles
\eta_{c,k}
in Theorem
1.1
for several values of
(c,\kappa)
.
The left panel displays profiles for
(1,0),(1,0.2),(1,0.4)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
associated with dark solitons.
In the center, profiles for
(1,-50),(1,-5),(1,0)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
associated also with dark solitons.
On the right, profiles for
(2,0.6),(2,1),(2,5)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
, associated with antidark solitons.

In Figure
2
, we depict the intensity profile
\eta_{c,k}
given by
(
1.26
) in Theorem
1.1

for several values of
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
. In the left and center panels, we plotted
\eta_{c,\kappa}
for parameters
in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, respectively, associated with dark solitons;
while plots for parameters in
\mathcal{D}_{3}
, associated with antidark solitons, are shown in the right panel. The case
\kappa=0
corresponds to the dark soliton (
1.4
), so that the intensity profile is given by

\eta_{c,0}(x)=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}=\frac{(2-c^{2})}{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}\Big(\frac{\sqrt{2-c^{2}}}{2}x\Big),\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

From the left and center panel, we deduce that, at a fixed speed, the profile wavelength of the dark soliton narrows when
\kappa
increases from
-\infty
to
1/2
. We notice the same effect for the antidark solitons in the right panel when
\kappa
decreases from
\infty
to
1/2
.

In addition, we show in Proposition
3.8
smooth dependence of the dark soliton
u_{c,\kappa}
,
with respect to
(c,\kappa)
in
\mathcal{D}
. Consequently, the dark solitons
u_{c,\kappa}
in Theorem
1.1
converge to the dark soliton (
1.4
),
as
\kappa\to 0
, in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
.

Notice that the uniqueness stated in Theorem
1.1
is in the set
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. This restriction is due to the existence of finite energy weak solutions that are not of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
in the new set of parameters

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}\cup\mathcal{B}_{-}\cup\mathcal{B}_{+},

and also in
\mathcal{C}
.
We argue that these sets are well-suited for studying weak solutions.
Indeed, the next result shows that there are no solutions outside
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\,\cup\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
c\geq 0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
If
(c,\kappa)\notin(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\,\cup\mathcal{C})
, then
either
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
so that it is explicitly given by Theorem
1.1
, or

u_{c,\kappa}
is a constant function of modulus one.

We call a weak solution that is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, a singular solution. The rest of this subsection is devoted to explaining our results for singular solutions. We show that if
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a singular solution, then the function
|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
must reach the value
1/(2\kappa)
at some point. Thus, we call the singular set of
u_{c,\kappa}
the nonempty set given by

\displaystyle\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})=\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|u_{c,\kappa}(x)|^{2}=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\Big\}.

(1.31)

This condition comes from the following observation:
Given a solution
u_{c,\kappa}=u_{1}+iu_{2}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), with
u_{1}=\operatorname{Re}(u_{c,\kappa})
,
u_{2}=\operatorname{Im}(u_{c,\kappa})
,
and recalling that
u_{c,\kappa}
is continuous (see Lemma
2.1
),
we can show that (
1.15
) can be recast
as a system of two
real
equations, satisfied in the weak sense,

A(u_{c,\kappa})\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix}^{\prime\prime}=c\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{pmatrix}^{\prime}+(\eta_{c,\kappa}-2\kappa|u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}|^{2})\begin{pmatrix}u_{2}\\
-u_{1}\end{pmatrix},\text{ with }A(u_{c,\kappa})=\begin{pmatrix}2\kappa u_{2}u_{1}&-1+2\kappa u_{2}^{2}\\
1-2\kappa u_{1}^{2}&-2\kappa u_{1}u_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(1.32)

so that

\det(A(u))=1-2\kappa|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}=1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa}.

(1.33)

Thus, the set
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
corresponds to the points where
\det(A(u_{c,\kappa}))

vanishes, so that the dispersion part of equation (
1.32
) is singular.
In this manner, as proved in Lemma
4.1
,
u_{c,\kappa}
is smooth on the complement of
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
, denoted by

\Omega(u_{c,\kappa})=\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})^{c}
. Also, if we define the set of points where

u_{c,\kappa}
is not differentiable (in the classical sense)

\mathcal{N}(u_{c,\kappa})=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:u_{c,\kappa}\text{ is not differentiable in }x\},

then
\mathcal{N}(u_{c,\kappa})\subseteq\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
. We want to focus now on the case where
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
is not empty and bounded so that we can define the real numbers

\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}=\inf\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}=\sup\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}).

(1.34)

For instance, by condition (
1.1
), the set
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
is bounded for any singular solution in the case
\kappa\neq 1/2
. To give explicit formulas for singular solutions, we use the same approach as in Theorem
1.1
, introducing
the intervals

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=[1-1/(2\kappa),0),\quad\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}^{\circ}=(1-1/(2\kappa),0),\quad\text{ if }\kappa\in(0,1/2),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=(0,1-1/(2\kappa)],\quad\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}^{\circ}=(0,1-1/(2\kappa)),\quad\text{ if }\kappa\in(1/2,\infty),

and the following functions (see Lemma
4.4
).

(i) For
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, we set
f_{c,\kappa}:\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the function defined, for all
y\in\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}
, by

\displaystyle f_{c,\kappa}(y)=2\sqrt{\kappa}\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)+2\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa}{2-c^{2}}}\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2}-2y)}}\Big)-\pi\sqrt{\kappa}.

(1.35)

(ii) For
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
, we set
h_{c,\kappa}:\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the function defined, for all
y\in\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}
, by

\displaystyle h_{c,\kappa}(y)=f_{c,\kappa}(y).

(1.36)

(iii) For
(\sqrt{2},\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{-}
, we set
g_{\kappa}:\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the function defined, for all
y\in\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}
, by

\displaystyle g_{\kappa}(y)=2\sqrt{\kappa}\operatorname{atan}(\sqrt{\frac{-2\kappa y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}})+\sqrt{2}\sqrt{-\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{y}}-\pi\sqrt{\kappa},

(1.37)

(iv) For
(\sqrt{2},\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{+}
, we set
\tilde{g}_{\kappa}:\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the function defined, for all
y\in\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}
, by

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{\kappa}(y)=g_{\kappa}(y).

(1.38)

By Lemma
4.4
, the functions (
1.35
)–(
1.38
) are injective and positive-valued, and their inverse functions

f_{c,\kappa}^{-1}
,
h_{c,\kappa}^{-1}
,
g_{\kappa}^{-1}
,
\tilde{g}_{\kappa}^{-1}

are well-defined on
(0,\infty)
. Notice that the functions
f_{c,\kappa}^{-1}
and
g_{\kappa}^{-1}
will describe antidark cuspons, while
h_{c,\kappa}^{-1}
and
\tilde{g}_{\kappa}^{-1}
will describe dark cuspons (see Figure
3
). Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
and
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
be a singular solution, so that
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}
given by (
1.34
) are reals. The next result establishes that
u_{c,\kappa}

is smooth outside the interval
[\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}]
, that

\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}
belong to
\mathcal{N}(u_{c,\kappa})
,
and that
u_{c,\kappa}
is explicitly given by one of the functions in (
1.35
)–(
1.38
), i.e. that it has a dark or antidark cuspon profile on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}]
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, if
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a singular solution for (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) so that
u_{c,\kappa}
is nontrivial and the critical set
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
is not empty and bounded.
Let
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}
be defined as in (
1.34
) and assume without loss of generality that
\mathfrak{b}=0
. Then
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
satisfies, for all
x\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=f_{c,\kappa}^{-1}(x),\ \text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1},\quad\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=h_{c,\kappa}^{-1}(x),\ \text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3},

(1.39)

\displaystyle\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=g_{\kappa}^{-1}(x),\ \text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{-},\quad\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\tilde{g}_{\kappa}^{-1}(x),\ \text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{+}.

For all
x\leq\mathfrak{a}
,
\eta_{c,\kappa}
is obtained by reflection as
\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(\mathfrak{a}-x)
.
Additionally, for all
x\in(-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup(0,\infty)
, we have

1-\frac{1}{2\kappa}<\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)<0,\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{B}_{-},\quad\text{ whereas }\quad 0<\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)<1-\frac{1}{2\kappa},\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}\cup\mathcal{B}_{+},

and

u_{c,\kappa}
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle u_{c,\kappa}(x)=\sqrt{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)}e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}(x)},\quad\text{ with }\quad\theta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x)=c\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)/(2-2\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)).

(1.40)

Also,

u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup(0,\infty)))\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})
.
Moreover,

\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}^{-})=-\infty
and

\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(0^{+})=\infty,
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{B}_{-},
while
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}^{-})=\infty
and

\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(0^{+})=-\infty,

if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}\cup\mathcal{B}_{+}.

Finally
u_{c,\kappa}
satisfies the following decay estimates: for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
such that, for all
x\geq 1,

|D^{j}u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x)|+|D^{j}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)|\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}x}
, if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}
, and

|D^{j}u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x)|+|D^{j}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)|\leq C_{3}x^{-(2+j)}
,
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{+}\cup\mathcal{B}_{-}
.

In Figure
3
, we place ourselves in the case
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}=\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}=0
to illustrate the antidark cuspon intensity profile given by the function
f^{-1}_{c,\kappa}
, and the dark cuspon intensity profile given by
h^{-1}_{c,\kappa}
.
We show in Lemma
4.5
that if
\kappa=1/2
, then
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}=-\infty
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}=\infty
, so that there are infinitely many points in
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
.
Therefore, Theorem
1.3
allows us to complete the analysis if
u_{c,\kappa}
has only one singular point, as explained in the following result.

Corollary 1.4

(Cuspons)
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
. Assume that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a nontrivial solution for (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) such that
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})=\{0\}
.
Then, up to phase shift, the solution
u_{c,\kappa}
is explicitly given by
u_{c,\kappa}=\sqrt{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}}e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}},

where
\eta_{c,\kappa}
is the function in (
1.39
),
and
\theta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}
. In particular
\mathcal{N}(u_{c,\kappa})=\{0\}
.

\begin{overpic}[figures/SolitonWD1]
\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[figures/SolitonWD3]
\end{overpic}

Figure 3:
Plots of the antidark cuspon intensity profile in Corollary
1.4
. The left panel displays antidark cuspons for the parameters
(0,0.4)
,
(1,0.4)
and
(1.3,0.4)
in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. On the right, dark cuspons for the parameters
(1.5,0.6)
,
(2,0.6)
and
(5,0.6)
in
\mathcal{D}_{3}
.

with

Classification of solutions with two or more singular points

We have provided the classification of solutions in the cases
\mathrm{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))=0
and
\mathrm{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))=1
. Now we will focus on the case

\mathrm{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))\geq 2,

i.e. when
u_{c,\kappa}
has at least two singular points, and we do not require
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
to be bounded anymore. Between two singular points
a,

b\in\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
, the function
|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
may exhibit one of the following behaviors:

(i)

It reaches a non-singular local extremum at some
x_{0}\in(a,b)
, so that
|u_{c,\kappa}(x_{0})|^{2}\neq 1/(2\kappa)
.

(ii)

It remains constant so that
|u_{c,\kappa}(x)|^{2}=1/(2\kappa)
, for all
x\in(a,b)
.

Assume that case
(i)
holds, so that there exists
x_{0}\in(a,b)
belonging to the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}):x\text{ is a local extremum of }1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}\}.

(1.41)

When
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, we know, by Theorem
1.3
, that
\eta_{c,\kappa}
coincides with a cuspon-like solution outside the bounded set
(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa})
, so that it is monotonous on
\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa})
. Therefore, we deduce that
\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})\subset(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa})
.
In conclusion, for every
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}},

we can define the closest singular points to any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
as the real numbers

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa}=\sup\{x\in[\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},x_{0}):x\in\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa}=\inf\{x\in(x_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}]:x\in\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})\}.

(1.42)

In the case,
\kappa=1/2
, by Lemma
4.5
, we have
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}=-\infty
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}=\infty
, and we can still define
\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa}
as
above, with the obvious modifications.
In particular, for any
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cap\mathcal{C}
, by Lemma
4.1
,
u_{c,\kappa}
is smooth on
(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
, and
\eta^{\prime}_{c,\kappa}(x_{0})=0
. Letting
\eta_{0}=\eta_{c,\kappa}(x_{0})
, we establish in Lemma
4.6
that there exist
K_{0}\geq 0
and
K_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\eta=\eta_{c,\kappa}
satisfies the following ODEs in
(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
:

\displaystyle 2(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)\eta^{\prime\prime}+2\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=P(\eta)+(\eta-\eta_{0})P^{\prime}(\eta),

(1.43)

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)(\eta^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=(\eta-\eta_{0})P(\eta),

(1.44)

where

P(y)=-2y^{2}+(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})y+(2-c^{2})\eta_{0}-4K_{0}-4cK_{1},\quad\text{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.45)

Using (
1.43
)–(
1.44
), we establish that for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
, the function
\eta_{c,\kappa}
must be symmetric on

(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
with respect to
x_{0}
, and strictly monotone on
(x_{0},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
,
as follows.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
. Assume that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) and let
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
. Suppose that
\mathrm{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))\geq 2
and that there exists
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
, and consider its closest singular points

\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa}
, as in (
1.42
). Then

\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa}=2x_{0}-\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa}
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(2x_{0}-x)
, for all
x\in(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},x_{0})
.
Moreover, if
0<|u_{c,\kappa}(x_{0})|^{2}<1/(2\kappa)
, then
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}<0
in
(x_{0},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
, while if

|u_{c,\kappa}(x_{0})|^{2}>1/(2\kappa)
, then
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}>0
in
(x_{0},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
.

This monotonicity property implies that if
u_{c,\kappa}
vanishes at some points, then these points must be isolated. We deduce the following result that simplifies the analysis. It guarantees that the solutions are nonvanishing if
c>0
, while if
c=0
, the problem (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) reduces to one real differential equation.

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
and
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
be a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). If
c>0
, then
u\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
. On the other hand, if
c=0
, then there exists
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
such that
e^{i\phi}u_{c,\kappa}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

An important remark is that, using
(\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime})^{2}\geq 0
, equation (
1.44
) yields algebraic constraints on
P
. For instance, we establish in the proof of the Theorem
1.5
that
P(\eta_{c,\kappa})<0
, for all
x\in[x_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
, thus, using the monotonicity of
\eta_{c,\kappa}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle-2y^{2}+(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})y+(2-c^{2})\eta_{0}-4K_{0}-4cK_{1}<0,\text{ for all }y\in[\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)).

(1.46)

Also, if
u_{c,\kappa}
is a singular solution, then we infer that
K_{0}=|u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x_{0})|^{2}
.
When
\eta_{0}<1
, we show in Lemma
4.7
that
K_{1}=0
so that, (
1.46
) reduces to

-2y^{2}+(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})y+(2-c^{2})\eta_{0}-c^{2}\eta_{0}^{2}/(1-\eta_{0})<0,\text{ for all }y\in[\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)),

(1.47)

whereas if
\eta_{0}=1
(and thus
c=0
), then (
1.46
) becomes

y^{2}+2K_{0}-1>0,\text{ for all }y\in(1-1/(2\kappa),1].

(1.48)

Returning to case
(ii)
, the obtained solution, which remains constant between two singular points
a<b
is sometimes referred to as a stumpon
[
42
]
. However, the following proposition establishes that case
(ii)
cannot occur unless
\kappa=1/2
, in which case we have
|u(x)|^{2}=1
, for all
x\in(a,b)
.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
. Assume that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). If there exist real numbers
a<b
such that

|u_{c,\kappa}(x)|^{2}=1/(2\kappa),\quad\text{ for all }x\in(a,b),

(1.49)

then
\kappa=1/2
.

In view of Proposition
1.7
, one might expect that case
(ii)
implies that
u_{c,\kappa}
is a constant function on
\mathbb{R}
, with
|u_{c,\kappa}|\equiv 1
. However, the critical case
\kappa=1/2
is degenerate, and there are many more possibilities for the solutions. The most remarkable singular solutions are dark compactons, i.e. such that the intensity profile
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
has compact support; some of them are explicitly given in Proposition
1.9
.

So far we ruled out case
(ii)
, and obtained, in case
(i)
, the necessary conditions (
1.47
)–(
1.48
).
We show in Propositions
4.11
–
4.13
that these conditions are, in fact, sufficient to construct local solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
More precisely, if
\eta_{0}<1
is such that (
1.47
) holds, then there exists
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0}<\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
and a unique
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{2}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))\cap H^{1}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
satisfying (
1.43
)–(
1.44
) with
\eta(0)=\eta_{0}
and
K_{0}=(c\eta_{0})^{2}/(4-4\eta_{0})
so that

u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta},\quad\text{ with }\quad\theta^{\prime}=\frac{c\eta}{2(1-\eta)},

(1.50)

is a
\mathcal{C}^{2}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
-solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
Similarly, if
\eta_{0}=1
and
K_{0}\geq 0
is such that (
1.48
) holds, then there exists
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0}<\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
and a unique
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{2}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))\cap H^{1}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
satisfying (
1.43
)–(
1.44
) with
\eta(0)=1
so that

u(x)=\pm\sqrt{1-\eta(x)},\quad\text{ for all }\pm x\in[0,\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0}),

(1.51)

is a
\mathcal{C}^{2}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
-solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=0
.
In both cases, we also find that the intensity profile of
u
satisfies the boundary conditions
\eta(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0})=\eta(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})=1-1/(2\kappa).

These local solutions allow us to build every possible solution such that
\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa}))<\infty
. Using Lemma
4.8
to continuously glue together finitely many local solutions and extending them on
\mathbb{R}
with a cuspon-like solution (respectively with constants of modulus one if
\kappa=1/2
), one obtains a composite wave solution (respectively a compacton). This ends the classification in the case
2\leq\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))<\infty
,
since in this case, as explained in Lemma
4.14
, we have
\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa}))=\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))-1
.

We can explicitly compute the set of admissible
\eta_{0}
in terms of
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{D}\cup\mathcal{C}
using (
1.47
)–(
1.48
), however, for the sake of simplicity, we only show that singular solutions
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathcal{Z}(u)=\{0\}
exists when
\eta_{0}
is negative enough.

Proposition 1.8

(Composite waves)
.

For any
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
, there exists a negative constant
A<1-1/(2\kappa)
, such that if
\eta_{0}\in(-\infty,A)
, then there exists a unique solution
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, up to phase shift, to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) satisfying

\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})=\{0\}\quad\text{ and }\eta_{c,\kappa}(0)=\eta_{0}.

Also,
u\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
,
\eta_{0}
is the global minimum of
\eta_{c,\kappa}
, and

\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{N}(u_{c,\kappa}))=2
.
In addition, if
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, then
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})=\mathcal{N}(u_{c,\kappa})
,
whereas if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{C}
, then
u_{c,\kappa}
is a compacton (i.e.
\eta_{c,\kappa}
is compactly supported).

The left panel in Figure
4
displays a numerical approximation of the solution
\eta_{c,\kappa}
to (
1.43
)–(
1.44
), with
\eta_{0}=-10
,
c=1
,
\kappa=1/2
and
K_{0}=c\eta_{0}/(4-4\eta_{0})
(and
K_{1}=0
), given by Proposition
1.8
.
On the other hand, in the critical case
\kappa=1/2
, we can also give
a family of explicit compactons by solving (
1.43
)–(
1.44
), with
\eta_{0}=1-c^{2}/2
,
K_{0}=(2-c^{2})^{2}/8
(and
K_{1}=0
). In fact, with this choice of parameters, equation (
1.44
) becomes (
1.16
) with
\kappa=1/2
, so we recover the explicit solution computed in Proposition
3.2
with
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}=\pi/\sqrt{2}
, as follows.

Proposition 1.9

(Compactons)
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{C}
with
c\neq\sqrt{2}
. For
j\geq 1
an odd integer, set the interval
I_{j}=(-j\pi/\sqrt{2},j\pi/\sqrt{2})
. If
c=0
, we define

u^{(j)}_{c,1/2}(x)=\sin(x/\sqrt{2}),\quad\text{ for all }I_{j},

whereas if
c>0
with
c\neq\sqrt{2}
, we define, for all
x\in I_{j}
,

u^{(j)}_{c,1/2}(x)=\sqrt{1-\frac{(2-c^{2})}{2}\cos^{2}\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)}\,e^{i\theta(x)},\text{ with }\theta(y)=\frac{\pi}{2}+k\pi-\frac{cy}{2}-\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{c}{\sqrt{2}}\cot\Big(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big),

for
y\in(k\sqrt{2}\pi,(k+1)\sqrt{2}\pi)\cap I_{j}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In both cases, we extend
u^{(j)}_{c,1/2}
to
\mathbb{R}
as a continuous function, which is constant outside
I_{j}
. Then
u^{(j)}_{c,1/2}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
and is a weak solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

Notice that the family of compactons
(u^{(j)})_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
given by Proposition
1.9
,
satisfies
\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa}^{(j)})=\{k\sqrt{2}\pi~|~k\in\mathbb{Z},~|k|<|j|\}
and
\mathcal{N}(u_{c,\kappa})=\emptyset
, yet
(u^{(j)})_{j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is not a family of
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-solutions because the second order derivative is discontinuous at
x=\pm j\pi/\sqrt{2}
.
In Figure
4
, we also plot the intensity profile of the compacton
u^{3}_{1,1/2}
in the center panel, and its phase in the right panel.

\begin{overpic}[figures/bu] \end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[figures/BU05]
\par\end{overpic}

\begin{overpic}[figures/PBU05]
\par\end{overpic}

Figure 4:

On the left, the intensity profile of the compacton given by Proposition
1.8
with
\eta_{0}=-10
,
c=1
and
\kappa=1/2
.
The center and the right panel display, respectively, the intensity profile and the phase of the compacton
u_{1,1/2}^{(3)}
in Proposition
1.9
.

To our knowledge, only few results deal with dark solitons for (
QGP
) when
\kappa\neq 0
. A branch of explicit dark solitons was found in
[
40
]
for
\kappa\in[0,1/2)
. Also, in the setting of Korteweg models, Benzoni-Gavage, Danchin, Descombes and Jamin
[
5
]
obtained existence of smooth homoclinic and heteroclinic traveling waves by classical ODE phase portrait analysis. Their result applies to (
QGP
) and provides the existence of dark solitons for
c\in(0,\sqrt{2})
and
\kappa\leq 0
, which corresponds to homoclinic waves in their setting. In view of the uniqueness stated in Theorem
1.1
, these homoclinic waves are equal to our smooth dark solitons. However, it is not clear how to extend the method in
[
5
]
to classify dark solitons when
\kappa>0
, because one needs to prevent the coefficient
(1-2\kappa|u|^{2})
in (
1.16
) from vanishing. Therefore, the analysis presented above extends the results
[
40
,
5
]
for all
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, and provides a complete classification of finite energy localized solutions, including exotic solitons.

Concerning the higher dimensional case, the only result the authors are aware of is the work by Audiard
[
3
]
, proving existence of small energy dark solitons, for
\kappa<0
, in two dimensions by constrained minimization, in the spirit of
[
8
]
.

1.3
Variational characterization and stability

We explain now the strategy to study the orbital stability of solitons associated with parameters in
\mathcal{D}_{2}

by using a variational characterization.
In Lemma
6.1
, we show that equation (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) can be recast in terms of the energy
E_{\kappa}
and the momentum
p
as

\textup{d}E_{\kappa}(u)=c\,\textup{d}p(u),

(1.52)

so that it is natural to consider the following minimization problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})

\displaystyle=\inf\{E_{\kappa}(u):u\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}),~p(u)=\mathfrak{q}\}.

(1.53)

Indeed, if a minimizer exists, it must satisfy the Euler–Lagrange equation (
1.52
), and thus (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), where
c\in\mathbb{R}
appears as a Lagrange multiplier.
We will show in Proposition
6.2
, that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=-\infty
, if
\kappa>0
, so that we restrict ourselves to the case
\kappa\leq 0
.
This minimization problem was studied for
\kappa=0
in
[
7
,
22
]
, where the authors showed that the curve
\mathfrak{q}\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{0}(\mathfrak{q})
is a well-defined function on
\mathbb{R}
, that is even and continuous, so it suffices to consider
\mathfrak{q}\geq 0
. Moreover, for
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\pi/2)
, the minimizer is attained, and corresponds, up to invariances, to the dark soliton
u_{c,0}
in (
1.4
), where the speed
c\in(0,\sqrt{2})
is given by the equation

\pi/2-\operatorname{atan}(c/\sqrt{2-c^{2}})-c\sqrt{2-c^{2}}/2=\mathfrak{q}.

Also, the energy of the solution is
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\mathfrak{q})=E(u_{c,0})=(2-c^{2})^{3/2}/3
.
For
\mathfrak{q}>\pi/2
, the curve
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is constant, and the minimizers are not reached.

Therefore, we focus now on the case
\kappa<0
. In view of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, if a minimizer of
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is attained, then it is
given, for
c\in(0,\sqrt{2})
, by the dark soliton in (
1.27
),
i.e.

\displaystyle u_{c,\kappa}(x)=\sqrt{1-\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}(x)}\,e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}(x)},\ \text{ with }\ \theta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}(y)}{1-\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}(y)}dy,\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.54)

depicted in the center panel of Figure
2
.

A complete study of the function
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is done in Section
6
. In particular, we will prove that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is even and continuous
on
\mathbb{R}
, and also that it is nondecreasing, concave, and strictly subadditive on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. These properties will enable us to apply the concentration-compactness argument to study the minimizers of
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
.

On the other hand, the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is not well-adapted to study solitons that vanish at some point, such as the black solitons. Indeed, their analysis is much more involved, as explained in
[
9
,
29
,
1
,
26
]
. Hence, we only consider here nonvanishing solitons. Moreover, to prove the existence of minimizers, we need to guarantee that the limit of the minimizing sequences can be lifted, so that the momentum of the limit function is well-defined. For this purpose, we follow the
method developed by de Laire and Mennuni in
[
25
]
, by introducing the following the critical value for the momentum

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}=\sup\{\mathfrak{q}>0:\forall v\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}),~E_{\kappa}(v)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\Rightarrow\underset{\mathbb{R}}{\inf}|v|>0\}.

(1.55)

We show that
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
is related to the energy of the black soliton
u_{0,\kappa},

u_{0,\kappa}(x)=\pm\sqrt{1-\mathcal{G}_{0,\kappa}(x)},\text{ for all }\pm x\geq 0.

(1.56)

Indeed, it will be key to introduce the critical value for the speed

c_{\kappa}^{*}=\max\{c\in[0,\sqrt{2}):E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})\},

(1.57)

that enables us to characterize
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
in the following manner:

\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}=p(u_{c^{*}_{\kappa},\kappa}),\ \text{if}\ c_{\kappa}^{*}>0,\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}=\pi/2,\ \text{ if }c_{\kappa}^{*}=0.

(1.58)

In Section
5
, we obtain formulas for the energy and momentum of solitons and cuspons, in particular for the energy and momentum of dark solitons
u_{c,\kappa}
with
\kappa<0
and
0\leq c<\sqrt{2}
. This enables us to show in Lemma
5.7
and Corollary
5.9
that there exists a threshold
\kappa_{0}\approx-3.636
such that we have

c_{\kappa}^{*}=0
if
\kappa\in[\kappa_{0},0)
, and

c_{\kappa}^{*}\in(0,\sqrt{2})
if
\kappa<\kappa_{0}.

Corollary
5.9
also shows that the function
p_{\kappa}:[c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2}]\to[0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}]
, given by

p_{\kappa}(c)=p(u_{c,\kappa})
, is continuous, bijective, and strictly decreasing, so its inverse is well-defined, and we denote it by

\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}:[0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}]\to[c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2}].

Moreover, we introduce the number
\tilde{c}_{\kappa}\in[0,c^{*}_{\kappa}]
satisying

\frac{d}{dc}p(u_{c,\kappa})\geq 0,\quad\text{ for all }c\in(0,\tilde{c}_{\kappa}),\quad\text{ and, }\quad\frac{d}{dc}p(u_{c,\kappa})<0,\quad\text{ for all }c\in(\tilde{c}_{\kappa},\sqrt{2}).

(1.59)

In Figure
5
, we plot, for
\kappa=-50
, the functions
E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})
and
p(u_{c,\kappa})
depending on
c\in(0,\sqrt{2})
, where the quantities defined above are approximatively
c_{\kappa}^{*}\approx 0.75
,
\tilde{c}_{\kappa}\approx 0.51
, and
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}\approx 3.5
.

\put(-4.0,62.0){\small{$p(u_{c,\kappa})$}}
\put(38.0,7.0){\tiny{$\tilde{c}_{\kappa}$}}
\put(53.0,7.0){\tiny{$c^{*}_{\kappa}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 5:
Plot of the energy
E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})
and momentum
p(u_{c,\kappa})
of the dark solitons
u_{c,\kappa}
, for
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
, with
\kappa=-50
. The left panel displays
E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})
and the constant line crossing
E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
in orange. The value
\tilde{c}_{\kappa}\approx 0.51
is defined in (
1.59
),
c^{*}_{\kappa}\approx 0.75
is the point defined in (
1.57
). The right panel depicts
p(u_{c,\kappa})
, and the values of
\tilde{c}_{\kappa}
and
c_{\kappa}^{*}
.

There are two possible behaviors for the energy-momentum diagram of dark solitons,
depending on the critical value
\kappa_{0}
.
For
\kappa\in[\kappa_{0},0)
, the shape of the energy-momentum diagram is a concave increasing curve, as the one depicted in the left panel in Figure
6
for
\kappa=-3
.
For
\kappa\in(-\infty,\kappa_{0})
, there is a cusp in the diagram, as seen in the right panel in Figure
6
for
\kappa=-50
. To explain the cusp at
c=\tilde{c_{\kappa}}
, recall the Hamiltonian group property (see Lemma
5.5
):

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dc}E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})=c\frac{d}{dc}p(u_{c,\kappa}).

(1.60)

Hence, due to the change in the monotonicity of the functions
c\mapsto p_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})
and
c\mapsto E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa}
) at
c=\tilde{c}_{\kappa}
, we observe the cusp in the right diagram of Figure
6
because the tangent vector must jump from
(1,\tilde{c}_{\kappa}^{-})
to
-(1,\tilde{c}_{\kappa}^{+})
.

Figure 6:
Energy-momentum diagram of dark solitons with parameters in
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, with
\kappa=-3
(left) and
\kappa=-50
(right).

Using
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
in (
1.55
) and the function
\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}
, we can state our main result concerning the variational characterization of the dark solitons in the region
\mathcal{D}_{2}
as follows.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
\kappa\leq 0
and
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
. Then the infimum for the minimization problem (
1.53
) is attained at
the dark soliton
u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}
in (
1.54
), i.e.
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=E_{\kappa}(u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}),

and is the only minimizer, up to invariances. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
for all
\mathfrak{q}>\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
and this infimum is not attained.

Remark 1.11
.

Theorem
1.10
establishes that the curve
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
coincides the energy of the dark solitons
u_{c,\kappa}
with moment equal to
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
and speed
c\in(c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2})
. Moreover, the curve
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is constant equal to
E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
in
(\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*},\infty)
. In particular, if
\kappa\in(-\infty,\kappa_{0}),
so that
c_{\kappa}^{*}\in(0,\sqrt{2})
, we see that the energy of the soliton
u_{c,\kappa}
with
c\in(0,c_{\kappa}^{*})
lies above the curve
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
, as illustrated in the right panel of Figure
6
. Therefore, solitons with speed
c\in(0,c_{\kappa}^{*})
are not minimizers for
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}.

We will see in Section
7
that the Cauchy problem for (
1.61
), with
k<0
, is locally well-posed in
u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, for
s>5/2
as stated in Corollary
7.2
,
including the conservation of energy and momentum by the flow.
Therefore, by using the Cazenave–Lions argument
[
12
]
,
and endowing
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
with
the distance:

d(u_{1},u_{2})=\lVert u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{2}^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\lVert|u_{1}|-|u_{2}|\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+|u_{1}(0)-u_{2}(0)|,

we will deduce that the variational characterization leads to the orbital stability of the dark solitons, as follows.

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
\kappa<0
and
c\in(c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2})
, then the dark soliton
u_{c,\kappa}
in (
1.54
) is orbitally stable in
(\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}),d)
, in the following sense.
For all
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
\delta>0
such that if

\Psi_{0}\in u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
,
s>5/2
, satisfies
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}|\Psi_{0}|>0
and

d(\Psi_{0},u_{c,\kappa})\leq\delta,

then, for all
t\in[0,T_{\Psi_{0}})
,

\inf_{(y,\phi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}d(\Psi(\cdot,t),e^{i\phi}u_{c,\kappa}(\cdot-y))\leq\varepsilon,

where
\Psi\in\mathcal{C}([0,T_{\Psi_{0}});u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
is the solution in Corollary
7.2
, with initial condition
\Psi_{0}
.

Remark 1.13
.

Equation (
1.2
) can be written in a more general form as

i{\partial}_{t}\Phi=\Delta\Phi+V(x)\Phi+\mathfrak{s}\Phi(f(|\Phi|^{2})\Phi+\kappa h^{\prime}(|\Phi|^{2})\Delta h(|\Phi|^{2})),\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R},

(1.61)

with
f(y)=h(y)=y
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
V\equiv 0
.
Considering vanishing conditions at infinity with focusing nonlinearities
\mathfrak{s}=1
and
\kappa\leq 0
, the existence and orbital stability of bright solitons for equation (
1.61
) has been addressed in
[
15
,
16
]

for
V\equiv 0
, and in
[
43
,
53
]
for wide classes of
V\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
bounded from below.
After a phase shift, these solutions are real-valued, which simplifies the problem.
This fact is key to using the duality method in
[
15
,
16
]
,
which enables the transformation of the elliptic quasilinear problem into a semilinear one.
As explained by Selvitella
[
54
]
, the dual method does not work for complex-valued functions, thus it is not well-suited for the study of (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) for
c\neq 0
.

Remark 1.14
.

The global well-posedness in the energy space and the properties of dark soliton for (
1.5
) have been addressed by the first author in
[
18
,
25
]
. Assuming that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
is
bounded
, with
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(\xi)\geq(1-\kappa\xi^{2})^{+}
, for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\kappa\in[0,1/2],
and some additional conditions, it was shown that there exists a branch of dark solitons to (
1.5
).
Although there is no analytical formula for these dark solitons, it was proven in
[
25
]
that some of them can be obtained by minimization at fixed momentum,
using the value
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
in (
1.55
). However, obtaining good estimates for
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
in the nonlocal case remains an open problem.

As discussed in Remark
1.11
, some dark solitons of the branch are not global minimizers, with fixed momentum.
The analysis of the orbital stability of the other dark solitons with parameters in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is done in the companion paper
[
50
]
. More precisely, in
[
50
]
the author shows that

(a)

for
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
c\neq 0
, dark solitons
u_{c,\kappa}
are orbitally stable.

(b)

for
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
dark solitons are stable whenever
c\in(\tilde{c}_{\kappa},\sqrt{2})
and unstable if
c\in(0,\tilde{c}_{\kappa})
.

The idea relies on the general Grillakis–Shatah–Strauss theory
[
30
]
, which reduces the stability of solitons to
the study of the second derivative of the action
d(c)=E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})-cp(u_{c,\kappa}),

in addition to some spectral conditions. From (
1.60
), we get

d^{\prime\prime}(c)=-\frac{d}{dc}p(u_{c,\kappa})
, so that
d^{\prime\prime}(c)>0
is equivalent to the so-called Vakhitov–Kolokolov stability criterion:

\frac{d}{dc}p(u_{c,\kappa})<0.

In view of (
1.59
), this criterion explains the result in
b
.

The proofs in
[
50
]
use the explicit formulas for the momentum in Section
5
, and the arguments introduced in
[
5
,
3
]
in the context of the Euler–Korteweg system. We refer to
[
50
]
for more details.

To conclude this introduction, we provide two curves of the energy of traveling waves in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{3}
, using the formulas in Section
5
.
Figure
7
displays on the same plot the energy of dark solitons
E_{\kappa}(c)
(in black) and the energy of cuspons
\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)
(in orange) given by (
5.2
)–(
5.3
).
The left panel corresponds dark solitons and antidark cuspons in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, with
\kappa=0.4
, and the right panel illustrates the behavior
of antidark solitons and dark cuspons in
\mathcal{D}_{3}
, with
\kappa=0.6
.
We see that in both cases the least energy solution does not remain on the same branch of solutions as
c
varies. In view of (
1.60
), the energy and the momentum have the same monotony, as functions of
c
.
Thus, as stated in (
a
), the whole branch of dark solitons in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is stable, which is illustrated by the decrease of the energy in the left panel. The stability or instability of the other branches
is an open problem.

Plot of
E_{\kappa}(c)
in black and
\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)
in orange. On the left panel, we take parameters in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
with
\kappa=0.4
and
0\leq c<\sqrt{2}
. On the right panel, the parameters belong to
\mathcal{D}_{3}
, with
\kappa=0.6
and
\sqrt{2}<c\leq 2
.

The outline of this paper is the following. In Section
2
,
we deduce the ODEs for the intensity profile.
Section
3

is devoted to the classification and construction of smooth solutions for the ODEs, while the analysis of weak solutions is done in Section
4
.
In Section
5
, we provide some formulas for the energy and momentum
of traveling waves. We study the minimization of the energy at fixed momentum in Section
6
.
in Section
7
, we show the local well-posedness of (
QGP
) and the stability of dark solitons.

Notations.

The usual Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces of real-valued functions will be denoted, respectively, by
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
and
W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R})
, for
p\in[1,\infty]
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover,
W^{k,2}(\mathbb{R})=H^{k}(\mathbb{R})
. If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}
is an open interval, then
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
denotes the closure of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
H^{1}(\Omega)
. The notation for the Lebesgue spaces of complex-valued functions will be
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
, and analogously for the Sobolev spaces of complex-valued functions, or simply
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
, if there is no ambiguity. For
k\geq 1
, we introduce the homogeneous space
\dot{H}^{k}(\Omega)=\{u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}):u^{\prime}\in H^{k-1}(\Omega)\}
where
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
is the Lebesgue space of functions integrable on every compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
.
Given a function
f
,
f(a^{+})
and
f(a^{-})
denote
the lateral limits of
f(x)
, as
x\to a^{+}
and

x\to a^{-}
, respectively. We denote by
{\langle~,~\rangle}
the real scalar product on
\mathbb{C}
:

{\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle}=\operatorname{Re}(z_{1}\bar{z_{2}}).

For a complex-valued function
u
(typically a function in
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
),
we define its intensity profile as the real-valued function
\eta=\eta_{u}=1-|u|^{2}
.
In addition, we use the notation
u_{c,\kappa}
for a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) and

\eta_{c,\kappa}
for its intensity profile, but
we will remove the subscripts
{c,\kappa}
in the proofs, when there is no ambiguity.

2
Equations for the intensity profile

We start by recalling properties satisfied by functions in
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
u
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, and its intensity profile

\eta_{u}=1-|u|^{2}
lies in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, with,

\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\eta_{u}=0.

(2.1)

Proof.

The fact that
u
is bounded and 1/2-Hölder continuous follows from the Morrey inequality. Then, using that
\eta_{u}^{\prime}=-2{\langle u^{\prime},u\rangle}
, we conclude that
\eta_{u}^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, so that
\eta_{u}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and therefore (
2.1
) holds.
∎

For notational simplicity, we omit from now on the subscript
u
in the profile intensity
\eta_{u}
, if there is no ambiguity.

For the sake of completeness, we also recall the following result concerning the lifting of smooth functions.

Lemma 2.2
.

If
u\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, satisfies that
u(x)\neq 0
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, then there exists a phase
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, such that the lifting
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
holds in
\mathbb{R}
, where

\rho\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
is given by
\rho={|u|}
.
Similarly, if
u\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, then there is
\theta\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
in
\mathbb{R}
, and also

\rho=|u|\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

If
u\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
does not vanish, it is immediate that
\rho=\sqrt{u\bar{u}}
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{k}.
Now, let
w(x)=u(x)/|u(x)|
and
\tilde{\theta}=\int_{0}^{x}-iw^{\prime}(s)\bar{w}(s)ds=\int_{0}^{x}-{\langle iu^{\prime}(s),u(s)\rangle}/|u|^{2}(s)ds
, then we have
(w(x)e^{-i\tilde{\theta}(x)})^{\prime}=e^{-i\tilde{\theta}}(w^{\prime}-ww^{\prime}\bar{w})=0,

since
w\bar{w}\equiv 1
. Hence,
w(x)=w(0)e^{i\tilde{\theta}(x)}
, and we the conclusion follows by setting
\theta(x)=\tilde{\theta}(x)+\mathop{\mathrm{arg}}(w(0))
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{arg}}
is any continuous determination of the argument defined near
w(0)
.

The same argument holds for
u\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, showing that
\theta
is continuous. Also, since
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

u^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}e^{i\theta}+i\rho\theta^{\prime}e^{i\theta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

(2.2)

which implies that
\rho^{\prime},\rho\theta^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Since
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}\rho>0
, we conclude that
\theta^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
∎

For a nonvanishing solution
u
to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), the lifting
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
is commonly used to derive a system of equations satisfied by
\rho
and
\eta
, which are related to the hydrodynamical formulation for the
Gross–Pitaevskii equation
[
11
]
. The equivalence between the hydrodynamical formulation and the traveling wave equation has been obtained for nonlocal Gross–Pitaevskii equations in
[
23
]
. However, they use that the solutions are smooth. We verify now that this hydrodynamical formulation also holds for weak solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
u=\rho e^{i\theta}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, with
\rho,\theta\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
be weak solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
Then
\theta^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
(\rho,\theta)
satisfies the following system:

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{c(1-\rho^{2})}{2\rho^{2}}.

(2.3)

\displaystyle\Big(\rho^{\prime}(1-2\kappa\rho^{2})\Big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-2\kappa\rho(\rho^{\prime})^{2}-\rho(1-\rho^{2})+\frac{c^{2}(1-\rho^{2})^{2}}{4\rho^{3}}+\frac{c^{2}(1-\rho^{2})}{2\rho}.

(2.4)

Conversely, if there exists a function
\rho\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}\rho>0
and
1-\rho^{2}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, satisfying (
2.4
), then there exists a unique (up to a constant) function
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying (
2.3
). Moreover, the function defined by
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
belongs to
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
and is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

Proof.

Let
u=\rho e^{i\theta}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
. To show (
2.3
)–(
2.4
), we take
\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and write (
1.15
) with
\phi=e^{i\theta}\varphi
.
We have
\phi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and the equation reads, using that
{\langle z_{1}e^{i\theta},z_{2}e^{i\theta}\rangle}={\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle}
, for all
z_{1},

z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
and all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\langle ic\rho^{\prime}-c\theta^{\prime}\rho+\rho(1-\rho^{2}),\varphi\rangle}-{\langle\rho^{\prime}+i\rho\theta^{\prime},i\theta^{\prime}\varphi+\varphi^{\prime}\rangle}+2\kappa{\langle\rho,\rho^{\prime}+i\theta^{\prime}\rho\rangle}{\langle\rho,\varphi\rangle}^{\prime}=0.

(2.5)

Taking
i\varphi_{1}
and then
\varphi_{1}
for any
\varphi_{1}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
in place of
\varphi
in (
2.5
), we deduce:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}c\rho^{\prime}\varphi_{1}+\rho^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}\varphi_{1}-\theta^{\prime}\rho\varphi_{1}^{\prime}=0,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}(-c\theta^{\prime}\rho+\rho(1-\rho^{2}))\varphi_{1}-(\theta^{\prime})^{2}\rho\varphi_{1}-\rho^{\prime}\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+2\kappa\rho\rho^{\prime}(\rho\varphi_{1})^{\prime}=0.

(2.7)

Setting
\varphi_{1}=\rho\varphi_{2}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
in (
2.6
), we obtain
\int_{\mathbb{R}}-c(1-\rho^{2})^{\prime}\varphi_{2}/2-\theta^{\prime}\rho^{2}\varphi_{2}^{\prime}=0
, it follows, by integration by parts of the first term, that
(-c(1-\rho^{2})/2+\theta^{\prime}\rho^{2})^{\prime}=0
in the distributional sense. We conclude from the integrability at infinity of
1-\rho^{2}
and
\theta^{\prime 2}
that equation (
2.3
) is satisfied in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, which in turn implies that
\theta^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. In particular, (
2.3
) is satisfied pointwisely. To deduce equation (
2.4
), we just replace
\theta^{\prime}
by
c(1-\rho^{2})/(2\rho^{2})
in (
2.7
).

We now prove the converse; it follows from the assumptions that
\rho
and
1/\rho
are essentially bounded, with weak derivatives in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Similarly to what has been done to obtain (
2.3
)–(
2.4
) from (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), choosing appropriate test functions in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we deduce that
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
belongs to
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
and satisfies equation (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in the weak sense.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

We also deduce from the proof of Corollary
2.3
that if
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), with

\inf_{[a,b]}|u|>0
, for some interval
(a,b)
, so that
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
in
[a,b]
, then there exists a constant
K\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\theta^{\prime}=\frac{c(1-\rho^{2})}{2\rho^{2}}+K,\quad\text{ in }(a,b).

(2.8)

The next result shows that equation (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) can be recast as two equations for the (real-valued) intensity profile
\eta
, which is key for our classification results, in the same spirit of
[
7
,
23
,
20
,
24
]
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
be a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). Then
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2},
satisfies

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa})\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime\prime}+\kappa(\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime})^{2}=-3\eta_{c,\kappa}^{2}+(2-c^{2})\eta_{c,\kappa},\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}.

(2.9)

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa})(\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime})^{2}=\eta_{c,\kappa}^{2}(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{c,\kappa}),\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}.

(2.10)

In particular, assuming without loss of generality that

|\eta_{c,\kappa}|
reaches a global maximum at the origin, we obtain either

\displaystyle\eta(x)=0,\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},\quad\text{ or }\quad\eta(0)=1-\frac{c^{2}}{2}.

(2.11)

Proof.

Let
u=u_{1}+iu_{2}
, writing the equations satisfied by
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, we obtain

u_{1}^{\prime\prime}-cu_{2}^{\prime}+u_{1}(\eta+\kappa\eta^{\prime\prime})=0,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R},

(2.12)

u_{2}^{\prime\prime}+cu_{1}^{\prime}+u_{2}(\eta+\kappa\eta^{\prime\prime})=0,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}.

(2.13)

Multiplying (
2.12
) by
-u_{2}
, and (
2.13
) by
u_{1}
, and adding these equations, we get

\displaystyle(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})^{\prime}=\frac{c}{2}\eta^{\prime}.

Since
u^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})
, there exists a sequence
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}R_{n}=\infty
and
u^{\prime}(R_{n})=u_{1}^{\prime}(R_{n})+iu_{2}^{\prime}(R_{n})\rightarrow 0\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

Integrating from
x\in\mathbb{R}
to
R_{n}
, we obtain

(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})(x)=\frac{c}{2}\eta(x)+K_{n},\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

(2.14)

where
K_{n}=(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})(R_{n})-\frac{c}{2}\eta(R_{n})\rightarrow 0
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
Thus equation (
2.14
) reads

(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})=\frac{c}{2}\eta,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}.

(2.15)

On the other hand, multiplying (
2.12
) by
u_{1}^{\prime}
and (
2.13
) by
u_{2}^{\prime}
, and adding these equations, we have

\frac{1}{2}\Big((u_{1}^{\prime})^{2}+(u_{2}^{\prime})^{2}\Big)^{\prime}=\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{2}(\eta+\kappa\eta^{\prime\prime}),

so integrating this relation from
x
to
R_{n}
, and taking the limit as before, we obtain

\displaystyle|u^{\prime}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\eta^{2}+\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{2}).

(2.16)

In addition, multiplying (
2.12
) by
u_{1}
, (
2.13
) by
u_{2}
and adding these equations, we have

\displaystyle c(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})=u_{1}u_{1}^{\prime\prime}+u_{2}u_{2}^{\prime\prime}+|u|^{2}(\eta+\kappa\eta^{\prime\prime}).

We are now in a position to deduce (
2.9
). Indeed, since

\eta^{\prime\prime}=-2(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+u_{1}u_{1}^{\prime\prime}+u_{2}u_{2}^{\prime\prime})
, using (
2.15
)–(
2.16
), we get

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-(\eta^{2}+\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{2})-{c^{2}}\eta+2|u|^{2}(\eta+\kappa\eta^{\prime\prime}),

which is exactly (
2.9
).
To obtain (
2.10
), we notice that

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{(\eta^{\prime})^{2}}{2}(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)\Big)^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime\prime}\eta^{\prime}(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)+\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{3},

(2.17)

so that multiplying (
2.9
) by
\eta^{\prime}
, integrating from
x
to
R_{n}
and taking the limit as
n\to\infty
, we finally deduce (
2.10
). Since
\eta
satisfies (
2.1
), it must reach a global extremum at some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. We fix
x_{0}=0
and evaluate (
2.10
) at
0
to obtain (
2.11
).
∎

Remark 2.6
.

Notice that if
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies (
2.10
), and if
\eta^{\prime}
only vanishes on a set of measure zero, then
\eta
satisfies (
2.9
). Conversely, if
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies (
2.9
) and if
\eta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, then
\eta
satisfies (
2.10
). This remark motivates our choice to study solutions to both (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) to shorten the statements of the results.

Equation (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) provides a simpler formulation to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) given by two real-valued
equations. Combining Proposition
2.5
with the following result, we deduce the equivalence of these problems for regular solutions with nonzero speed
c
.

Proposition 2.7
.

If
c>0
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
), then,
\eta_{c,\kappa}<1
on
\mathbb{R}
, and the function

\theta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{a}^{x}\Big(\frac{\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}\Big)dy,\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

(2.18)

is well-defined in
\mathbb{R}
, for all
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, the function
u_{c,\kappa}=\sqrt{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}}e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
and satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). Also, if
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, then
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Let
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
, with
\rho=\sqrt{1-\eta}
, we show first that
\rho(x)>0,
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
\rho(x_{0})=0
, for some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\eta(x_{0})=1
and is necessarily a global maximum of
\eta
. Equation (
2.11
) implies that
c=0
, contradicting our assumptions.
We conclude that
\theta
is well-defined and belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
. In view of (
2.18
), we have

u^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{e^{i\theta(x)}\eta^{\prime}(x)}{2\sqrt{1-\eta(x)}}+ie^{i\theta(x)}\frac{c\eta(x)}{2\sqrt{1-\eta(x)}}.

(2.19)

Computing the left-hand side of (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) using (
2.19
), one can check that the imaginary part is zero. Using (
2.9
)–(
2.10
), computations yield that the real part is zero too, thus
u
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). Now suppose additionally that
\eta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Using (
2.19
) we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\eta^{\prime 2}}{4(1-\eta)}+\frac{c^{2}\eta^{2}}{4(1-\eta)},

(2.20)

Then
|u^{\prime}|^{2}
is integrable, since
\eta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}(1-\eta)>0
. Therefore
u\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

If
u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

),
then the formulas for
E_{\kappa}(u)
and
p(u)
in (
1.13
) and (
1.12
),
can be simplified as

E_{\kappa}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad p(u)=\frac{c}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\eta^{2}}{1-\eta},

(2.21)

by using respectively (
2.16
) and (
2.3
).

3
Classification of smooth traveling waves

Equation (
2.10
) yields necessary conditions on
(c,\kappa)
for the existence of nontrivial traveling waves. For instance,
taking
x
large enough, we expect that

0<(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa),\text{ or equivalently }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D},

(3.1)

is a necessary condition for the existence of nontrivial solutions.
Corollary
3.1
and Proposition
3.2
aim to provide a rigorous proof of this fact.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
c\geq 0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\kappa\neq 1/2
. Assume that
(c,\kappa)\notin\mathcal{D}
and that
u\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
Then
u
is a trivial solution, i.e., there is a constant
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
such that

u(x)=e^{i\phi}
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
be the solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) given by Proposition
2.5
. We distinguish three cases for the parameters:
(i)
c=\sqrt{2}
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, (ii)
c>\sqrt{2}
and
\kappa<1/2
, (iii)
0\leq c<\sqrt{2}
and
\kappa>1/2
. We show now that in each case we can conclude that
\eta\equiv 0
in
\mathbb{R}
.

If (i) is satisfied, then (
2.11
) implies that
0
is the only possible global extremum of
\eta
, thus
\eta\equiv 0
.
In the case (ii), suppose by contradiction that
\eta
is not the zero function, then, using (
2.11
),
\eta(0)=1-c^{2}/2<0
. By (
2.1
) and, using the intermediate value theorem, we infer that there exists
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\eta(x_{1})=\mathrm{max}\Big\{\frac{2-c^{2}}{4},\frac{2\kappa-1}{4\kappa}\Big\}\text{ if }\kappa>0,\text{ and }\eta(x_{1})=\frac{2-c^{2}}{4}\text{ if }\kappa\leq 0.

Hence
\eta(x_{1})^{2}(2-c^{2}-2\eta(x_{1}))<0
and
1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x_{1})>0
. Therefore, computing the sign of both sides of equation (
2.10
), we obtain a contradiction. We conclude that
\eta\equiv 0
.
Case (iii) can be treated analogously to case (ii), by taking
\eta(x_{1})=\mathrm{min}\Big\{\frac{2-c^{2}}{4},\frac{2\kappa-1}{4\kappa}\Big\}.

∎

Corollary
3.1
