provides a first nonexistence result for nontrivial finite energy solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), where the case
\kappa=1/2
was excluded.
We will handle the case
\kappa=1/2
, finding the explicit smooth solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), and checking that this solution does not belong to the energy space. This explicit solution will also enable us to construct the dark compactons in Subsection
4.2
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\kappa=1/2
and
c\geq 0
. Assume that
\eta\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a nonzero solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
), satisfying (
2.11
). Then for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\eta(x)=\frac{(2-c^{2})}{2}\cos^{2}\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big).

(3.2)

In particular, there is no nontrivial solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

)
with
\kappa=1/2
in
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

If
c=\sqrt{2}
, then (
2.11
) shows that

\eta\equiv 0
is the only solution, so (
3.2
) is trivially satisfied.

Let us now verify formula (
3.2
) in the case
0\leq c<\sqrt{2}
. Notice first that (
2.9
) reads

\displaystyle\eta\eta^{\prime\prime}+(\eta^{\prime})^{2}/2=-3\eta^{2}+(2-c^{2})\eta,\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}.

(3.3)

By (
2.11
), we can assume that
|\eta|
reaches a global maximum at
x=0
with
\eta(0)=1-c^{2}/2
,
hence
\eta^{\prime}(0)=0
and (
3.3
) yields

\eta^{\prime\prime}(0)<0
. Since
\eta
is strictly concave at
x=0
,
\eta(0)
is the global maximum of
\eta
and letting
R=\sup\{x>0:\eta(s)>0,~\text{ for all }s\in(0,x)\}
, we infer that
\eta^{\prime}<0
on
(0,R)
. Indeed, otherwise setting
x_{1}=\inf\{x>0,~\eta^{\prime}(x)=0\},

we would have
x_{1}<R
and
0<x_{1}
by concavity. Then, evaluating (
2.10
) at
x_{1}
yields
\eta(x_{1})=1-c^{2}/2
since
\eta(x_{1})>0
by definition of
R
. Using Rolle’s theorem, the latter identity contradicts the minimality of
x_{1}
. We deduce again from (
2.10
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\eta(2-c^{2}-2\eta)}}=-1,\quad\text{ in }(0,R).

(3.4)

By direct integration, we obtain
R=\pi/\sqrt{2}
and

\eta(x)=\frac{2-c^{2}}{2\tan^{2}\big(x/\sqrt{2}\big)+2}=\frac{(2-c^{2})}{2}\cos^{2}\big(x/\sqrt{2}\big),\quad x\in\Big[0,\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big).

(3.5)

The same argument shows that the formula (
3.5
) remains valid for
-{\pi}/{\sqrt{2}}<x<0
, so (
3.2
) holds on
(-{\pi}/{\sqrt{2}},{\pi}/{\sqrt{2}})
. It follows that,

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2-c^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\sin\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\cos\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big),\quad\text{ for all }x\in\Big(-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big),

(3.6)

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2-c^{2}}{2}\Big(\cos^{2}\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)-\sin^{2}\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big),\quad\text{ for all }x\in\Big(-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}\Big).

(3.7)

Since
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we conclude from these explicit formulas that

\eta(\pm\pi/\sqrt{2})=0
,
\eta^{\prime}(\pm\pi/\sqrt{2})=0
, and
\eta^{\prime\prime}(\pm\pi/\sqrt{2})=(2-c^{2})/2.

Because
\eta
is strictly convex at
x=-\pi/\sqrt{2}
, letting

R_{2}=\inf\{x<-\pi/\sqrt{2}:\eta(s)<1-c^{2}/2,\text{ for all }s\in(x,-\pi/\sqrt{2})\},

we deduce that (
3.4
) still holds replacing
(0,R)
by
(R_{2},-\pi/\sqrt{2})
, arguing as before.
Integrating (
3.4
), we obtain
R_{2}=-\sqrt{2}\pi
, and (
3.5
) remains true in
(-\sqrt{2}\pi,-\pi/\sqrt{2}]
. From the same arguments and an induction procedure, we infer that
\eta
satisfies (
3.2
).
In the case
c>\sqrt{2}
, similar ideas allow us to deduce that (
3.2
) also holds.

Finally, suppose by contradiction that
u\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
\kappa=1/2
and is nontrivial. Then
\eta=1-|u|^{2}
satisfies (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) and (
2.11
), by Proposition
2.5
. Hence (
3.2
) holds, and therefore
\eta\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, contradicting
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
. Additionally, we can check that (
2.21
) also holds in that case, so that we have
E_{\kappa}(u)=\infty
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Using (
3.2
) and (
2.18
), we deduce that the only nontrivial
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-solution
u
to (TW
(c,1/2))
such that
\eta=1-|u|^{2}
satisfies (
2.11
) is given, up to invariances and for any
c>0
by

u(x)=\sqrt{{1-\frac{(2-c^{2})}{2}\cos^{2}\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)}}e^{i\theta(x)},

where
\theta
is a smooth odd function satisfying, for all
x\geq 0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\theta(x)=-\frac{cx}{2}-\Bigg(\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{c}{\sqrt{2}}\cot\Big(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big)-\frac{\pi}{2}-k\pi\Bigg),\quad\text{ if }x\in\big(k\sqrt{2}\pi,(k+1)\sqrt{2}\pi\big).

In the case
c=0
, this solution degenerates to the periodic solution to (TW
(0,1/2))
given by

u(x)=i\sin(x/\sqrt{2})
.

We continue the study of the equations (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) in Proposition
2.5
when the parameters
(c,\kappa)
belong to
\mathcal{D}
, using the regions
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{3}
, defined in(
1.18
)–(
1.19
).

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
and assume that

\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a nonzero solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) satisfying (
2.1
).
Then up to translation,
\eta
is even, reaching a global extremum at the origin with
\eta(0)=1-c^{2}/2
.
Moreover, we have
\eta>0
and
\eta^{\prime}<0
on
(0,\infty)
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}
, while
\eta<0
and
\eta^{\prime}>0
on
(0,\infty)
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
.
Additionally, the function
\eta
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and is exponentially decaying, as well as all its derivatives, i.e. for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists positive constants
C_{0}
and
C
such that

|D^{j}\eta(x)|\leq C_{0}e^{-C(|x|-1)},\quad\text{ for all }|x|\geq 1.

Proof.

We recall that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}
. Let us treat case first the case
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}
. Using (
2.11
), we assume that
\eta
reaches a
positive
global maximum
1-c^{2}/2
at
x=0
.
Notice that
\eta>0
, indeed, otherwise we would have
\eta(x_{1})=0
, for some
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, equation (
2.10
) yields
\eta^{\prime}(x_{1})=0
. Using
(
2.9
)
, we infer that
(\eta,\eta^{\prime})^{T}
satisfies

\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\eta^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}^{\prime}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\eta^{\prime}(x)\\
\frac{-3\eta^{2}(x)+(2-c^{2})\eta(x)-\kappa(\eta^{\prime}(x))^{2}}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x)}\end{pmatrix}=F((\eta,\eta^{\prime})^{T}),

(3.8)

in a neighborhood of
x_{1}
. Since
F
is well-defined and locally Lipschitz in
(\mathbb{R}\backslash\{1-1/(2\kappa)\})\times\mathbb{R}
, by Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, for any initial condition in
(\mathbb{R}\backslash\{1-1/(2\kappa)\})\times\mathbb{R}
, there is a unique maximal solution of (
3.8
). In particular, since
(\eta(x_{1}),\eta^{\prime}(x_{1}))^{T}=(0,0)^{T}
, is an equilibrium, we deduce that
\eta\equiv 0
in
\mathbb{R}
, which is a contradiction.

The fact that
\eta
is even also follows from the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem.
Indeed, setting
\tilde{\eta}(x)=\eta(-x)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we deduce that

(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})^{T}
satisfies (
3.8
) with initial condition
(\tilde{\eta}(0),\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(0))^{T}=(1-c^{2}/2,0)^{T}
. Thus
\eta_{2}=\eta
.

We show now that
\eta^{\prime}(x)<0
, for all
x\in(0,\infty)
. Evaluating equation (
2.9
) at
x=0
, yields
\eta^{\prime\prime}(0)<0
, hence
\eta^{\prime}
is decreasing in the vicinity of
0
. This implies that
\eta^{\prime}(x)<0
, for
x>0
near
0
. Now suppose by contradiction that
\eta^{\prime}(x_{1})=0
, for some
x_{1}>0
, and let
x_{2}=\inf\{x>0:\eta^{\prime}(x)=0\}
. Since
\eta(0)=1-c^{2}/2
, we infer from Rolle’s theorem that we cannot have
\eta(x_{2})=1-c^{2}/2
. Hence, evaluating (
2.10
) at
x=x_{2}
, we obtain
\eta(x_{2})=0
, which contradicts the positivity of
\eta
. Thus
\eta^{\prime}
does not change sign and remains negative in
(0,\infty)
.

To prove the remainder properties, we use that
\eta>0
on
\mathbb{R}
, thus

1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x)>1-2\kappa>0,\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},

and therefore
\eta
remains in the domain of
F
, so it is a global solution to (
3.8
). Thus, the smoothness of
\eta
follows by an induction argument on the ODE (
3.8
). For the decay estimates, since
\eta
is decreasing in
(0,\infty)
, we get from (
2.10
)

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}(x)=-\eta(x)\sqrt{\frac{-2\eta(x)+(2-c^{2})}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x)}},\quad\text{ for all }x>0.

(3.9)

Thus
\eta^{\prime}\leq-C\eta(x)
, for all
x\geq 1
, where
C=\inf_{x\geq 1}\Big\{\sqrt{\frac{-2\eta(x)+(2-c^{2})}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x)}}\Big\}>0.

Integrating this differential inequality, we get
\eta(x)\leq\eta(1)e^{-C(x-1)}
. The decay of higher other derivatives is then obtained by differentiating (
3.9
), together with an induction argument.

Finally, the case
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
can be treated similarly, noticing that
\eta
also satisfies (
3.9
), since
\eta<0
and
\eta^{\prime}>0
on
(0,\infty)
.
∎

Now we prove the main result of this subsection: the existence and uniqueness (up to translation) of
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying (
2.9
)–(
2.10
), for any
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
. Then, in the case
c>0
, Theorem
1.1
is deduced using the equivalence stated in Proposition
2.7
.
We prove beforehand, that the functions (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) are well-defined, and injective so that we can define their inverse. The idea behind (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) is to integrate explicitly the
ODE (
3.9
) taking into account the properties of the solutions stated in Corollary
3.4
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The functions
F_{c,\kappa}
,
G_{c,\kappa}
and
H_{c,\kappa}
are well-defined. Moreover,

(i)

F_{c,\kappa}
is decreasing, belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I}_{c})
, and

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}F_{c,\kappa}(y)=\infty,\ F_{c,\kappa}(1-c^{2}/2)=0,\quad F_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(y)=-\frac{1}{y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}},\ \text{ for }y\in\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ}.

(3.10)

(ii)

H_{c,\kappa}
is increasing, belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{J}_{c}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J}_{c})
, and

H_{c,\kappa}(1-c^{2}/2)=0,\ \lim_{y\to 0^{-}}H_{c,\kappa}(y)=\infty,\quad H_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(y)=-\frac{1}{y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}},\ \text{ for }y\in\mathcal{J}_{c}^{\circ}.

(3.11)

(iii)

G_{c,\kappa}
is decreasing, belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I}_{c})
, and

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}G_{c,\kappa}(y)=\infty,\ G_{c,\kappa}(1-c^{2}/2)=0,\quad G_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(y)=-\frac{1}{y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}},\ \text{ for }y\in\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ}.

(3.12)

Proof.

It is immediate to check that

\displaystyle(\sigma,(c,\kappa))\in\mathcal{I}_{c}\times\mathcal{D}_{1}\Longrightarrow 1-2\kappa+2\kappa\sigma\geq 1-2\kappa>0,

(3.13)

\displaystyle(\sigma,(c,\kappa))\in\mathcal{I}_{c}\times\mathcal{D}_{2}\Longrightarrow 1-2\kappa+2\kappa\sigma\geq 1-\kappa c^{2}>0,

(3.14)

\displaystyle(\sigma,(c,\kappa))\in\mathcal{J}_{c}\times\mathcal{D}_{3}\ \Longrightarrow 1-2\kappa+2\kappa\sigma<1-2\kappa<0,

(3.15)

so the square roots in (
1.22
)–(
1.23
) and in (
3.10
)–(
3.12
) are well-defined. Since the domain of definition of
\operatorname{atanh}
is
(-1,1)
, in case (i),
it remains to check that
(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2}-2y)<(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)
, which is equivalent to the condition

y(c^{2}\kappa-1)<0,

(3.16)

which is satisfied in this case, since
y>0
. Thus,
F_{c,\kappa}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I}_{c})
, and
one gets immediately the values of
F_{c,\kappa}
at
y=0^{+}
and
y=1-c^{2}/2
in (
3.10
).
Finally, simple computations give

\frac{d}{dy}\Bigg(\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)\Bigg)=-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}},

and

\frac{d}{dy}\Bigg(\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2}-2y)}{(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}}\Big)\Bigg)=-\frac{\sqrt{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2})}}{2y\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}},

so that we obtain the derivative of
F_{c,\kappa}
in (
3.10
).

The case (ii) follows using the same computations, noticing that
(
3.16
) is still satisfied in this case, since
y<0
.

In case (iii), we check that for all
y\in\mathcal{I}_{c}
,

0\leq-\kappa(2-c^{2}-2y)<1-2\kappa+2\kappa y,

and (
3.16
) is still satisfied, since
\kappa<0
and
y>0
.
Therefore, both
\operatorname{atanh}
in (
1.23
) are well-defined. Noticing that

\frac{d}{dy}\Bigg(\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{-\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)\Bigg)=-\frac{\sqrt{-\kappa}}{\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}},

the conclusion follows as in the previous cases.
∎

In view of Lemma
3.5
, the inverse functions of
F_{c,\kappa}
,

H_{c,\kappa}
and
G_{c,\kappa}
are well-defined in
(0,\infty)
:

\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}=F^{-1}_{c,\kappa},\text{ for }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1};\quad\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}=G^{-1}_{c,\kappa},\text{ for }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2};\quad\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}=H^{-1}_{c,\kappa},\text{ for }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}.

(3.17)

We show that these functions can be smoothly extended to
\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
,
\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}
be defined as in (
3.17
).
Then they extend to
\mathbb{R}
as even functions, that we still denote by

\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
,
\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}
, respectively,
and satisfy

\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa})=(0,1-c^{2}/2],\quad\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa})=(0,1-c^{2}/2],\quad\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa})=[1-c^{2}/2,0).

(3.18)

In addition, these extensions belong to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

We prove the statements only for
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
, since the proofs for
\mathcal{G}_{c,\kappa}
and
\mathcal{H}_{c,\kappa}
are similar. Since
F^{\prime}_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ};(0,\infty))
is negative valued, we have
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}=F_{c,\kappa}^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}((0,\infty))

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{F_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}(x))}=-\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}(x)\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}(x)}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}(x)}},

(3.19)

in fact, this yields
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\circ}).

Using (
3.19
) we know the monotonicity of
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
,
and we conclude that
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}([0,\infty);\mathcal{I}_{c})
, with
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}(0)=1-1/c^{2}
. Symmetrizing
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
with respect to the ordinate axis, and still denoting by
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
the extension, we deduce that
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
defines an even function and belongs to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\})
. It remains to show that
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
is smooth at the origin. For this purpose, let
h\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, so that, by the mean value theorem, there exists
0<|x_{h}|<|h|
such that

\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}(h)-\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}(0)=h\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x_{h}).

Regarding (
3.19
), we have
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x_{h})\rightarrow 0
as
h\rightarrow 0
, therefore
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{I}_{c})
and
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(0)=0
. Differentiating (
3.19
) and proceeding by induction, we infer that
\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{I}_{c})
, using the same arguments.
∎

We are in a position to show that the function
\eta_{c,\kappa}

defined in (
1.26
) is the nonzero smooth solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
).

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}
defined in (
1.26
).
Then
\eta_{c,\kappa}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and is a nonzero to solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
). Moreover,
it is, up to a translation, the unique nonzero solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) in
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

We show the result only for
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, so that
\eta_{c,\kappa}=\mathcal{F}_{c,\kappa}
, since the other cases are
analogous. For the sake of simplicity, we omit the subscripts
c
and
\kappa
. By definition,
\eta
is even, with
\eta(0)=1-c^{2}/2
,
\eta^{\prime}(0)=0
,
\eta>0
on
\mathbb{R}
, and

F_{c,\kappa}(\eta(x))=x,\quad\text{ for }x>0.

(3.20)

In particular,
\eta
satisfies (
2.10
) for
x=0
.
Bearing in mind that
\eta(x)\in\mathcal{I}_{c}
, for all
x>0
, we can invoke Lemma
3.5
to differentiate (
3.20
), using (
3.10
), to obtain

\frac{\eta^{\prime}(x)}{\eta(x)}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x)}{2-c^{2}-2\eta(x)}}=-1,\quad\text{ for all }x>0,

(3.21)

so that
\eta
satisfies (
2.10
) for all
x\geq 0
. Since
\eta
is even, we conclude that

\eta
satisfies (
2.10
) in
\mathbb{R}.
Also, by differentiating (
2.10
), we deduce that
\eta
also solves (
2.9
), using Remark
2.6
.

The property
\eta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is a consequence of the decay of global solutions to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
), stated in Corollary
3.4
.

Now, for the uniqueness, suppose that there exists another function
\tilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, a nonzero solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
). Then (
2.11
) applies, so that we can assume, up to a translation, that
\tilde{\eta}
reaches a global extremum at the origin, with
\tilde{\eta}(0)=1-c^{2}/2
. Thus
(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})
is also a global solution to the ODE (
3.8
) with initial condition
(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})(0)=(1-c^{2}/2,0).
Therefore, by Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, we conclude that
\eta=\tilde{\eta}
.
∎

Now we can prove Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The assertion
(i)
is a direct consequence of Corollary
3.1
and Proposition
3.2
.

To prove
(ii)
, let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}1
, let
\eta
be the unique (up to a translation) global solution to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) given by Proposition
3.7
.
In the case
c>0
, from Proposition
2.7
,
u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta}
with
\theta
given by (
2.18
) is up to invariances the unique finite energy solution of (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). The smoothness and decay of
u
is a consequence of the smoothness and the decay of
\eta
.

It remains to analyze the case
c=0
, where
\eta(0)=1
and
0<\eta<1
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, and

u
is the odd real function defined in (
1.28
), so that
u(0)=0
.
Hence, we need to modify the arguments given above.
It is clear that
u
is smooth for
x\neq 0
. To avoid tedious computations,
we use the following argument to verify that
u
is a smooth solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

Let us define
\tilde{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-R,R))
as the local real solution, given by the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, of

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa\tilde{u}^{2})\tilde{u}^{\prime\prime}+\tilde{u}(1-\tilde{u}^{2}-2\kappa(\tilde{u}^{\prime})^{2})=0,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(0)=0,~\tilde{u}^{\prime}(0)=1/{\sqrt{2}},

(3.23)

for some
R>0
, since
1-2\kappa y^{2}>0
, for
y
near
0
. In this manner,
\tilde{u}
satisfies
(
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=0
on
(-R,R)
. Notice that we chose the value of
\tilde{u}^{\prime}(0)
to have compatibility with the identity in (
2.16
), since
\eta(0)=1
and
\eta^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Let
\tilde{\eta}=1-\tilde{u}^{2}
. Since
\tilde{\eta}\leq 1
in
(-R,R)
, with

\tilde{\eta}(0)=1
, we infer that
\tilde{\eta}
reaches a global maximum at the origin, so that
\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
2.5
, with
u_{2}\equiv 0
and
c=0
, but integrating between
0
and
x
, instead of
x
and
R_{n}
, we conclude that
\tilde{\eta}
satisfies equation (
2.9
) with
c=0
in
(-R,R)
. Thus,
\tilde{\eta}
and
\eta
satisfy the same ODE problem (
3.8
), with the same initial condition. By Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, we have
\tilde{\eta}=\eta
, in
(-R,R)
, i.e.

|\tilde{u}(x)|=|u(x)|,\quad\text{ for all }x\in(-R,R).

(3.24)

Recall that
u>0
in
(0,\infty)
, and that
u<0
in
(-\infty,0)
. Since
\tilde{u}^{\prime}(0)>0
, we deduce that there is some
R_{0}\in(0,R]
such that

\tilde{u}>0
in
(0,R_{0})
and
\tilde{u}<0
in
(0,R_{0})
.
We conclude from (
3.24
) that
\tilde{u}=u
in
(-R_{0},\tilde{R}_{0})
, so that

u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. This also implies that
\tilde{u}

is a global solution to (
3.22
)–(
3.23
), and that
\tilde{u}=u
in
\mathbb{R}
, so that
u

is solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), with
c=0
.

Finally, we need to prove the uniqueness of the black soliton
u
in (
1.28
), up to invariances. Let us assume that
\check{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is another solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=0
. Setting
\check{\eta}=1-|\check{u}|^{2}
,
we deduce that
\check{\eta}
satisfies (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) by Proposition
2.5
, so that
Proposition
3.7
implies that

\check{\eta}=\eta(\cdot-x_{0})
for some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Up to a translation, we assume that
x_{0}=0
.
Also, by Corollary (
3.4
), we deduce that

|\check{u}|\leq 1\text{ on }\mathbb{R}.

(3.25)

In this manner, we have
\check{\eta}(0)=1
and
\check{\eta}^{\prime}(0)=0
, so that

\check{u}(0)=0
. By using (
2.16
), we also get
|\check{u}^{\prime}|(x_{0})=1/\sqrt{2},
thus
\check{u}^{\prime}(0)=e^{i\phi}/\sqrt{2}
, for some
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.

To conclude, we consider the function

v=e^{-i\phi}\check{u}
. It is clear that
v
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=0
.
Therefore, the real-valued function
V=(\operatorname{Re}(v),\operatorname{Im}(v))

satisfies the ODE system in (
1.32
), with
c=0
, that we rewrite as

V^{\prime\prime}=G(V,V^{\prime}),

(3.26)

with initial condition

V(0)=(0,0),\quad V(0)=(1/\sqrt{2},0).

(3.27)

Notice that
G(V,V^{\prime})
includes the multiplication by
A(V)^{-1}
, which is well-defined on
\mathbb{R}
, in view of (
3.25
), since
2\kappa<1
.
Because the black soliton
u
satisfies (
3.22
)–(
3.23
),
the function
U=(u,0)
is also a solution to (
3.26
)–(
3.27
).
Therefore, by the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, we conclude that
U=V
, i.e. that

\check{u}=e^{i\phi}u
on
\mathbb{R}
, completing the proof.
∎

We end this section with a word on the regularity of
u_{c,\kappa}
with respect to
(c,\kappa)
. Because
\eta_{c,\kappa}
satisfies the autonomous ODE (
3.8
) and since the right-hand side is a smooth vector field
F=F(c,\kappa,\eta,\eta_{2})
, the regularity of the flow with respect to the initial condition (see e.g
[
32
]
) yields
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}\times\mathbb{R})
, where
\eta(c,\kappa,x)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(x).
We prove by induction that
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D},H^{k}(\mathbb{R}))
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

For every multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},j)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
,
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
, there exists
R
,

C_{0}
,
C
positive constants depending continuously on
(c,\kappa)
but not on
x\geq R
, such that for all
|x|\geq R

\displaystyle|D^{\alpha}_{c,\kappa,x}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)|\leq C_{0}e^{-C(|x|-R)},

(3.28)

where
D^{\alpha}_{c,\kappa,x}=\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\kappa}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{j}
.
Moreover,
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D};H^{j}(\mathbb{R}))
where
\eta(c,\kappa,x)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)
.

Proof.

By simplicity, we prove (
3.28
) only for
\alpha_{2}=0
, since the case
\alpha_{2}\geq 1
follows by induction on
\alpha_{2}
.

By induction on
\alpha_{1}
, the case
\alpha_{1}=0
is just the exponential decay of
\eta
and all its derivatives in
x
. Since
\eta_{c,\kappa}
satisfies (
2.9
)–(
2.10
), from Corollary
3.4
and the regularity of the flow, we have for all
\alpha_{1}\geq 0
and all
x>0

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=-\frac{d^{\alpha_{1}}}{(dc)^{\alpha_{1}}}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)),\quad\text{ where }F(c,\kappa,y)=y\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}.

(3.29)

We infer that using the induction hypothesis, there exists
R>1
such that for all
x\geq R

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)+\partial_{y}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa}(x))\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)|\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}(x-R)}\text{, for some }C_{1},C_{2}>0.

(3.30)

For instance, with
\alpha_{1}=1,
(
3.29
) yields

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\partial_{c}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=-\frac{d}{dc}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa}(x))=-\partial_{c}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)\partial_{y}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa})-\eta_{c,\kappa}\frac{\partial_{c}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa})}{\eta_{c,\kappa}},

(3.31)

Using
|\eta(x)|\leq|\eta(1)|<|1-c^{2}/2|
for all
x\geq 1
, we can bound
(\partial_{c}F)/\eta
independently of
x\geq 1
, we can conclude that (
3.31
) implies (
3.30
). On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{y}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa})=\frac{(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa})(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{c,\kappa})+\eta_{c,\kappa}(\kappa c^{2}-1)}{\sqrt{(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa})^{3}(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{c,\kappa})}}.

(3.32)

Since
\partial_{y}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa}(x))
tends to
\sqrt{(2-c^{2})/(1-2\kappa)}
, as
x\to\infty
, we get
C_{3}<\partial_{y}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa}(x))
for all
x\geq R
and some
C_{3}>0
and
R>1
. From (
3.30
), we deduce the decay estimate as follows. Let
G(x)=\int_{R}^{x}\partial_{y}F(c,\kappa,\eta_{c,\kappa}(s))ds
, then, multiply (
3.30
) by
e^{G(x)}
and integrate from
R
to
w\geq R
. By integration by part of
\int_{R}^{w}e^{G(x)}\partial_{x}\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)dx
, we obtain

-C_{1}\int_{R}^{w}e^{G(x)-C_{2}(x-R)}dx\leq e^{G(w)}\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(w)-\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(R)\leq C_{1}\int_{R}^{w}e^{G(x)-C_{2}(x-R)}dx.

(3.33)

Moreover, we can check that for all
R\leq x\leq w
, we have
G(x)-G(w)\leq-C_{3}(w-x)
.
Adding
\partial_{c,\kappa}\eta_{(c,\kappa)}(R)
and multiplying by
e^{-G(w)}
every side of (
3.33
) we get, using the latter estimate on
G

\partial_{c,\kappa}\eta_{c,\kappa}(R)e^{-G(w)}-C_{1}\int_{R}^{w}e^{-C_{3}(w-x)-C_{2}(x-R)}dx\leq\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(w),

(3.34)

and also the upper bound on
\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}

\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(w)\leq\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(R)e^{-G(w)}+C_{1}\int_{R}^{w}e^{-C_{3}(w-x)-C_{2}(x-R)}dx.

(3.35)

If
C_{2}=C_{3}
we obtain using (
3.34
)–(
3.35
)

|\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(w)|\leq(|\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(R)|+C_{1}(w-R))e^{-C_{2}(w-R)},

(3.36)

while if
C_{2}\neq C_{3}
, computing the integrals in (
3.34
)–(
3.35
) yields

|\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(w)|\leq|\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\eta_{c,\kappa}(R)|e^{-C_{3}(w-R)}+\frac{C_{1}}{|C_{3}-C_{2}|}(e^{-C_{2}(w-R)}+e^{-C_{3}(w-R)})

(3.37)

From (
3.36
)–(
3.37
) follows the exponential decay estimate (
3.28
) for
j=0
. Using the same method, we get (
3.28
) for
x\leq-R
. The case
j\geq 1
is then obtained by induction, differentiating (
3.29
)
j-1
times with respect to
x
.

Since
(\eta_{c+h,\kappa}(x)-\eta_{c,\kappa}(x))/h-\partial_{c}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)
converges to
0
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
as
h\rightarrow 0
and
\sup_{c_{h}\in(c-h,c+h)}|\partial_{c}\eta_{c_{h},\kappa}(\cdot)|
is a continuous exponentially decaying function uniformly in
(c,\kappa)
since the constant in (
3.28
) are continuous with respect to
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
. We also get the convergence to 0 in the
L^{2}
-norm using the dominated convergence theorem. Induction of this argument ensures that
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D};H^{k}(\mathbb{R}))
.
∎

4
Classification of singular traveling waves

In the following subsection, we prove Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
,
1.5
, and Propositions
1.6
and
1.7
about qualitative properties on the weak solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

4.1
Properties of singular solutions

Let
(c,\kappa)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, throughout this subsection, we assume that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
Let
u_{1}=\operatorname{Re}(u_{c,\kappa})
,
u_{2}=\operatorname{Im}(u_{c,\kappa})
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
.
Taking
\phi=\phi_{1}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and
\phi=\phi_{2}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
,
in (
1.15
), we see that (
1.15
) is equivalent to the system of two real equations in (
1.32
), satisfied in the weak sense.
Therefore, since
u_{c,\kappa}
is continuous, we expect that solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

)
are smooth on the open set

\Omega(u_{c,\kappa}):=\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}\neq\frac{1}{2\kappa}\Big\}=\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})^{c}.

(4.1)

This is exactly the conclusion of the result below.
Note that if
\kappa\leq 0
, we trivially have
\Omega(u_{c,\kappa})=\mathbb{R}
. Also,
in the case
\kappa\neq 1/2
, bearing in mind (
2.1
), we can find
R>0
such that
-\infty<-R\leq\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}\leq R<\infty
and
(-\infty,-R)\cup(R,\infty)\subset\Omega(u_{c,\kappa})
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}
,
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}
are given by (
1.34
).

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. If
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), then
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Omega(u_{c,\kappa}))
. In particular, if
\kappa\neq 1/2
so that
-\infty<\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}<\infty
, then
u_{c,\kappa}
is smooth in
(-\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa})\cup(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa},\infty)
.

Proof.

As usual, let
u=u_{c,\kappa}
and write
u=u_{1}+iu_{2}
, so that
U=(u_{1},u_{2})
satisfies the system (
1.32
), that we recast as

A(u)U^{\prime\prime}=F(U,U^{\prime}),

in the distributional sense, where
F(U,U^{\prime})
denotes the right-hand side in (
1.32
).
Because
F(U,U^{\prime})
belongs to
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
, we conclude that
A(u)U^{\prime\prime}
also belongs to
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}).
Since the determinant (
1.33
) in nonzero on
\Omega(u)
, we deduce that
(A(u))^{-1}\in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega(u))
, and thus

U^{\prime\prime}=(A(u))^{-1}F(U,U^{\prime})\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega(u))
.
Therefore,
U^{\prime}\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega(u))
.
Using that, by the Sobolev embedding theorem, the functions
W_{\rm{loc}}^{1,1}(\Omega(u))
are continuous, we conclude that
u^{\prime}
is in
\mathcal{C}(\Omega(u))
. This implies that
F(U,U^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}(\Omega(u))
, so
u
is twice continuously differentiable in
\Omega(u)
. The smoothness follows using a bootstrap argument.
∎

If
|u_{c,\kappa}(x)|^{2}\neq 1/(2\kappa)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, then by Lemma
4.1
,
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
so we can refer to Theorem
1.1
to determine
u_{c,\kappa}
. Hence, from now on, we always assume
\Omega(u_{c,\kappa})\neq\mathbb{R}
(or equivalently
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})\neq\emptyset
) in an attempt to describe singular solutions to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
To analyze the behavior at infinity of singular solutions (see Theorem
1.3
), we introduce the following variant of equations (
2.9
)–(
2.10
). Let
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
be a singular solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) such that
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
is bounded, and define the real numbers
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}
,
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}
according to (
1.34
). Repeating the arguments of Proposition
2.5
, we deduce that
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
satisfies the following ODEs:

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa})\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime\prime}+\kappa(\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime})^{2}=-3\eta_{c,\kappa}^{2}+(2-c^{2})\eta_{c,\kappa},\quad\text{ in }(-\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa})\cup(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa},\infty),

(4.2)

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa})(\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime})^{2}=\eta_{c,\kappa}^{2}(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{c,\kappa}),\quad\text{ in }(-\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa})\cup(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa},\infty).

(4.3)

In particular, the sign constraints in equation (
4.3
) imply some conditions on
(c,\kappa)
, which we discuss in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
and assume that
\kappa\neq 1/2
. If
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) and
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})\neq\emptyset
, then we necessarily have
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
.

Proof.

Let us remove the subscripts of
u_{c,\kappa}
.
We saw above that since
\kappa\neq 1/2
,
\Gamma(u)
is bounded, so that the function
\eta=1-|u|^{2}
satisfies (
4.2
)–(
4.3
). By contradiction, assume that
(c,\kappa)\in([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})\backslash\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
. Then, since
\kappa\neq 1/2
, we have either
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
or
(c,\kappa)
meet the assumption of Corollary
3.1
.
By definition of
\Gamma(u)
, if
\kappa\leq 0
, then
\Gamma(u)=\emptyset
, therefore
(c,\kappa)
meet the assumption of Corollary
3.1
.
Using ideas along the same lines as in Corollary
3.1
, we infer that there exists
x_{0}>\mathfrak{b}
such that
(2-c^{2}-2\eta(x_{0}))/(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x_{0}))<0
, which further implies that
(\eta^{\prime}(x_{0}))^{2}<0
in (
4.3
), a contradiction. We conclude that
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
1.2
, using the previous lemmas.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\notin(\tilde{D}\cup\mathcal{C})
and assume by contradiction that
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})\neq\emptyset
. Since
(c,\kappa)\notin\mathcal{C}
, we can apply Lemma
4.2
to deduce that
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, contradicting the assumptions on
(c,\kappa)
.
Thus
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})=\emptyset
, and using Lemma
4.1
, we deduce that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, so that the conclusion follows from Theorem
1.1
.
∎

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, and
\mathfrak{a}\leq\mathfrak{b}
be given real numbers. We discuss the behavior of any solution
\eta
to (
4.2
)–(
4.3
) with
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}=\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}=\mathfrak{b}
reaching the critical value at the boundaries
\eta(\mathfrak{a}^{-})=\eta(\mathfrak{b}^{+})=1-1/(2\kappa)
. We will show later, in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, that such a solution is uniquely determined using (
1.39
) and satisfies
\eta(x)=\eta(\mathfrak{a}-x+\mathfrak{b})
, for all
x<\mathfrak{a}
. Therefore, in the next result, we consider only the properties of the function on
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
. Most of the ideas in this result come from Corollary
3.4
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
and assume that one of the following conditions holds:

(
i
)

(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{B}_{-}
or
(
ii
)

(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}\cup\mathcal{B}_{+}
.

Suppose that
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{2}((-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup(\mathfrak{b},\infty))
satisfies (
2.1
), (
4.2
)–(
4.3
), and the boundary conditions

\eta(\mathfrak{a}^{-})=\eta(\mathfrak{b}^{+})=1-1/(2\kappa).

Then
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((\mathfrak{b},\infty))
, we have
\eta<0
and
\eta^{\prime}>0
on
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
in case
(i)
, while in case (ii),
\eta>0
and
\eta^{\prime}<0
on
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
.
In addition, if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}
, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
C,C_{0}>0
such that

|\partial_{x}^{j}\eta(x)|\leq C_{0}e^{-C_{1}(|x|-\mathfrak{b}-1)},\quad\text{ for all }|x|\geq\mathfrak{b}+1,

Finally, if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{-}\cup\mathcal{B}_{+}
, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
C_{0}
,
C_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{j}\eta(x)|\leq\frac{C_{0}}{(x-\mathfrak{b}-1+C_{1})^{2+j}},\quad\text{ for all }|x|\geq\mathfrak{b}+1.

(4.4)

Proof.

We place ourselves in case (i). We prove that the function
\eta
does not vanish in
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
using the same ODE argument as in Corollary
3.4
. Then, since
\eta(\mathfrak{b}^{+})<0
, we get
\eta<0
in
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
. Using condition (
2.1
), there must exist
R>\mathfrak{b}
such that
1-1/(2\kappa)<\eta(R)<0
, hence by the mean value theorem, there exists
\mathfrak{b}<x_{0}<R
such that
\eta^{\prime}(x_{0})(R-\mathfrak{b})=\eta(R)-1+1/(2\kappa)
. We conclude that
\eta^{\prime}>0
is the neighborhood of
x_{0}
. Now suppose that
\eta^{\prime}(\tilde{x})=0
, for some
\tilde{x}\in(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
. From (
4.3
), we infer that
\eta(\tilde{x})=0
or
\eta(\tilde{x})=1-c^{2}/2\geq 0
, which is absurd since
\eta
cannot vanish in
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
. Therefore,
\eta^{\prime}>0
in
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
. Proceeding along the same lines as in Corollary
3.4
we recover the exponential decay of
\eta
if
c\neq\sqrt{2}
.
It remains to prove (
4.4
) for
(\sqrt{2},\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{-}
. In this case, since
\eta<0
and
\eta^{\prime}>0
in
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
, we obtain using (
4.3
)

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\sqrt{-2\eta^{3}(x)}}{\sqrt{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x)}},\quad\text{ for all }x>\mathfrak{b}.

(4.5)

Thus
\eta^{\prime}(x)\geq\sqrt{-2\eta^{3}(x)}/\sqrt{1-2\kappa}
, for all
x\geq\mathfrak{b}+1
, and we obtain (
4.4
) for
j=0
by integrating this inequality. We get (
4.4
) for higher order derivatives using (
4.5
) for
j=1
, and differentiating (
4.2
) with respect to
x
, together with an induction argument for
j\geq 2
. Case (ii) is analogous, bearing in mind that
\eta(\mathfrak{b}^{+})=1-1/(2\kappa)>0
, so that
\eta>0
and
\eta^{\prime}<0
in
(\mathfrak{b},\infty)
.
∎

We now prove an adapted version of Lemma
3.5
to the functions (
1.35
)–(
1.38
), using the same arguments.

Lemma 4.4
.

The functions
f_{c,\kappa}
,
g_{\kappa}
,
\tilde{g}_{\kappa}
and
h_{c,\kappa}
are well-defined. Moreover,

(i)

f_{c,\kappa}
is increasing, belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa})
, and

f_{c,\kappa}(1-1/(2\kappa))=0,\ \lim_{y\to 0^{-}}f_{c,\kappa}(y)=\infty,\ f_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(y)=-\frac{1}{y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}},\ \text{ for }y\in\mathfrak{T}^{\circ}_{\kappa}.

(4.6)

(ii)

g_{\kappa}
is increasing, belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{T}^{\circ}_{\kappa})
, and

g_{\kappa}(1-1/(2\kappa))=0,\ \lim_{y\to 0^{-}}g_{\kappa}(y)=\infty,\ g_{\kappa}^{\prime}(y)=-\frac{1}{y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{-2y}},\ \text{ for }y\in\mathfrak{T}^{\circ}_{\kappa}.

(4.7)

(iii)

\tilde{g}_{\kappa}
is decreasing, belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa})
, and

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}\tilde{g}_{\kappa}(y)=\infty,\ \tilde{g}_{\kappa}(1-1/(2\kappa))=0,\ \tilde{g}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(y)=-\frac{1}{y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{-2y}},\ \text{ for }y\in\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}.

(4.8)

(iv)

h_{c,\kappa}
is decreasing, belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}^{\circ})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{J}^{\circ}_{\kappa})
, and

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}h_{c,\kappa}(y)=\infty,\ h_{c,\kappa}(1-1/(2\kappa))=0,\ h_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(y)=-\frac{1}{y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}},\ \text{ for }y\in\mathfrak{J}^{\circ}_{\kappa}.

(4.9)

Consequently, the functions in (
1.35
)–(
1.38
) are injective and their inverse lie in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{T}^{\circ}_{\kappa})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa})
in cases
(i)
–
(ii)
(respectively in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{J}^{\circ}_{\kappa})\cap\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa})
in cases
(iii)
–
(iv)
).

Proof.

For case
(i)
, it can be proved just as in Lemma
3.5
that the square roots are well-defined for all
y\in\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}^{\circ}
. The only difference between the formula of
f_{c,\kappa}
and
F_{c,\kappa}
given by (
1.22
), is the inversion of the argument in
\operatorname{atanh}
. Here, we have
y(c^{2}\kappa-1)>0
, which is equivalent to the condition
(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2}-2y)>(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)>0
. Therefore, the

\operatorname{atanh}
in
f_{c,\kappa}
is well-defined.
Moreover, we have for all
y\in\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}^{\circ}
,

\frac{d}{dy}\Bigg(\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{(2-c^{2})(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2}-2y)}}\Big)\Bigg)=-\frac{\sqrt{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2})}}{2y\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}},

We refer to Lemma
3.5
for the formula of the derivative of the other terms in
f_{c,\kappa}
.
The other cases are analogous to
(i)
.
∎

We are in a position to prove Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Recall that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa})\cap(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa},\infty))
with
-\infty<\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}<\infty
and that
\eta_{c,\kappa}
satisfies (
4.2
)–(
4.3
). Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}=0
and we set
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}
.
Let us treat the case
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, using Lemma
4.3
, we get
1-2\kappa<\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)<0,
(i.e.
\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)\in\mathfrak{T}^{\circ}_{\kappa}
) for all
x>0
and that
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}>0
for all
x>0
. From this and equation (
4.3
), we have in the fashion of (
3.9
)

\displaystyle\frac{\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}(x)}{\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)}{2-c^{2}-2\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)}}=-1,\quad\text{ for all }x>0.

(4.10)

Integrating (
4.10
) from
\varepsilon
to
x
and taking the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we obtain from (
4.6
) that
f_{c,\kappa}(\eta_{c,\kappa}(x))=x
for all
x\geq 0
and we obtain (
1.39
) by applying
f_{c,\kappa}^{-1}
to both sides. Additionally, the symmetry formula
\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(\mathfrak{a}-x)
holds for all
x\geq 0
. Indeed, using ideas from Lemma
4.3
, we can show that
\eta_{c,\kappa}<0
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}<0
in
(-\infty,\mathfrak{a})
. Thus,
\eta_{c,\kappa}(\mathfrak{a}-x)\in\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}
, for all
x\geq 0
, and also satisfies (
4.10
). Then, we infer that

f_{c,\kappa}(\eta_{c,\kappa}(\mathfrak{a}-x))=f_{c,\kappa}(\eta(x))=x,\quad\text{ for all }x\geq 0,

and we conclude by applying
f_{c,\kappa}^{-1}
to the previous equation. Since
1-1/(2\kappa)<\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)<0,
we have
\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)<1
for all
x\in(-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup(0,\infty)
. Hence, we recover
u_{c,\kappa}=\sqrt{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}}e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}}
with
\theta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}=c\eta_{c,\kappa}/(2-2\eta_{c,\kappa})
in
(-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup(0,\infty)
using ideas similar to the ones in Corollary
2.3
adapted to intervals of the form
(-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup(0,\infty)
. On the other hand, the limits of
\eta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}
as
x\to\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{-}
and
x\to 0^{+}
are obtained taking the limit in (
4.10
) and using
\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(\mathfrak{a}-x)
for all
x\leq\mathfrak{a}
.
The general result for
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{D}
follows from the same arguments because we can show that the formula (
4.10
) still holds in all cases.
Notice that formula (
1.39
) does not contradict the finite energy assumption on
u_{c,\kappa}.
Indeed, using ideas along the same line as in the proof of Proposition
2.7
, we deduce that the condition
u^{\prime}_{c,\kappa}\in L^{2}((-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup((0,\infty))
reduces to
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in H^{1}((-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup((0,\infty))
. From the symmetry formula, this question further simplifies to whether or not
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in H^{1}((0,\infty))
. For all
\varepsilon>0
due to the exponential decay estimates in Lemma
4.3
, we have
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in H^{1}((\varepsilon,\infty))
. Since
\eta_{c,\kappa}
in continuous in
(0,\infty)
, we have
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in L^{2}((0,\varepsilon))
, and, concerning
\eta^{\prime}_{c,\kappa}
, which is unbounded near 0, we get using equation (
4.10
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\eta^{\prime}(x)^{2}dx=-\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\eta^{\prime}(x)\eta(x)\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2\eta(x)}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x)}}dx.

(4.11)

Performing the change of variable
y=\eta(x)
, the integrability problem in (
4.11
) simplifies to the integrability of
s\mapsto(\sqrt{s})^{-1}
near 0. Therefore,
\eta_{c,\kappa}\in H^{1}((-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup((0,\infty))
and
u^{\prime}_{c,\kappa}\in L^{2}((-\infty,\mathfrak{a})\cup((0,\infty))
follows.
∎

At this stage, we could prove Corollary
1.4
, which provides a weak solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

). However, we postpone its proof to Subsection
4.2
, where we establish a more general result to glue local pointwise solutions to form a global weak solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) (see Lemma
4.8
).

Properties of solution with two or more singular points

We are now interested in the behavior of a singular solution
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
in the interval
(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa})
. We assume that this interval is nonempty, i.e. that
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))\geq 2.

If
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
so that
-\infty<\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}<\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}<\infty
, then from the variations of
\eta_{c,\kappa}
in
(-\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa})\cup(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa},\infty)
(see the proof of Theorem
1.3
) we have

\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})\subset(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}),\quad\text{ and }\quad-\infty<\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0}<x_{0}<\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}<\infty,

(4.12)

where
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
are the closest singular points to
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
defined in (
1.42
). Notice that we omit the dependence of
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
on
x_{0}
for notational simplicity.
The following lemma establishes that if
\kappa=1/2
, then
\mathfrak{a}_{c,1/2}=-\infty
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}=\infty
, so that there are infinitely many points in
\Gamma(u_{c,1/2})
and (
4.12
) still holds unless
u_{c,1/2}
is trivial.

Whether the set
\Gamma(u_{c,1/2})
contains countably or uncountably many points is nontrivial and out of the scope of this work. For instance, a solution
u_{c,1/2}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
consisting of countably many bubbles with a smaller and smaller amplitude, and such that the set
\Gamma(u_{c,1/2})
countable, may exist.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{C}
. If
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
\kappa=1/2
, then
\mathfrak{a}_{c,1/2}=-\infty
and
\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}=\infty
. In particular
|u(x)|=1
for an infinite number of
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

If
\Gamma(u)=\emptyset
, then from Lemma
4.1
, we have
u\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and Proposition
3.2
yields that
u
is the trivial solution. Now assume that
\Gamma(u)\neq\emptyset
so that
\mathfrak{a}_{c,1/2}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}
. By contradiction, if
-\infty<\mathfrak{a}_{c,1/2}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}<\infty
, then
\eta=1-|u|^{2}
satisfies (
4.2
)–(
4.3
). In view of (
2.1
), the definition of
\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}
implies that
\eta
must reach a nonzero extremum at some
x_{0}>\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}
. Hence, ideas along the same lines as in Proposition
3.2
yield
\eta(x)=(1-c^{2}/2)\cos^{2}((x-x_{0})/\sqrt{2})
, for all
x>\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}
; in particular,
\eta(x_{0}+\pi/\sqrt{2})=0
. This implies that
\mathfrak{b}\geq x_{0}+\pi\sqrt{2}
, which is absurd. This proves that
\mathfrak{b}_{c,1/2}=\infty
, and the proof of
\mathfrak{a}_{c,1/2}=-\infty
is analogous.
∎

In the fashion of Proposition
2.5
, we obtain near any local extremum
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
a system of equations similar to (
2.9
)–(
2.10
) satisfied by
\eta=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
in
(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
, making the analysis tractable.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
. Consider
u=u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
a nontrivial solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))\geq 2
, so that there exists
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
satisfying
-\infty<\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa}<x_{0}<\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa}<\infty
. Then, denoting
\eta_{0}=\eta(x_{0})
and
K_{0}=|u^{\prime}(x_{0})|^{2}
, we have
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa}))
, and there exists
K_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\eta=1-|u|^{2}
satisfies in
(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})

\displaystyle 2(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)\eta^{\prime\prime}+2\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=P(\eta)+(\eta-\eta_{0})P^{\prime}(\eta),

(4.13)

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)(\eta^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=(\eta-\eta_{0})P(\eta),

(4.14)

where
P(y)=-2y^{2}+(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})y+(2-c^{2})\eta_{0}-4K_{0}-4cK_{1}
, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By simplicity, we fix
x_{0}=0
and
we adapt arguments from Proposition
2.5
to obtain (
4.13
)–(
4.14
), taking this time
R_{n}=x_{0}=0
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Writing
u_{c,\kappa}=u_{1}+iu_{2}
, we obtain similarly to (
2.14
)

(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})(x)=\frac{c}{2}\eta(x)+K_{1},\quad\text{ in }(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}),

(4.15)

where
K_{1}=(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})(0)-c\eta_{0}/2
. We also get in the fashion of (
2.16
),

\displaystyle 2|u^{\prime}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}+\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{2}-\eta_{0}^{2}+2K_{0},\quad\text{ in }(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}).

(4.16)

From
u_{1}u_{1}^{\prime\prime}+u_{2}u_{2}^{\prime\prime}=c(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})-|u|^{2}(\eta+\kappa\eta^{\prime\prime})
and
\eta^{\prime\prime}=-2(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+u_{1}u_{1}^{\prime\prime}+u_{2}u_{2}^{\prime\prime})
, we recover

\eta^{\prime\prime}=-(\eta^{2}+\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{2}-\eta_{0}^{2}+2K_{0})-c^{2}\eta-2cK_{1}+2|u|^{2}(\eta+\kappa\eta^{\prime\prime})
, which can be recast as

\displaystyle(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)\eta^{\prime\prime}+\kappa(\eta^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=-3\eta^{2}+(2-c^{2})\eta+\eta_{0}^{2}-2K_{0}-2cK_{1}\quad\text{ in }(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}).

(4.17)

Multiplying (
4.17
) by
\eta^{\prime}
and integrating from
0
to
x
we get, using (
2.17
),

(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)(\eta^{\prime})^{2}=\eta^{2}(2-c^{2}-2\eta)+(2\eta_{0}^{2}-4K_{0}-4cK_{1})\eta-(2-c^{2})\eta_{0}^{2}+4K_{0}\eta_{0}+4cK_{1}\eta_{0},

which can be
algebraically
factorized into (
4.14
) due to the choice of integration bounds. From this factorization, we deduce that (
4.17
) can be recast as (
4.13
) by differentiation of (
4.14
) with respect to
x
.
∎

Equations (
4.13
)–(
4.14
) imply constraints on
P(\eta)
: By the intermediate value theorem,
\eta
reaches at least once every number in
I=(\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa))
. Thus, equation (
4.14
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{(y-\eta_{0})P(y)}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}\geq 0,\quad\text{ for all }y\in I.

(4.18)

We are in a position to prove Theorem
1.5
. A consequence of this result is that the strict inequality in (
1.46
) holds.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

The symmetry property follows from ODEs arguments along the same lines as in Corollary
3.4
. For the monotonicity of
\eta
in
(x_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
, we assume without loss of generality that
x_{0}=0
and that
\eta_{0}<1-1/(2\kappa)
. Suppose by contradiction that
\eta^{\prime}(x)=0
for some
x\in(0,\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
and let
x_{1}=\inf\{x>0:\eta^{\prime}(x)=0\}
. If
x_{1}=0
, then there is an infinite sequence
(x_{n})
of zeros of
\eta^{\prime}
tending to 0. This yields
\eta^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
, further implying that
P(\eta_{0})=0
so that
\eta_{0}
is an equilibrium of equation (
4.13
), a contradiction to
\eta(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})=1-1/(2\kappa)
. Therefore,
x_{1}>0
and we necessarily have
\eta(x_{1})\neq\eta_{0}
, otherwise Rolle’s theorem provides a contradiction to the minimality of
x_{1}
. Hence, evaluating equation (
4.14
) at
x_{1}
, we get
P(\eta(x_{1}))=0
. If
\eta(x_{1})
is a zero of
P
of multiplicity 2, notice that
\eta(x_{1})
is then an equilibrium point of equation (
4.13
). Therefore, we can apply ODE arguments along the same lines as in Corollary
3.4
to deduce that
\eta\equiv\eta(x_{1})
in
(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
, contradicting that
\eta_{0}\neq\eta(x_{1})
. Thus
\eta(x_{1})
is a zero of
P
of multiplicity 1, and we denote by
y_{1}
the other root of
P
. By contradiction, if
\eta_{0}
is a local maximum of
\eta
, then by definition of
x_{1}
, we have
\eta^{\prime}<0
in
(0,x_{1})
. In particular
(\eta-\eta_{0})<0
and
1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta<1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{0}<0
in
(0,x_{1})
, therefore the condition (
4.18
) yields
P(\eta)\geq 0
in
(0,x_{1})
. We deduce by analyzing the sign of
P
that
y_{1}>\eta(x)>\eta(x_{1})
, for all
x\in[0,x_{1})
. Notice that the inequality
y_{1}>\eta
is strict because otherwise,
\eta_{0}
would be a root of multiplicity 2 in the right-hand side of (
4.14
), thus an equilibrium point of (
4.13
), which contradicts that
\eta(\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})\neq\eta_{0}
.

Since
\eta(\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})=1-1/(2\kappa)>\eta_{0}
, using the intermediate value theorem, there exists
x_{2}\in(x_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
such that
\eta_{0}<\eta(x_{2})<y_{1}
so that
P(\eta(x_{2}))>0
. Since
x_{2}<\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
, we have
1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x_{2})<0
, therefore (
4.18
) does not hold for
y=\eta(x_{2})
. We deduce that
\eta_{0}
is not a local maximum of
\eta
.
Then, we can assume that
\eta_{0}
is a local minimum. Analogously to the previous case, we have
\eta^{\prime}>0
and
\eta-\eta_{0}>0
in
(0,x_{1})
. By the definition of
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
, we have
\eta(x_{1})<1-1/(2\kappa)
, and using the intermediate value theorem, we can find
x_{2}\in(x_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
satisfying
\eta(x_{1})<\eta(x_{2})<1-1/(2\kappa)
. Since
\eta^{\prime}>0
in
(0,x_{1})
, we obtain
1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta<0
in
(0,x_{1})
. Condition (
4.18
) implies that
P(\eta(x))\leq 0
, for all
x\in(0,x_{1})
, and
P(\eta(x_{2}))\leq 0
, therefore the analysis of sign of
P
gives
\eta(x)\leq\eta(x_{1})<y_{1}\leq\eta(x_{2})
. Once again, by the intermediate value theorem, there exists
x_{1}\leq x_{3}\leq x_{2}
satisfying
\eta(x_{1})<\eta(x_{3})<y_{1}
.
This relation implies that
\eta(x_{3})-\eta_{0}>0
, and,
\eta(x_{3})<y_{1}\leq\eta(x_{2})
yields
1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x_{3})<0
. Since
P(\eta(x_{3}))>0
, condition (
4.18
) is not fulfilled for
y=\eta(x_{3})
yielding a contradiction. We conclude that
\eta^{\prime}\neq 0
in
(0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
. By the mean value theorem, we deduce that
\eta^{\prime}>0
in
(0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
.

In the case
\eta_{0}>1-1/(2\kappa)
, the result follows using the same ideas.
∎

By the monotonicity of
\eta_{c,\kappa}
, we can check that if
u(x_{0})=0
, then
|u_{c,\kappa}|>0
in
(x_{0},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
, so that, using Remark
2.4
, we can lift
u_{c,\kappa}
in
(x_{0},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
as

u_{c,\kappa}=\sqrt{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}}e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}}\quad\text{ with }\quad\theta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}=\frac{c\eta_{c,\kappa}}{2(1-\eta)}+K,

(4.19)

for some
K\in\mathbb{R}
. We will use this fact in the
proof of Proposition
1.6
, as follows.

Proof of Proposition
1.6
.

Assume by contradiction that
u_{c,\kappa}(0)=0
and
c>0
. If
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))<2
, this contradicts the explicit formula of
u_{c,\kappa}
given in Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
. Hence,
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))\geq 2
and we infer that
0\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
. We deduce that (
4.19
) holds, with
\eta=\eta_{c,\kappa}
solution to (
4.13
)–(
4.14
) in
(0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
. The contradiction comes from the fact that
u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}
cannot be square integrable near 0.
Indeed, from (
4.19
),

u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}=e^{i\theta}\Big(\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{1-\eta}}+i\frac{c\eta}{2\sqrt{1-\eta}}+iK\sqrt{1-\eta}\Big),\quad\text{ in }(0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}).

Since
u_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we deduce that
c\eta/(2\sqrt{1-\eta})+K\sqrt{1-\eta}\in L^{2}((0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
, which implies that

\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}}\frac{c^{2}\eta^{2}}{4(1-\eta)}<\infty.

(4.20)

To compute (
4.20
), we proceed as in (
4.11
). Precisely, using (
4.14
) and inequality (
1.46
), we have

\eta^{\prime}(x)\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta}{(\eta-1)P(\eta)}}=-1,

(4.21)

so plugging (
4.21
) into (
4.20
), and performing the change of variable
y=\eta(x)
, we obtain

\int_{1-1/(2\kappa)}^{1}\frac{cy^{2}}{4(1-y)}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{(1-y)P(y)}}<\infty.

However, this is a contradiction with the non-integrability of
(1-y)^{-3/2}
near 1. We conclude that if
c>0
, then
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|u_{c,\kappa}(x)|>0
.

Now suppose that
c=0
and
u_{0,\kappa}(0)=0
. We observe a weaker correlation between the two real equations in (
1.32
), indeed, if
u_{0,\kappa}^{\prime}(0)=re^{i\phi}
for some
r\geq 0
and
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
, then the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem yields that
\tilde{u}=u_{0,\kappa}e^{-i\phi}
must coincide with the local real solution to the simpler problem (
3.22
) with initial condition
(0,r)
. Thus
\tilde{u}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
, for all
x\in(-R,R)
, for some
R>0
. Assume by contradiction that there exists
\tilde{x}>0
such that
\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{u})(\tilde{x})\neq 0
and let
x_{0}=\sup\{\tau>0:\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{u})=0,\text{ in }(0,\tau)\}
. By continuity, we infer that
x_{0}\leq\tilde{x}
and that
\operatorname{Im}\tilde{u}(x_{0})=0
. If
\tilde{u}(x_{0})=0
, then the latter argument applies so that there exists
\phi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
e^{-i\phi_{0}}\tilde{u}\in\mathbb{R}
in
(x_{0}-R_{0},x_{0}+R_{0})
, for some
R_{0}>0
. But then, since
\tilde{u}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
, for all
0<x<x_{0}
, we conclude that
\phi_{0}=2l\pi
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
, contradicting the definition of
x_{0}
. We deduce that
\tilde{u}(x_{0})\neq 0
so that we can lift
\tilde{u}=\rho e^{i\theta}
in a neighborhood of
x_{0}
with
\theta^{\prime}=K
for some
K\in\mathbb{R}
by Remark
2.4
.
Once again, since
\tilde{u}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
0<x<x_{0}
, the constant
K
must be 0, which further implies that
\theta
is constant in the neighborhood of
x_{0}
. Therefore,
\tilde{u}
remains real-valued in the neighborhood of
x_{0}
, contradicting its definition. Consequently, there is no
\tilde{x}>0
such that
\operatorname{Im}\tilde{u}(\tilde{x})\neq 0
. The same holds for all
x<0
with similar arguments, which means that
\tilde{u}
is real-valued. If
c=0
, and
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, the proof is easier since we get from Corollary
2.3
that we can lift
u_{c,\kappa}=\rho e^{i\theta}
with
\theta^{\prime}\equiv 0
in
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

In the setting of Lemma
4.6
, the next result establishes that the constant
K_{1}
in (
4.13
)–(
4.14
) must be zero.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
and
u=u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
be a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) satisfying
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))\geq 2
so that there exists
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
and Lemma
4.6
applies.
Then
\eta=1-|u|^{2}
satisfies (
4.13
)–(
4.14
) near
x_{0}
and the constant
K_{1}
in those equations must be 0.

Proof.

If
u\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, then we can lift
u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta}
with
\theta^{\prime}=c\eta/(2-2\eta)
by Corollary
2.3
. Clearly the expression for
K_{1}=(u_{1}u_{2}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\prime}u_{2})(x_{0})-c\eta(x_{0})/2
can be recast as

K_{1}=(1-\eta(x_{0}))\theta^{\prime}(x_{0})-c\eta(x_{0})/2,

(4.22)

so that
K_{1}=0.

On the other hand, if
u
vanishes at some point, then by Proposition
1.6
, we must have
c=0
, and we can find
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
so that
e^{i\phi}u(x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
u(x_{0})=0
then we get
K_{1}=0,
whereas if
u(x_{0})\neq 0
we can lift
u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta}
near
x_{0}
, so that we can rewrite
K_{1}
with (
4.22
) and
c=0
. We must have
\theta^{\prime}=0
in the neighborhood of
x_{0}
otherwise contradicting the fact that
e^{i\phi}u(x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}.
This lets us conclude that
K_{1}=0

∎

We deduce that (
1.46
) simplifies to

\displaystyle-2y^{2}+(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})y+(2-c^{2})\eta_{0}-4K_{0}<0,\text{ for all }y\in[\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)).

(4.23)

We now prove Proposition
1.7
stating that unless
\kappa=1/2
,
u_{c,\kappa}
cannot be of constant intensity in subintervals
(a,b)\subset(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa})
. Indeed,
assume that
|u_{c,\kappa}|(x)=1/(2\kappa)
for all
x\in(a,b)
, then
u_{c,\kappa}\neq 0
in
(a,b)
so that we can apply the same reasoning as in Remark
2.4
to recover that
u_{c,\kappa}=\rho e^{i\theta}
in
(a,b)
with
\theta^{\prime}\equiv\dot{\theta_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\rho\equiv 1/\sqrt{2\kappa}
satisfying

(\dot{\theta_{0}})^{2}+c\dot{\theta_{0}}+\rho^{2}-1=0.

(4.24)

Although one can build local solutions in
(a,b)
, choosing adequate
\dot{\theta_{0}}
, we show that global solutions add constraints on
\dot{\theta_{0}}
preventing this behavior.

Proof of Proposition
1.7
.

On one hand, if
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, then we can write
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
with
\theta
and
\rho
satisfying (
2.3
)–(
2.4
). Setting
\rho\equiv 1/\sqrt{2\kappa}
in (
2.3
) yields
\dot{\theta_{0}}=c(\kappa-1/2)
, which replaced in (
4.24
) implies that

(\kappa-1/2)(c^{2}(\kappa-1/2)+c^{2}-1/\kappa)=0
. Since
(c^{2}(\kappa-1/2)+c^{2}-1/\kappa)\neq 0
, for all
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, we deduce that
\kappa=1/2
.

On the other hand, if
u_{c,\kappa}(x_{0})=0
for some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, we deduce by Proposition
1.6
that
c=0
so that
\kappa\leq 1/2
. Indeed, if
\kappa>1/2
we deduce that
|u(x)|^{2}=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
by Theorem
1.2
, in particular
|u(x)|\neq 1/(2\kappa)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}.

Thus, equation (
4.24
) writes

(\dot{\theta_{0}})^{2}+1/(2\kappa)-1=0
, which further implies that
\kappa=1/2
and
\dot{\theta_{0}}=0
.
∎

4.2
Construction of singular solutions

We are now in a position to show the existence of singular solutions.
We start with the case
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))=1
, i.e. the cuspons
in Corollary
1.4
, constructed using the functions in Theorem
1.3
. To show that the cuspons defined by parts is indeed a global weak solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), we use the following lemma, which provides sufficient conditions for gluing two strong solutions into a weak solution.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
c\geq 0
and
k\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},

and let
I_{1},I_{2}
be two nonempty disjoint open intervals such that
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
share one bound
a\in\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
\mathrm{cl}(I_{1})\cap\mathrm{cl}(I_{2})=\{a\}
, where
\mathrm{cl}
is the usual closure on
\mathbb{R}
, and set
I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\}
.
Suppose that
u\in\mathcal{C}(I)\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(I_{1}\cup I_{2})
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) on
I_{1}\cup I_{2}
, and assume that
|u(a)|^{2}=1/(2\kappa)
, so that we can write
u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta}
with
\eta<1
in
(a-2\delta,a+2\delta)
for some
\delta>0
. If
\theta^{\prime}
has a well-defined limit as
x\to a
, and if the function
\eta
belongs to
H^{1}((a-\delta,a+\delta))
, with

\lim_{x\to a}\eta^{\prime}(x)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta(x))=0,

(4.25)

then
u
is a weak solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) on
I
, i.e.
u
satisfies (
1.15
) for every
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(I;\mathbb{C})
.

Remark 4.9
.

It is enough to assume that the limit in (
4.25
) exists, but this limit is going to be equal to zero in all our applications.

Remark 4.10
.

This result can be adapted to handle strong solution in
\bigcup_{j\in J}I_{j}
where
(I_{j})_{j\in J}
is a finite family of open intervals touching at one of their bounds and to recover a weak solution in
\mathrm{cl}(\bigcup_{j\in J}I_{j})

Proof.

Since we are assuming that
u
lies in
\mathcal{C}(I)\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(I_{1}\cup I_{2})
, we deduce that
\theta
is also is continuous on
I
. In addition,
since
\theta^{\prime}
has a limit as
x\to a
, we infer that
\theta^{\prime}
is bounded in

(a-\delta,a+\delta)
. Therefore, using that

u^{\prime}(x)=\frac{e^{i\theta(x)}}{2\sqrt{1-\eta(x)}}\big(-\eta^{\prime}(x)+i(1-\eta)\theta^{\prime}(x)\big),

(4.26)

we conclude that
u^{\prime}\in L^{2}((a-\delta,a+\delta)).

To check that
u
is a weak solution, let us take without loss of generality
a=0
,
I_{1}=(r_{1},0)
and
I_{2}=(0,r_{2})
, for some
-\infty\leq r_{1}<0<r_{2}\leq\infty
,
0<\varepsilon<\delta
small, and
I_{\varepsilon}=I\setminus(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
,
so that
u\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathrm{cl}(I_{\varepsilon}))
. Taking
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(I;\mathbb{C})
, and integrating by parts, we deduce that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\int_{I_{\varepsilon}}(icu^{\prime}+u\eta)\bar{\varphi}-u^{\prime}\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}+2\kappa{\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle}(u\bar{\varphi})^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\int_{I_{\varepsilon}}(icu^{\prime}+u\eta+u^{\prime\prime}-2\kappa u(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+{\langle u,u^{\prime\prime}\rangle}))\bar{\varphi}

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Re}\Big[-u^{\prime}\bar{\varphi}+2\kappa{\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle}(u\bar{\varphi})\Big]_{x=\varepsilon}^{x=-\varepsilon}

(4.27)

Since
u\in\dot{H}^{1}((-\varepsilon,\varepsilon))\cap C(I)
, we can use the dominated convergence theorem to conclude that the integral in the left-hand side of (
4.2
) converges to the integral in
I
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
On the other hand, the integral in the right-hand side of (
4.2
) vanishes because
u
is a pointwise solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) on
I_{\varepsilon}
.

We verify now that the last term in (
4.2
) goes to zero, as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Using (
4.26
), we have for all
x\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
with
x\neq 0

-u^{\prime}(x)+2\kappa{\langle u(x),u^{\prime}(x)\rangle}u(x)=\frac{e^{i\theta}}{2\sqrt{1-\eta(x)}}\Big(\eta^{\prime}(x)(1-2\kappa-2\kappa\eta(x))-i(1-\eta(x))\theta^{\prime}(x)\Big).

Finally, invoking (
4.25
), the continuity of
\eta
and
\theta
and the fact that
\theta^{\prime}
has a limit as
x\to 0
, we conclude that the last term in (
4.2
) goes to zero, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, which completes the proof.
∎

Applying this result we deduce Corollary
1.4
.

Proof of Corollary
1.4
.

Since
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})=\{0\}
, we deduce from Theorem
1.3

that
\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}=\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}=0
, and that, up to phase shift,

u_{c,\kappa}
must be given by
u_{c,\kappa}=\sqrt{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}}e^{i\theta_{c,\kappa}},

where
\eta_{c,\kappa}
is the function in (
1.39
),
and
\theta_{c,\kappa}(x)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}{1-\eta_{c,\kappa}(y)}
.
In the course of the proof of Theorem
1.3
, we established that
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
,
thus it remains to verify that
u_{c,\kappa}
is indeed a weak solution. Since
\eta_{c,\kappa}
satisfies (
4.3
) in
\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
, we can check that (
4.25
) holds multiplying (
4.3
) by
1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta_{c,\kappa}
, and taking the square root of both sides of the equation.
It is straightforward to check that
\theta_{c,\kappa}
is well-defined and that
u\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\})
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in
\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
using ideas similar to the ones in Proposition
2.7
.
Finally,

\theta_{c,\kappa}^{\prime}
tends to
c\kappa(1-2\kappa)
, as
x\to 0
, therefore we can apply Lemma
4.8
to conclude.
∎

We focus now on the construction of solutions satisfying
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))\geq 2
. Thus,
we need to find solutions to (
4.13
)–(
4.14
) satisfying the boundary condition
\eta(\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})=1-1/(2\kappa)
, for some
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. We show now that such solutions exist when the numbers
\eta_{0}
and
K_{0}
satisfy (
4.23
).

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
. For any
\eta_{0}\leq 1
, with
\eta_{0}\neq 1-1/(2\kappa)
, and any
K_{0}\geq 0
satisfying (
4.23
), there exist a nonempty interval
(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
containing 0, and a unique solution
\eta
to (
1.43
)–(
1.44
), with
K_{1}=0
, on
(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
, such that
\eta(0)=\eta_{0}
. Moreover
\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa}=-\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa}
, the function
\eta
is even in
(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa},\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
, and monotonous in
(0,\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})
. Also
\eta
belongs to
H^{1}(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))\cap\mathcal{C}([\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}])
,
with

\eta(\mathfrak{a}^{0}_{c,\kappa})=\eta(\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})=1-1/(2\kappa).

(4.28)

Proof.

Let us assume first that
\eta_{0}<1-1/(2\kappa)
.
We look for a solution
\eta
in
(0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
with
\eta(0)=\eta_{0}
and
\eta((\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{c,\kappa})^{-})=1-1/(2\kappa)
, with
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}>0
to be determined.
Let
y\in[\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)],
and

F(y)=\int_{\eta_{0}}^{y}f(s)ds,\text{ where }f(s)=-\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa s}{(s-\eta_{0})P(s)}},\text{ for all }s\in(\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)).

Using condition (
4.23
), which can be recast as

P(y)<0
in
[\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa))
with
P
given by (
1.45
), we see that
f
is a well-defined smooth function in
(\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)).
Also,
f(s)={O}((s-1+1/(2\kappa))^{-1/2})
, as
s\to 1-1/(2\kappa)
, and
since
P(\eta_{0})<0
, it follows that
f(s)={O}((s-\eta_{0})^{-1/2})
as
s\to\eta_{0}
, so that
f
is locally integrable in
(\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa))
. We deduce that
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)))
is a well-defined function, which we can extend by continuity to
[\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)]
, with
F(\eta_{0})=0
and
F(1-1/(2\kappa)):=\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
.

Since
F^{\prime}(y)<0
, for all
y\in(\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa)),
we deduce that

F
is bijective, and its inverse function
\mathcal{F}=F^{-1}
lies in
\mathcal{C}([0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}])\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
. In addition,
\mathcal{F}
can be extended by reflection to
[-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}]
as an even function, that we still denote by
\mathcal{F}
. This extension satisfies
\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{F})=[\eta_{0},1-1/2\kappa]
with
\mathcal{F}(0)=\eta_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}(\pm\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})=1-1/(2\kappa)
. Moreover, we can prove, using ideas similar to the ones in Lemma
3.6
, that
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
. Letting
\eta=\mathcal{F}
, we get
\eta^{\prime}=1/f(\eta)
in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
. We see that this imply that
\eta
is not only a solution to (
4.14
), but also satisfies (
4.13
) in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{0})\backslash\{0\}
. Since
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
, it must satisfy (
4.13
) at
x=0
, by a continuity argument. It remains to check that
\eta\in H^{1}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
. Since
\eta
is a continuous function, we just need to show that
\eta^{\prime}
is square integrable near
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
to conclude.
Indeed, since

\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}}(\eta^{\prime})^{2}=-\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}}\eta^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{(\eta-\eta_{0})P(\eta)}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta}},

(4.29)

the change of variable
y=\eta
in (
4.29
), yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}}(\eta^{\prime})^{2}\leq\Bigg|\int_{\eta_{0}}^{1-1/(2\kappa)}\sqrt{\frac{(y-\eta_{0})P(y)}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Bigg|<\infty,

where we used that
y\mapsto(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)^{-1/2}
is integrable near
1-1/(2\kappa).

This concludes the existence part of the result.

For the uniqueness, let
\check{\eta}
be another solution in
(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})

such that
\check{\eta}(0)=\eta_{0}
. Then, by (
4.14
) we obtain
(\check{\eta})^{\prime}(0)=0
. Thus, it also satisfies (
4.13
) with the same initial condition at
x=0
as
\eta
. By the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, we deduce that
\check{\eta}(x)=\eta(x)
for all
x\in(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})

The same ideas apply to treat the case
\eta_{0}>1-1/(2\kappa)
.
∎

Inspired by Corollary
2.7
, we prove now that getting a
C^{2}((\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
-solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in
(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
from
\eta
solution to (
4.13
)–(
4.14
), is equivalent to

K_{0}=(c\eta_{0})^{2}/(4-4\eta_{0}).

(4.30)

In this manner, condition (
4.23
) reduces to (
1.47
).

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{D}\cup\mathcal{C}
. Assume that
\eta_{0}<1
, with
\eta_{0}\neq 1-1/(2\kappa)
, and
K_{0}\geq 0
fulfill (
4.23
). If
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}>0
and
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
are given by Proposition
4.11
, then the function
u=\sqrt{1-\eta}e^{i\theta}
defined in (
1.50
) satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) if and only if
K_{0}=c^{2}\eta_{0}^{2}/(4-4\eta_{0})
. Moreover, this is, up to phase shift, the unique
\mathcal{C}^{2}((-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
-solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) such that
1-|u|^{2}(0)=\eta_{0}
and
\theta^{\prime}(0)=c\eta_{0}/(2-2\eta_{0})
.

Proof.

Using (
4.26
) together with the formula for
\theta^{\prime}
in (
1.50
), we can compute the left-hand side of (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
A direct verification yields that the imaginary part is zero. On the other hand, the real part writes

\displaystyle-c\sqrt{1-\eta}\theta^{\prime}-\frac{\eta^{\prime\prime}}{2\sqrt{1-\eta}}(1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta)-\frac{(\eta^{\prime})^{2}}{4(1-\eta)^{3/2}}-(\theta^{\prime})^{2}\sqrt{1-\eta}-\eta\sqrt{1-\eta}.

(4.31)

We can factorize
(-4(1-\eta)^{3/2})^{-1}
in (
4.31
) and use consecutively equations (
4.13
)–(
4.14
) to remove respectively the term in
\eta^{\prime\prime}
and the terms in
(\eta^{\prime})^{2}
. Then, we infer that, since
(1-\eta)
does not vanish in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
, formula (
4.31
) is zero if and only if

2c^{2}\eta-c^{2}\eta^{2}+(1-\eta_{0})P(\eta)+(\eta-\eta_{0})(1-\eta)P^{\prime}(\eta)-4\eta(1-\eta)^{2}=0,

(4.32)

where
P
is given by (
1.45
) with
K_{1}=0
.
In addition, when expanding all the terms in the left-hand side of (
4.32
), we see that (
4.32
)
reduces to the identity
(1-\eta_{0})P(0)-\eta_{0}P^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Thus, we check that (
4.31
) is zero if and only if
(
4.30
) holds.

Let us prove the uniqueness. Assume that
\check{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}((-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}))
is another solution that can be lifted near
0
,
\check{u}=\sqrt{1-\check{\eta}}\exp({i\check{\theta}})
, with
\check{\eta}(0)=\eta_{0}
and
\check{\theta}^{\prime}(0)=c\eta_{0}/(2-2\eta_{0})
. Then, by Lemma
4.6
and the condition (
4.30
) for
K_{0}
, we deduce that
\eta
and
\check{\eta}
both satisfy (
4.13
)–(
4.14
). From Proposition
4.11
, we obtain
\check{\eta}=\eta
in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
, thus we can lift
\check{u}
in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
. Using the latter identity and Remark
2.4
, (or equivalently using (
4.15
)), we check that
\check{\theta}^{\prime}
satisfies the same equation as
\theta^{\prime}
in (
1.50
) in
(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
, therefore
\check{\theta}=\theta+\phi
, which completes the proof.
∎

In the case
c=0
, we can also obtain a local solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) vanishing at the origin (so that
\eta_{0}=1
), using ideas similar to the ones in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
c=0
and
0<\kappa\leq 1/2
. Assume that
\eta_{0}=1
and
K_{0}\geq 0
are real numbers
so that (
4.23
) holds. If
\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0}>0
and
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0}))
are given by Proposition
4.11
, then the real-valued function
u(x)=\pm\sqrt{1-\eta(x)}
for all
\pm x\in[0,\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0})
defined in (
1.51
) is, up to phase shift, the unique
\mathcal{C}^{2}((\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0}))
-solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=0
such that
u(0)=0
and
|u^{\prime}(0)|^{2}=K_{0}
.

Proof.

Using the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem, define
\tilde{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((-R,R))
to be the local real solution of equation (
3.22
), with initial conditions
\tilde{u}(0)=0
and
\tilde{u}^{\prime}(0)=\sqrt{K_{0}}
, for some
R>0
. Note that
\tilde{u}
is a local solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=0
such that
0\in\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{u})
.

Then, following computations along the same lines as in Lemma
4.6
, we infer that
\tilde{\eta}=1-|\tilde{u}|^{2}
satisfies (
4.13
)–(
4.14
) with
\eta_{0}=1
and
K_{0}=(u^{\prime}(0))^{2}
(and
K_{1}=0
).
By Proposition
4.11
, we obtain
\tilde{\eta}=\eta
in
I=(-\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0})\cap(-R,R)
. We deduce that
|\tilde{u}|=\sqrt{1-\eta}
in
I
. Since
\tilde{u}^{\prime}(0)>0
, we see that
\tilde{u}
is negative and then becomes positive in the neighborhood of 0. Therefore,
\tilde{u}
and
u
coincide in
I
. This yields that
\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{u}^{\prime}
are bounded and
\tilde{\eta}
does not reach
1-1/(2\kappa)
in
I
if
R<\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0}
. Thus, we can assume that the local solution
\tilde{u}
exist in
(-R,R)
with
R\geq\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0}
, which further implies that
u
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{0,\kappa}^{0})
with
c=0
.

To prove uniqueness, suppose that
\check{u}
is another solution in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
. Then by assumption,
\check{u}
satisfies the initial value problem associated to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
\check{u}(0)=0
and
\check{u}^{\prime}(0)=e^{i\phi}\sqrt{K_{0}}
, for some
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence
e^{-i\phi}\check{u}
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with the same initial conditions as
u
, therefore
\check{u}=u
, by the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem.
∎

We can extend these local solutions to
\mathbb{R}
by a cuspon-like solution outside
(\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
if
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, and by constants of modulus one if
\kappa=1/2
. By Lemma
4.8
, we recover a composite-wave (and a compacton if
\kappa=1/2
) satisfying (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) weekly. For instance, this procedure yields the solutions in Proposition
1.8
.
To obtain Proposition
1.8
, we just need to show that (
1.47
) holds for
\eta_{0}
small enough

Proof of Proposition
1.8
.

Let
\eta_{0}<1-1/(2\kappa)
and
K_{0}=c^{2}\eta_{0}^{2}/(4-4\eta_{0})
, then condition (
1.47
) writes
P(y)<0
for all
y\in(\eta_{0},1-1/(2\kappa))
where
P
is the quadratic polynomial in (
1.45
) with
K_{1}=0
. Computing the discriminant of
P(y)
yields

\Delta=(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})^{2}+8\frac{(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})\eta_{0}}{1-\eta_{0}}.

(4.33)

We can check that
\Delta\to\infty
as
\eta_{0}\to-\infty
, thus there exists
A_{1}(c,\kappa)<0
such that
\Delta>0
for all
\eta_{0}<A_{1}
.
We show that
1-1/(2\kappa)
is smaller than the smallest root of
P
,
y_{1}=(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0}-\sqrt{\Delta})/4
for
\eta_{0}
small enough. Indeed,
y_{1}=(2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})(1-\sqrt{1+8\eta_{0}/((2-c^{2}-2\eta_{0})(1-\eta_{0}))})/4
for
\eta_{0}<1-c^{2}/2
and we can check that
y_{1}\to 1
as
\eta_{0}\to-\infty
so there exists
A(c,\kappa)<A_{1}(c,\kappa)
such that
1-1/(2\kappa)<y_{1}
for all
\eta_{0}\leq A(c,\kappa)
. This implies (
1.47
), so we can build the composite wave solution
u
by extending the local solution in
(-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0})
of Proposition
4.12
with a cuspon like solution or a constant solution if
\kappa=1/2
. The uniqueness follows from the uniqueness in Proposition
4.12
and from Theorem
1.3
ruling out the behavior at infinity of the solutions. Finally, since
P(1-1/(2\kappa))<0
we obtain
(1-|u|^{2})^{\prime}\to\infty
as
x\to\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
using (
1.44
) so that
\mathcal{N}(u)=\{-\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0},\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}\}
.
∎

Finally, the existence of explicit compactons is a simple consequence of Lemma
4.8
and the explicit local solutions obtained in (
3.2
).

Proof of Proposition
1.9
.

By construction
u_{c,1/2}^{(j)}
is continuous
\mathbb{R}
,
is a strong solution in
I_{j}
by Proposition
3.2
, and is a constant function of modulus 1 outside
I_{j}
. Thus, it is simple to check that

u_{c,1/2}^{(j)}
is a global weak solution by invoking Lemma
4.8
.
∎
