This ends the classification in the case
\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa}))<\infty
. Moreover, the next result establishes that the classification of solutions with
2\leq\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))<\infty
can be reduced to the cases already treated.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\cup\mathcal{C}
and
u_{c,\kappa}
be a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).
If
2\leq\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))<\infty
, then
\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa}))=\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))-1
.

Proof.

We show that between two elements of
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
lies exactly one element of
\mathcal{Z}(u_{c,\kappa})
.
Let
a<b
be two consecutive elements of
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}).
By Lemma
4.1
,
u_{c,\kappa}
is smooth in
(a,b)
. Since the intensity profile
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
satisfies
\eta_{c,\kappa}(a)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(b)=1-1/(2\kappa)
, we can apply Rolle’s theorem to deduce the existence of
x_{0}\in(a,b)
such that
\eta^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
. Since
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})\cap(a,b)=\emptyset,
we deduce that
x_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
and that
a=\mathfrak{a}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
and
b=\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
. Using the monotonicity of
\eta_{c,\kappa}
in
(a,b)
established in Theorem
1.5
, the conclusion follows.
∎

In conclusion, we classified all the solutions with
\operatorname{card}(\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa}))<\infty
. On the other hand,
by Proposition
1.7
, if
\kappa\neq 1/2
then
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
, is a closed bounded set of empty interior. Hence, it is possible that
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
contains a countable number of points (for instance,
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})=\{1/n:n\geq 1\}
), or even uncountable many points (for instance,
\Gamma(u_{c,\kappa})
is the Cantor set). These cases are beyond the scope of this paper, and we refer to
[
42
]
treating these kinds of considerations in the context of the
Camassa–Holm equation. Similarly, the question of whether
\Gamma(u_{c,1/2})
can be any closed subset of
\mathbb{R}
is still open.
Also, as explained in the proof of Proposition
4.11
, in general, the value of
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
with respect to the parameters is not explicit and relies on our ability to compute the integral from
\eta_{0}
to
1-1/(2\kappa)
of a function
f_{c,\kappa,\eta_{0}}
.
It is an open problem to determine if there is a one-to-one correspondence between the maximal amplitude
\eta_{0}
and the half-length
\mathfrak{b}_{c,\kappa}^{0}
of the interval of existence.

We end this section with a comment on the regularity and decay of the cuspon solutions to mirror Proposition
3.8
about the dark solitons. Let
u_{c,\kappa}
be the cuspon solution given by Corollary
1.4
, then, from the regularity of the flow of ODEs, for all
x\neq 0
, and all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\partial_{x}^{j}\eta(\cdot,\cdot,x)
is smooth for all
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
where
\eta(c,\kappa,x)=\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)
(the derivatives in the
c
direction are in the sense of Dini for
c=\sqrt{2},
and
\kappa\neq 1/2
). At
x=0
,
\eta_{c,\kappa}(0)=1-1/(2\kappa)
so
\eta(\cdot,\cdot,0)
is also smooth in
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, however,
\eta_{c,\kappa}(\cdot)
is not continuously differentiable in 0.
To summarize, we have
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\})
and the following decay estimates. Since the proof involves the same ideas as the ones in Proposition
3.8
, we omit it.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}
. Consider
u_{c,\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
the singular solution given by Corollary
1.4
and
\eta_{c,\kappa}=1-|u_{c,\kappa}|^{2}
be its intensity profile. Then, for every multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},k)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, there exist
R
,
C_{0}
, and
C
positive numbers such that for all
|x|\geq R

\displaystyle|D^{\alpha}_{c,\kappa,x}\eta_{c,\kappa}(x)|\leq C_{0}e^{-C(|x|-R)},\quad\text{ where }D^{\alpha}_{c,\kappa,x}=\partial_{c}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\kappa}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{k}.

(4.34)

5
Energy and momentum of traveling waves

In this section, we compute explicit formulas for the energy and the momentum of the dark solitons and the cuspons given by Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.4
in
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively. In this manner, we can check the stability condition

\frac{d}{dc}p(u_{c,\kappa})<0.

(5.1)

For the sake of simplicity, we use the notations
E_{\kappa}(c)
,
p_{\kappa}(c)
,

\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)
and
\tilde{p}_{\kappa}(c)
, corresponding to the set of parameters
\mathcal{D}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, defined by

\displaystyle E_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle:=E_{k}(u_{c,k})\text{ and }p_{\kappa}(c):=p(u_{c,k}),\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D},\text{ with $u_{c,\kappa}$ given by Theorem~\ref{thm:classiftwregu}},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle:=E_{k}(u_{c,k})\text{ and }\tilde{p}_{\kappa}(c):=p(u_{c,k}),\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}},\text{ with $u_{c,\kappa}$ given by Corollary~\ref{cor:cuspon}}.

(5.3)

The starting point is the following elementary result.

Lemma 5.1
.

For all
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
, we have

\displaystyle E_{\kappa}(c)=\int_{1-c^{2}/2}^{0}-y\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy,\qquad p_{\kappa}(c)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{1-c^{2}/2}^{0}\frac{-y}{1-y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy,

(5.4)

where we exclude the value
c=0
for momentum. Similarly, for all
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)=\int_{1-1/(2\kappa)}^{0}-y\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy,\qquad\tilde{p}_{\kappa}(c)=\frac{c}{2}\int_{1-1/(2\kappa)}^{0}\frac{-y}{1-y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy.

(5.5)

Proof.

Since
\eta
is even, from (
2.21
) we obtain

E_{\kappa}(u)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\eta^{2}
. Equation (
3.21
) yields
E_{\kappa}(c)=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\eta\eta^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa\eta}{2-c^{2}-2\eta}}
, so that the formula for
E_{\kappa}(u)
in (
5.4
) follows from a change of variables. Concerning
p_{\kappa}(c)
, for
c>0
we have
1-\eta>0
, therefore the momentum in (
2.21
) is well-defined, and the same argument gives us the expression for
p_{\kappa}(c)
.

Noticing that the formulas in (
2.21
) are still valid for the cuspons,
we obtain similarly the expressions in (
5.5
), by using (
4.10
).
∎

It remains to compute the integrals in (
5.4
)–(
5.5
).
To simplify our results, let us set the constants

\displaystyle A_{c,\kappa}=3c^{4}\kappa^{2}-8c^{2}\kappa^{2}-2c^{2}\kappa+8\kappa-1,\ B_{c,\kappa}=3c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1,

\displaystyle C_{c,\kappa}=c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1,\ D_{c,\kappa}=\sqrt{(1-2\kappa)(2-c^{2})},~L_{c,\kappa}=\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}}{1-2\kappa}}.

Proposition 5.2
.

(i)
Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}
, and set
\mathfrak{s}=1
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and
\mathfrak{s}=-1
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
.
Then

\displaystyle E_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\kappa^{3/2}}\Big(A_{c,\kappa}\operatorname{atan}(\sqrt{\kappa}L_{c,\kappa})-\mathfrak{s}\sqrt{\kappa}B_{c,\kappa}D_{c,\kappa}\Big),

(5.6)

\displaystyle p_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{4}\Big(\frac{C_{c,\kappa}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\operatorname{atan}(\sqrt{\kappa}L_{c,\kappa})-\mathfrak{s}D_{c,\kappa}\Big)+\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{L_{c,\kappa}}{c}\Big),\quad\text{ if }c>0.

(5.7)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\kappa^{3/2}}\Big(A_{c,\kappa}\Big[\operatorname{atan}(\sqrt{\kappa}L_{c,\kappa})-\pi/2\Big]-\mathfrak{s}\sqrt{\kappa}B_{c,\kappa}D_{c,\kappa}\Big),

(5.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{4}\Big(\frac{C_{c,\kappa}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\Big[\operatorname{atan}(\sqrt{\kappa}L_{c,\kappa})-\pi/2\Big]-\mathfrak{s}D_{c,\kappa}\Big)+\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{L_{c,\kappa}}{c}\Big)-\pi/2.

(5.9)

(ii)

For
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
, with
\kappa<0
, we have

\displaystyle E_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{16|\kappa|^{3/2}}\Big(A_{c,\kappa}\operatorname{atanh}(\sqrt{|\kappa|}L_{c,\kappa})-\sqrt{|\kappa|}B_{c,\kappa}D_{c,\kappa}\Big),

(5.10)

\displaystyle p_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{4}\Big(\frac{C_{c,\kappa}}{\sqrt{|\kappa|}}\operatorname{atanh}(\sqrt{|\kappa|}L_{c,\kappa})-D_{c,\kappa}\Big)+\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{L_{c,\kappa}}{c}\Big),\quad\text{ if }c>0.

(5.11)

(iii)
If
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{B}_{+}\cup\mathcal{B}_{-}
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{(2\kappa-1)^{2}}{32\kappa^{3/2}},\quad\tilde{p}_{\kappa}(c)=\pi\frac{(\sqrt{2\kappa}-1)^{2}}{4\sqrt{2\kappa}}.

(5.12)

Proof.

Given Lemma
5.1
, we use the following antiderivatives: In case (I), for all
y\in\mathcal{I}_{c}\cup\mathfrak{T}_{\kappa}
,

\int-y\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy=\frac{1}{16\kappa^{3/2}}\Big((3c^{4}\kappa^{2}-8c^{2}\kappa^{2}-2c^{2}\kappa+8\kappa-1)\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)\\
\phantom{=\int-y\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy}-\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}(3c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa y-4\kappa-1)\Big),

and

\frac{c}{2}\int\frac{-y}{1-y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy=\frac{c}{4}\Big(\frac{c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)\\
\phantom{=\frac{c}{2}\int\frac{-y}{1-y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy}-\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}\ \Big)+\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{1}{c}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big).

In case (ii) and for all
y\in\mathcal{I}_{c}
, we take
\sqrt{-\kappa}
instead of
\sqrt{\kappa}
and
\operatorname{atanh}
instead of the first
\operatorname{atan}
in the formulas above, in the fashion of Lemma
3.5
.
In case (iii), for all
y\in\mathcal{J}_{c}\cup\mathfrak{J}_{\kappa}
,
we take

\displaystyle\int-y\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\kappa^{3/2}}\Big((3c^{4}\kappa^{2}-8c^{2}\kappa^{2}-2c^{2}\kappa+8\kappa-1)\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}(3c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa y-4\kappa-1)\Big),

\displaystyle\frac{c}{2}\int\frac{-y}{1-y}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}{2-c^{2}-2y}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{4}\Big(\frac{c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}\ \Big)+\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{1}{c}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{1-2\kappa+2\kappa y}}\Big).

We verify that these are indeed the antiderivatives of the integrands in (
5.4
)–(
5.5
) from the derivatives computed in Lemma
3.5
, and using that

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}y}\Bigg(\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}-2y}{c^{2}(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}}\Big)\Bigg)=\frac{-c}{2(1-y)\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}},

\frac{\textup{d}}{\textup{d}y}\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}=\frac{-4\kappa y+4\kappa-\kappa c^{2}-1}{\sqrt{(2-c^{2}-2y)(1-2\kappa+2\kappa y)}}.

In the case (iii), by the dominated convergence theorem, the integrals (
5.5
) are continuous in
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
. Therefore, it suffices to take the limit as
c\rightarrow\sqrt{2}
in (
5.8
) to obtain (
5.12
).
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
5.2
,
we can compute the limit cases stated in the following result. We omit the proof, since it only uses Taylor expansions and the identity
\operatorname{atan}(x)+\operatorname{atan}(1/x)=\pi/2
, for all
x\neq 0
.

Corollary 5.3
.

For all
c>0
, as
\kappa\rightarrow 1/2
, we have

(E_{k}(c),p_{k}(c))\to\Big(\pi\frac{3c^{4}/4-3c^{2}+3}{8\sqrt{2}},\pi\Big(\frac{c^{3}/2-3c}{4\sqrt{2}}+1/2\Big)\Big)=E(u_{c,1/2}^{(1)}),p(u_{c,1/2}^{(1)}),

(5.13)

where
u_{c,1/2}^{(1)}
is the dark compacton given by Proposition
1.9
, and
(\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c),\tilde{p}_{\kappa}(c))
converges to
(0,0)
.
Also, for all
c>0
, as
\kappa\rightarrow 0
,

(E_{\kappa}(c),p_{\kappa}(c))\to\Big(\frac{(2-c^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}{3},\frac{\pi}{2}-\operatorname{atan}\Big(\frac{c}{\sqrt{2-c^{2}}}\Big)-\frac{c}{2}\sqrt{2-c^{2}}\Big)=(E(u_{c,0}),p(u_{c,0})),

where
u_{c,0}
is the dark soliton of the Gross–Pitaevskii equation, given in (
1.4
).
Finally, for all
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, as
c\rightarrow\sqrt{2}
,
(E_{\kappa}(c),p_{\kappa}(c))\to 0
, while
\tilde{E}_{\kappa}(c)

and
\tilde{p}_{\kappa}(c)
converge to the values for the energy and momentum in (
5.12
).

Remark 5.4
.

Notice that from (
5.7
), we deduce that
p_{\kappa}(0^{+})=\pi/2
for all
\kappa<1/2
.

We check now if the stability condition (
5.1
) is satisfied for the smooth solitons given by Theorem
1.1
, according to regions
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{3}
, for
c>0
. In this case the functions
c\mapsto p_{\kappa}(\cdot)
are
c\mapsto E_{\kappa}(\cdot)
are smooth with respect
c
, on
(0,\sqrt{2})
, so that we introduce the notations

p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)=\frac{d}{dc}p_{\kappa}(c)\quad\text{ and }\quad E^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)=\frac{d}{dc}E_{\kappa}(c).

(5.14)

In analogous manner, we define
\tilde{E}^{\prime}_{\kappa}
and
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{\kappa}

To rigorously compute the Vakhitov–Kolokolov criterion, we need the Hamilton group property, which is just a consequence of the formulas in Proposition
5.2
.

Lemma 5.5
.

If
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
with
c>0
,
then
{E^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)=c\,p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)}
.
In the same manner, if
(c,\kappa)\in\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
with
c>0
, then

\tilde{E}^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)=c\,\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)
.

Consequently, it is enough to compute
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}
, as follows.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}
with
c>0
. Then

\displaystyle p_{\kappa}^{\prime}(c)=

\displaystyle\frac{3c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1}{4\sqrt{\kappa}}\operatorname{atan}\Big(\sqrt{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}}{1-2\kappa}}\Big)-\frac{3(2-c^{2})}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa}{2-c^{2}}},\ \text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3},

(5.15)

\displaystyle p_{\kappa}^{\prime}(c)=

\displaystyle\frac{3c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1}{4\sqrt{-\kappa}}\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{-\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{2-c^{2}}{1-2\kappa}}\Big)-\frac{3(2-c^{2})}{4}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa}{2-c^{2}}},\ \text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}.

(5.16)

Furthermore, we have
p_{\kappa}^{\prime}(c)<0
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
,

p_{\kappa}^{\prime}(c)>0
if
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3},
and

\displaystyle p_{\kappa}^{\prime}(c)<\max\{p_{\kappa}^{\prime}(0^{+}),0\},\quad\text{ if }(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}.

(5.17)

Proof.

The formulas in (
5.15
)–(
5.16
) follow directly by differentiating the expressions in the momentum in Proposition
5.2
.

If
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then
3c^{2}<6<4+1/\kappa
, so that
3c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1<0
and we conclude that
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)>0
. Similarly, if

(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{3}
, then
3c^{2}\kappa-4\kappa-1>0
, and we get
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)>0
, since
c^{2}>2
.

Finally, we consider the case
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
. We remark from (
5.16
) that
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)

can be seen as a function of
c^{2}
. Differentiating twice with respect to
c^{2}
, we obtain

\frac{d^{2}}{(dc^{2})^{2}}\,p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)=\frac{(1-2\kappa)^{2}}{4(2-c^{2})(1-\kappa c^{2})^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa}{2-c^{2}}}.

(5.18)

Thus,
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)
is a strictly convex function of
c^{2}
, for
c^{2}\in(0,2)
, so that it is below its end points,

p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)<\max\{p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(0^{+}),p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\sqrt{2}^{-})\}.

Since
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(c)\to 0
, as
c\to\sqrt{2}^{-}
, the inequality in (
5.17
) follows.
∎

We focus now on the case
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
, where the sign of
p^{\prime}_{k}(c)
is unclear. Indeed, it depends on
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(0)
, which corresponds to the function in (
5.16
), evaluated at
c=0
, i.e.

w(k)=p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(0)=\frac{-4\kappa-1}{4\sqrt{-\kappa}}\operatorname{atanh}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{-2\kappa}{1-2\kappa}}\Big)-\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1-2\kappa}{2}},

(5.19)

where
w
is continuous on
(-\infty,0)
, with
w(-\infty)=\infty
and
w(0^{+})=-\sqrt{2}
.
In addition, using that
x\leq\operatorname{atanh}(x)
, for all
0\leq x<1
, one can verify that
w^{\prime}<0
on
(-\infty,0)
.
Therefore,
w
is strictly decreasing and
w
has a unique zero in
(-\infty,0)
, which we denote by
\kappa_{0}
. Using Newton’s method, we can check that
\kappa_{0}\approx-3.636
. This value allows us to establish the following result.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
(c,\kappa)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
with
c>0
. We have

E_{\kappa}(0^{+})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa}),\quad p_{\kappa}(0^{+})=\pi/2,\quad E_{\kappa}(\sqrt{2}^{-})=p_{\kappa}(\sqrt{2}^{-})=0.

(5.20)

If
\kappa\in[\kappa_{0},0)
, then
E^{\prime}_{\kappa}<0
and
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}<0
in
(0,\sqrt{2})
.
Also, if
\kappa<\kappa_{0}
, there is
\tilde{c}_{\kappa}\in(0,\sqrt{2})
, such that
E^{\prime}_{\kappa}>0
and
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}>0
in
(0,\tilde{c}_{\kappa})
, while
E^{\prime}_{\kappa}<0
and
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}<0
in
(\tilde{c}_{\kappa},\sqrt{2})
.

Proof.

The limits in (
5.20
) follow from (
5.10
)–(
5.11
).
If
\kappa\in[\kappa_{0},0)
, then, by definition of
\kappa
,

p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(0)=w(\kappa)\leq 0
. Therefore, the conclusion follows from (
5.17
) and (
5.14
).

Assume now that
\kappa<\kappa_{0}
, so that
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(0)=w(\kappa)>0
.
Recall that, from the proof of Proposition
5.6
,

p^{\prime}_{\kappa}
is a strictly convex function of
c^{2}
, satisfying

p^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\sqrt{2}^{-})=0
. Also, from (
5.18
),

\frac{d^{2}}{(dc^{2})^{2}}p_{\kappa}(c)
goes to
\infty
, as
c\to\sqrt{2}^{-}
. Therefore, we conclude that there exists a unique zero of

p^{\prime}_{\kappa}
in
(0,\sqrt{2})
, which we denote by
\tilde{c}_{\kappa}
, so that

p^{\prime}_{\kappa}>0
in
(0,\tilde{c}_{\kappa})
and
p^{\prime}_{\kappa}<0
in
(\tilde{c}_{\kappa},\sqrt{2})
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

In Lemma
5.7
, we have
p_{\kappa}(c)\to\pi/2
,
as
c\to 0^{+}
. However, it is not true that the momentum of the black soliton
u_{0,\kappa}
is
\pi/2
. A naive approach will be to try to use the formula (
1.9
), in a generalized sense. However, since
u_{0,\kappa}
is a real-valued function,
we have
\langle iu_{0,\kappa},u_{0,\kappa}^{\prime}\rangle=0
, so
this would imply that the momentum of
u_{0,\kappa}
is zero.
Indeed, a proper definition of the momentum of vanishing functions is a difficult problem, and it requires the use of the notion of untwisted momentum, explained in
[
9
,
22
]
. For this reason, the analysis of black solitons goes beyond the scope of this article.

An immediate consequence of the monotonicity of
E^{\prime}_{\kappa}
in Lemma
5.7
, is that the critical speed
c^{*}_{\kappa}
in (
1.57
) is well-defined, and that there is
a bijection between the speeds and the momenta as follows.

Corollary 5.9
.

(i)
If
\kappa\in[\kappa_{0},0)
, then
c^{*}_{\kappa}=0
.
If
\kappa<\kappa_{0}
, then there exists a unique
c\in(0,\sqrt{2})

such that
E_{\kappa}(0)=E_{\kappa}(c)
, thus this value corresponds to
c^{*}_{\kappa}
. In any case,

E_{\kappa}(c_{\kappa}^{*})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa}).

(ii)
Define
\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)
as
\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)=p_{\kappa}(c_{\kappa}^{*})
, if

c_{\kappa}^{*}\in(0,\sqrt{2})
, and as
\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)=\pi/2
, if
c_{\kappa}^{*}=0
.
Then the function
p_{\kappa}:(c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2})\to(0,\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa))

can be extended by continuity to
[c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2}]
,
is strictly decreasing, and it defines a continuous bijective function,
whose inverse function we denote by
\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}:[0,\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)]\to[c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2}]
,
with

\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(0)=\sqrt{2}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa))=c_{\kappa}^{*}
.

Proof.

In view of (
5.20
),
the assertions are a straightforward consequence of the monotonicity results in Lemma
5.7
.
∎

Finally, we remark that in the case
\kappa<\kappa_{0}
, the function
p_{\kappa}
defines also a bijection between the intervals
(0,\tilde{c}_{\kappa})
and
(\pi/2,p_{\kappa}(\tilde{c}_{\kappa}))
, but we do need to use this fact in the sequel. In addition, we will show in the next section that
\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)
is equal to
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
, defined in (
1.55
).

Notice that function
\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}
in Corollary
5.9
, allows to us determine that
u_{\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})}
is the unique smooth soliton of momentum
\mathfrak{q}
, up to invariances, for all
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa))
. In particular,
in view of the minimization problem (
1.53
), we have

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\leq E_{\kappa}(u_{\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}),\quad\text{for all }\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)).

(5.21)

6
Variational characterization of dark solitons

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.10
.
The starting point is that equation (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) corresponds to the Euler–Lagrange equation associated with the minimization of the energy at constant momentum, and that
c
appears as a Lagrange multiplier. Since
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
is not a vector space, we use the Gâteau differential, denoted by
d
, as follows.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
v\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
be a (complex valued) function and
h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
small enough, the functions
t\mapsto p(u+th)
and
t\mapsto E_{\kappa}(u+th)
are differentiable, and

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}p(u+th)\rvert_{t=0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\langle ih^{\prime},u\rangle},

\displaystyle\textup{d}E(u)[h]:=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{\kappa}(u+th)\rvert_{t=0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\langle u^{\prime},h^{\prime}\rangle}-\eta{\langle u,h\rangle}-2\kappa{\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle}{\langle u,h\rangle}.

Also for all
c\geq 0
,
c\,\textup{d}p(u)=\textup{d}E_{\kappa}(u)[h]
, for all
h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, if and only if
u
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

).

We omit the proof of Lemma
6.1
, since it is a straightforward adaptation of the computations in Lemma 6.1 in
[
25
]
.

6.1
The minimization curve

We first show that the minimization problem (
1.53
)
does not
define a real-valued function of
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{R}
, for
\kappa>0
.
Therefore, the variational problem is not well-suited to study the
dark solitons in
\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}
given by Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 6.2
.

For all
\kappa>0
and all
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=-\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\kappa>0
and
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{R}
. We define a family of functions in
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, indexed by
t\geq 0
, such that
t\mapsto E_{\kappa}(u(\cdot,t))
and
t\mapsto p(u(\cdot,t))
are continuous. More precisely, let
u=\rho e^{i\theta}
, where

\displaystyle\rho(x,t)=\begin{cases}-tx^{\alpha_{\kappa}}+t+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\kappa}}+1,\text{ for }x\in(0,1),\\
(2-x)(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\kappa}}+1)+x-1,\text{ for }x\in(1,2),\\
1,\text{ for }x\geq 2,\end{cases}\quad\theta(x,t)=\begin{cases}0,\text{ for }x\in(0,1),\\
\mathfrak{q}J_{\kappa}(x-1),\text{ for }x\in(1,2),\\
\mathfrak{q}J_{\kappa},\text{ for }x\geq 2,\end{cases}

for some
\alpha_{\kappa}>1
to be chosen later, and

J_{\kappa}=\Big(2\int_{1}^{2}(1-\rho(x,t)^{2})dx\Big)^{-1}=-3\kappa/(1+3\sqrt{2\kappa}).

We define
u(x,t)=u(-x,t)
, for all
x\leq 0
. Remark that
\partial_{x}u(t)=e^{i\theta(t)}(\partial_{x}\rho(t)+i\partial_{x}\theta(t)\rho(t))\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
for all
t\geq 0
. Also
1-\rho^{2}(t)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, for all
t\geq 0
, since it is continuous and compactly supported in
[-2,2]
. We have
\rho(t,x)\geq 1
, hence
u(\cdot,t)\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
for all
t\geq 0
. We compute, using the symmetry of
\rho
,

p(u(\cdot,t))=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1-\rho(t)^{2})\partial_{x}\theta(t)=2\int_{1}^{2}(1-\rho(t)^{2})\mathfrak{q}J_{\kappa}=\mathfrak{q}.

On the other hand, from (
1.8
) we obtain

\displaystyle E_{\kappa}(u(\cdot,t))=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}(t\alpha_{\kappa})^{2}x^{2\alpha_{k}-2}+(1-\rho^{2}(x,t))^{2}/2-2\kappa(t\alpha_{\kappa}x^{\alpha_{\kappa}-1}\rho(t,x))^{2}dx

(6.1)

\displaystyle+\int_{1}^{2}|\partial_{x}u(x,t)|^{2}+(1-\rho(x,t))^{2}/2-2\kappa(\partial_{x}\rho(x,t)\rho(x,t))^{2}dx.

(6.2)

We can prove that the second integral (
6.2
) is constant in
t
. Let us denote by
I(t)
the first integral (
6.1
).
It remains to show that, for
\alpha_{\kappa}>1
large enough,
I(t)
diverges to
-\infty
, as
t\to\infty
. Indeed,
We have
I(t)=t^{4}\beta_{k}+(t+1)^{3}R(t),

where

\beta_{k}=\Big(\frac{-4\kappa\alpha_{\kappa}^{4}}{(2\alpha_{\kappa}-1)(4\alpha_{\kappa}-1)(3\alpha_{\kappa}-1)}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\alpha_{\kappa}+1}+\frac{3}{2\alpha_{\kappa}+1}-\frac{2}{3\alpha_{\kappa}+1}+\frac{1}{8\alpha_{\kappa}+2}\Big),

and
R
is a bounded function, for
t\geq 0
. Since
\beta_{\kappa}\to-\infty
as
\alpha_{\kappa}\to\infty
, we deduce that for
\alpha_{\kappa}
large enough,
E_{\kappa}(u(\cdot,t))\to-\infty
as
t\to\infty
, which completes the proof.
∎

From now on,
we assume that
\kappa<0
, so that
the energy density
e_{\kappa}(u)
satisfies

e_{\kappa}(u)=\frac{|u^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}+\frac{|\kappa|}{4}{(\eta^{\prime})^{2}}\geq e_{0}(u)\geq 0.

(6.3)

Hence, several properties shown for the curve
E_{\min}
in
[
25
]
remain true. Indeed, the curve
E_{\min}
in
[
25
]
is associated with
the nonlocal energy

E_{\mathcal{W}}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}*\eta)\eta\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(\xi)|\widehat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi,

where
\mathcal{W}
is a tempered distribution, with bounded nonnegative Fourier transform
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
, among other hypotheses.
In our case, by using Plancherel’s theorem, we can recast the energy as

E_{\kappa}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\kappa}(\xi)|\widehat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi,\quad\text{with }\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\kappa}=1+|\kappa|\xi^{2},

for all
u\in X(\mathbb{R})
, so that
\eta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.
In this manner, we recover the potential (
1.7
) mentioned in the introduction.
Thus, formally,
\mathcal{W}
is the tempered distribution
\mathcal{W}_{\kappa}=\delta_{0}-|\kappa|(\delta_{0})^{\prime\prime}
, but we do not use this formulation, since
\eta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.
In conclusion, in most of the proofs in this subsection, we will use that
the potential energy can be written as

E_{\text{p}}(u)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}({\eta^{2}}+{|\kappa|}({\eta^{\prime}})^{2})dx,\quad\text{and }\quad E_{\text{p}}(u)=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\kappa}(\xi)|\widehat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi.

(6.4)

More precisely, we will rely on the potential energy written in the Fourier variable to invoke the arguments in
[
25
]
not needing that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
to be bounded. Of course, some arguments will be performed directly in the variable
x
, if they are simpler.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
k<0
. Then the function
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is well-defined, is even and Lipschitz continuous, with

|\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})|\leq\sqrt{2}|\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}|,\quad\text{ for all }\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{R}.

(6.5)

In particular,
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\leq\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{q}
, for all
\mathfrak{q}\geq 0
. Also
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is nondecreasing and concave on
[0,\infty)
.
Moreover,
for all
v\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
,

E_{\kappa}(v)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}),\text{ for some }\mathfrak{q}\in[0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})\ \Rightarrow v\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}).

(6.6)

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that Lemmas 3.1, 3.2, 3.4 in
[
25
]
hold with the same proofs. Corollary 3.7 is still true, and the proof is simpler, using the first expression for
E_{\text{p}}
in (
6.4
). Thus, using the notation in the proof of Corollary 3.7, we obtain

E_{\text{p}}(u_{n})=E_{\text{p}}(v_{n})+E_{\text{p}}(w_{n})
, which gives the conclusion.
As a consequence, we deduce, as in Corollary 3.8 and Proposition 3.9 in
[
25
]
, that

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=\inf\{E_{\kappa}(v):v\in\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}),\ p(v)=\mathfrak{q}\},

where

\mathcal{X}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})=\{v\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}):\exists R>0\text{ s.t.\ }v\text{ is constant on }B(0,R)^{c}\},

and that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
satisfies (
6.5
).
The fact that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is nondecreasing follows as in Lemma 3.11.

The proof of concavity is exactly the same as in Proposition 3.12, without any extra assumption needed. Indeed, using the reflection functions defined in the proof of Proposition 3.12 and the first expression in (
6.4
), it is immediate to verify that

E_{\text{p}}(u^{+})+E_{\text{p}}(u^{-})=2E_{\text{p}}(u),

so that the conclusion follows.

Finally, let
0\leq\mathfrak{q}<\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
so that, by definition of
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
, we can find
\mathfrak{q}<\check{\mathfrak{q}}<\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
satisfying

\forall v\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}),E_{\kappa}(v)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\check{\mathfrak{q}})\implies v\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}).

(6.7)

Since
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is nondecreasing on
[0,+\infty)
, we deduce that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\check{\mathfrak{q}})
, so that (
6.7
) holds with
\mathfrak{q}
instead of
\check{\mathfrak{q}}
, which proves (
6.6
).

∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Assume that
\kappa<0
.

(i)

Let
u=\rho e^{i\theta}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
and
assume that there is
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
such that

1-\varepsilon\leq|u|^{2}\leq 1+\varepsilon
on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}
. Then

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\eta\theta^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(1-\varepsilon)}{\int_{\Omega}e_{\kappa}(u)}.

(6.8)

In particular, if
1-\varepsilon\leq|u|^{2}\leq 1+\varepsilon
on
\mathbb{R}
, then
\sqrt{2}(1-\varepsilon)p(u)\leq E_{\kappa}(u).

(ii)

For any
u\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq(1+|\kappa|^{-1})E_{\kappa}(u).

(6.9)

(iii)

There is a constant
K_{0}>0

such that

\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{q}-K_{0}\mathfrak{q}^{3/2}\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}),\quad\text{for all }\mathfrak{q}\in\Bigg[0,\frac{|\kappa|}{8(1+|\kappa|)}\Bigg).

(6.10)

Proof.

Using the Cauchy inequality
ab\leq a^{2}/2+b^{2}/2
,
with
a=\eta/2
and
b=\theta^{\prime 2}
,
we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}\frac{\eta}{2}\theta^{\prime}\right|\leq\int_{\Omega}\Big(\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}+\frac{\theta^{\prime 2}}{2}\Big)\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega}\eta^{2}+\frac{1}{2(1-\varepsilon)}\int_{\Omega}\rho^{2}\theta^{\prime 2}.

(6.11)

Bearing in mind (
1.13
) and that
\kappa\leq 0
, (
6.8
) follows.

The estimate in (ii) is an immediate consequence of

\eta^{2}(x)=2\int_{-\infty}^{x}\eta\eta^{\prime}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\eta^{2}+\eta^{\prime 2})\leq 4(1-\kappa^{-1})E_{\kappa}(u).

In view of (
6.8
) and (
6.9
), the inequality in (
6.10
) follows exactly as in Proposition 3.14 in
[
25
]
.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
k<0
. We have

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})<\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{q},\quad\text{for all }\mathfrak{q}>0.

(6.12)

Proof.

In view of Proposition
6.5
, and since
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
in concave on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
we only need to prove that the strict inequality in (
6.12
) holds for
\mathfrak{q}

small. For this purpose, we will use the behavior of solitons
u_{c,\kappa}
in Proposition
5.2
-(ii)
for
c
close to
\sqrt{2}
. By setting
\varepsilon=\sqrt{2-c^{2}}
, and computing a Taylor expansion in formulas (
5.10
)–(
5.11
),
we deduce that

E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})=\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{3}\sqrt{1-2\kappa}+\frac{2\varepsilon^{5}\kappa}{15\sqrt{1-2\kappa}}+o(\varepsilon^{6}),\quad p(u_{c,\kappa})=\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{3\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{1-2\kappa}+\frac{3+2\kappa\varepsilon^{5}}{60\sqrt{2}(1-2\kappa)}+o(\varepsilon^{6}).

Therefore, there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small, such that

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(p(u_{c,\kappa}))\leq E_{\kappa}(u_{c,\kappa})<\sqrt{2}p(u_{c,\kappa})
, for all
c\in(c(\varepsilon_{0}),\sqrt{2})
, where
c(\varepsilon_{0})=(2-\varepsilon_{0}^{2})^{1/2}
.
By Corollary
5.9
, we can assume that

p_{\kappa}
is a diffeomorphism from
(c(\varepsilon_{0}),\sqrt{2})
to
(0,p_{\kappa}(c(\varepsilon_{0}))
.
Thus, we conclude that there exists
\mathfrak{q}_{0}>0
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})<\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{q}
,
for all
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,p_{\kappa}(c(\varepsilon_{0}))
.
∎

We can prove now the minimizing curve is strictly subadditive, which is the crucial property to deduce the compactness of minimizing sequences in Theorem
6.8
.

Corollary 6.6
.

If
\kappa<0
, then
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is strictly subadditive on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Proof.

From Propositions
6.3
and
6.4
-(iii), we deduce that
the right derivative of
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
at the origin, denoted by
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}^{+}
, exists and that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}^{+}(0)=\sqrt{2}
. By invoking Lemma 3.16 in
[
25
]
, Proposition
6.5

implies that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is strictly subadditive on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
∎

6.2
Compactness of minimizing sequences

We are now in a position to prove the compactness of minimizing sequences in the problem
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}),
with
\kappa<0
, and that the minimizers are the solitons
u_{c,\kappa}
, with
c=\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})
. Notice that in the rest of this section,
u_{c,\kappa}
refers to the dark soliton to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), given by Theorem
1.1
.

We will use the general argument given
[
25
]
,
based on the properties of the minimizing curve
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
and an adaptation of the concentration-compactness principle.
However, we need to guarantee the nonvanishing properties of the limit of the minimizing sequences. This is the purpose of the constant
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
defined in (
1.55
).
To relate
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
with
\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)
, let us state some properties related to the energy of the black soliton
u_{0,\kappa}
in (
1.56
), i.e. the solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=0
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\kappa<0
and
\mathfrak{q}\geq 0
.
There exists a sequence
(u_{n})\subset\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying

\displaystyle p(u_{n})=\mathfrak{q},\ \text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}E_{\kappa}(u_{n})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa}).

(6.13)

In addition,

\inf\{E_{\kappa}(v):v\in H^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}),~\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|v(x)|=0\}=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa}).

(6.14)

In particular, for all
\mathfrak{q}>0
, we have
0\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\leq E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
and
\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)\leq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
.

Proof.

The existence of a sequence
(u_{n})
satisfying (
6.13
) is analogous to the case
\kappa=0
, done in Proposition 3.4 in

[
6
]
.

The minimization problem (
6.14
) correspond to Lemma 1 in
[
7
]
in the case
\kappa=0
. In the case
\kappa<0
, the same proof holds, using that

u_{0,\kappa}
is the unique solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) that vanishes at some point, up to a translation.

Finally, let us show that (
6.14
) implies that

\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)\leq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
. Let
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa))
and let
v\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R})
such that

E_{\kappa}(v)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})
. By (
5.21
), we deduce that

E_{\kappa}(v)\leq E_{\kappa}(u_{\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa})
, with
\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\in(c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2})
.
Since, by definition,
E_{\kappa}(u_{\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa})=E_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{c}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}))
, and, by Lemma
5.7
, the map
c\mapsto E_{\kappa}(c)
is strictly decreasing on
[c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2}]
, we get

E_{\kappa}(v)<E_{\kappa}(c_{\kappa}^{*})
. By Corollary
5.9
, we have

E_{\kappa}(c_{\kappa}^{*})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
, so that

E_{\kappa}(v)<E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
. Thus, using (
6.14
),
we infer that
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}|v|>0
. This implies that
\mathfrak{q}\leq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
,
which completes the proof.
∎

In order to obtain the stability of minimizers, we will prove a more general result than the one stated in Theorem
1.10
, as follows.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
\kappa<0
,

\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa))
and
(u_{n})
in
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
be a sequence satisfying

E_{\kappa}(u_{n})\to\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\quad\text{ and }\quad p(u_{n})\to\mathfrak{q},\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

(6.15)

There exist
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
a sequence of points
(x_{n})

such that, up to a subsequence still denoted by
u_{n}
,
the following convergences hold, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle u_{n}(\cdot+x_{n})

\displaystyle\to e^{i\theta}u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot),

in

\displaystyle L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}),

(6.16)

\displaystyle 1-|u_{n}(\cdot+x_{n})|^{2}

\displaystyle\to 1-|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot)|^{2},

in

\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

(6.17)

\displaystyle u_{n}^{\prime}(\cdot+x_{n})

\displaystyle\to e^{i\theta}u^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot),

in

\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

(6.18)

Moreover, we have
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}=\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)
,

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=E_{\kappa}(u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa})
for
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
, and

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
for
\mathfrak{q}\geq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
. In particular,

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is strictly increasing on
(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
.

Proof.

As explained before, the proof follows the same steps as in Theorem 4.1 in
[
25
]
, without the extra decomposition introduced to handle the nonlocal interactions. The characterization of the limit function is obtained by invoking Corollary
5.9
.
Therefore, we only give a sketch of the proof.

First, using Proposition
6.5
, we can set
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{q}}=1-{E_{\textup{min}}(\mathfrak{q})}/{(\sqrt{2}\mathfrak{q})}\in(0,1)
, for any
\mathfrak{q}>0
.
In addition, without loss of generality, we can assume that

E_{\kappa}(u_{n})\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}).

(6.19)

Thus, we can apply Lemmas 2.4 and 2.5 in
[
25
]
, with
with
L=1+\Sigma_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
E=2\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})
and
m_{0}=\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{q}}:=\Sigma_{\mathfrak{q}}/L
,
to deduce that there exist
R>0
, two integers
\ell,l_{*}
, with
1\leq\ell\leq l_{*}
, depending on
E
and
\mathfrak{q}
, but not on
n
,
and points
x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n},\dots,x_{l_{*}}
, satisfying

|x_{k}^{n}-x_{j}^{n}|\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty,\text{ for }1\leq k\neq j\leq\ell,\text{ and }\ x_{j}^{n}\in\displaystyle\mathop{\cup}_{k=1}^{\ell}B(x_{k}^{n},R),\text{ for }\ell<j\leq l_{*}.

In addition, the sequence
\eta_{n}=1-|u_{n}|^{2}
satisfies

|\eta_{n}(x_{j}^{n})|\geq\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{q}},\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq\ell,\quad\text{ and }\quad|\eta_{n}(x)|\leq\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{q}},\ \forall x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{\ell}B(x_{j}^{n},R+1).

(6.20)

Applying standard weak compactness results for Hilbert spaces, the Rellich–Kondrachov theorem, and Fatou’s lemma, to the translated sequence
u_{n,j}(\cdot)=u_{n}(\cdot+x_{j}^{n})
,
we infer that there exist functions
v_{j}=\rho_{j}e^{i\phi_{j}}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
,
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
, satisfying, up to a subsequence,

\displaystyle u_{n,j}\to v_{j},\text{ in }L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}),\ u^{\prime}_{n,j}\rightharpoonup v_{j}^{\prime},\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\ \eta_{n,j}=1-|u_{n,j}|^{2}\rightharpoonup\eta_{j}=1-|v_{j}|^{2},\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

(6.21)

as
n\to\infty
, and also,

\displaystyle\int_{-A}^{A}|v_{j}^{\prime}|^{2}\leq\underset{n\to\infty}{\liminf}\int_{-A}^{A}|u_{n,j}^{\prime}|^{2},\ \text{ for all }A\in(0,\infty],

(6.22)

\displaystyle\int_{-A}^{A}({\eta_{j}^{2}}+{|\kappa|}({\eta_{j}^{\prime}})^{2})\leq\underset{n\to\infty}{\liminf}\int_{-A}^{A}({\eta_{n,j}^{2}}+{|\kappa|}({\eta_{n,j}^{\prime}})^{2}),\text{ for all }A\in(0,\infty],

(6.23)

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\int_{-A}^{A}\eta_{n,j}\phi_{n,j}^{\prime}=\int_{-A}^{A}\eta_{j}\phi_{j}^{\prime},\text{ for all }A\in(0,\infty),

(6.24)

as well as

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}_{j})\leq E_{\kappa}(v_{j})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}),

(6.25)

where
u_{n,j}=\rho_{n,j}e^{i\phi_{n,j}}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{j}=p(v_{j})
.
Notice that the fact that
v_{j}
belongs to
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, follows from
(
6.6
) and (
6.25
), since
\mathfrak{q}<\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)\leq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}.

We also remark that we only have an inequality in (
6.23
), whereas we had an equality in the potential energy in (4.25) in
[
25
]
.
The rest of the proof consists in showing the following steps.

Step 1
.

There exist
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{E}\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\geq\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}_{j})+\tilde{E}\quad\textit{ and }\quad\mathfrak{q}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathfrak{q}_{j}+\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}.

(6.26)

The proof of the first inequality is the same as in
[
25
]
, handling
the potential energy in the same manner as the kinetic energy in (
6.22
).
For the momentum, we use inequality (
6.8
), instead of Lemma 2.3 in
[
25
]
. Thus, there is no need to introduce cut-off functions that caused the appearance of some reminder terms.

Step 2
.

We have

\sqrt{2}\left(1-\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathfrak{q}}\right)|\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}|\leq\tilde{E}.

(6.27)

This inequality follows as in Claim 2 in
[
25
]
, but using
inequality (
6.8
), instead of introducing cut-off functions.

Step 3
.

We have
\tilde{E}=\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}=0
and
\ell=1
.

The proof of this step is the same as in Claim 3 in
[
25
]
, since it only uses the properties of the function
\mathcal{E}_{k}
in Propositions
6.3
,
6.5
and Corollary
6.6
.

Step 4
.

The weak convergences in (
6.21
) are also strong in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
(for
j=1
).

We set from now on
v=v_{1}
and
\eta=1-|v_{1}|^{2}
.
For the previous step, we have

p(u_{n,1})\to\mathfrak{q}=p(v)\quad\text{ and }\quad E_{\kappa}(u_{n,1})\to E_{\textup{min}}(\mathfrak{q})=E_{\kappa}(v).

(6.28)

To prove that
u^{\prime}_{n,1}\to v^{\prime}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, it is enough to show that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n,1}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|v^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(6.29)

Arguing by contradiction, taking a subsequence that we still denote by
u_{n,1}
,
we suppose that

M:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert u_{n,1}^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},\quad\text{ with }\quad M>\|v^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Hence, using (
6.28
),

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}~E_{\textup{p}}\left(u_{n,1}\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}~\big(E_{\kappa}(u_{n,1})-\lVert u_{n,1}^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}/2\big)=E_{\kappa}(v)-{M}/{2}<E_{\kappa}(v)-\|v^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}/2=E_{\textup{p}}(v),

which contradicts (
6.23
) (with
A=\infty
). Therefore,
u^{\prime}_{n,1}\to v^{\prime}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Let us show that this also implies that

\lVert\eta_{n,1}^{\prime}-\eta^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0.

(6.30)

Indeed, noticing that
\eta^{\prime}-\eta_{n,1}^{\prime}=2(\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle-\langle u_{n,1},u_{n,1}^{\prime}\rangle)
,
we have

\displaystyle\lVert\eta_{n,1}^{\prime}-\eta^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|(v-u_{n,1})v^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+2\|(v^{\prime}-u_{n,1}^{\prime})u_{n,1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(6.31)

From inequality (
6.9
), we obtain the existence of a constant
C(\mathfrak{q})>0
such that
\lVert u_{n,1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C(\mathfrak{q}).
Thus, the second term in the right-hand side of (
6.31
) goes to zero. By using the dominated convergence theorem, we also infer that the other term in the right-hand side of (
6.31
) also tends to zero, which completes the proof of (
6.30
).

Finally, going back to (
6.28
), we conclude that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\eta_{n,1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\|\eta\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

which combined with the last weak convergence in (
6.21
) (with
j=1
), implies that
\eta_{n,1}\to\eta
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Step 5
.

There exists
(\theta,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
v=e^{i\theta}u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot-y)
.

By Theorem 6.3 in
[
25
]
, since
p(v)=\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=E_{\kappa}(v)
,
we conclude that
v
is a solution to (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

), for some speed
c
. In addition, as in Theorem 4 in
[
25
]
, we deduce that
c\in(0,\sqrt{2})
. Therefore,
by Corollary
5.9
,
v
satisfies (
TW

(
c
,
κ
)

) with
c=\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q})
, and the conclusion follows from Theorem
1.1
.

Step 6
.

We have
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}=\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)
and
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
, for all
\mathfrak{q}\geq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
.

By continuity of
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
, invoking Corollary
5.9
, and using the previous step, we deduce that

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa))=\lim_{\mathfrak{q}\to\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)^{-}}\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})=\lim_{\mathfrak{q}\to\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)^{-}}E_{\kappa}(u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa})=E_{\kappa}(u_{c^{*}_{\kappa},\kappa})=E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})

(6.32)

where we used the definition (
1.57
) for the equality. Since

u_{0,\kappa}(0)=0
, we conclude, using the definition of
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
, that
\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa)\geq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
. By Lemma
6.7
, we have
\mathfrak{q}_{0}\leq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
, hence,
\mathfrak{q}_{0}=\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
.

Finally, we show that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is constant in
[\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*},\infty)
.
Indeed, for
\mathfrak{q}\geq\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
. using that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is nondecreasing,
and (
6.32
),
we have
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})\geq\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q}_{0}(\kappa))\geq E_{\kappa}(u_{0,\kappa})
.
Since the reverse inequality was already proved in Lemma
6.7
,
we conclude that the equality holds.

At last, in view of Lemma
5.7
, and Steps 5 and 6,
we conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is strictly increasing on
(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
, which finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

7
Local well-posedness of (
QGP
) and orbital stability

The Cauchy problem with vanishing conditions at infinity associated with (
1.2
) is locally well-posed in Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
of high regularity. This was shown for (
1.2
) in
[
14
,
16
]
, for
s\geq 3
.
Best regularity results were obtained in
[
44
]
in
l^{1}H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
spaces, containing
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, with
s>1/2+2
, and recently improved in
[
33
,
55
]
.

Concerning (
QGP
), we can decompose
\Psi
as
\Psi=v+\varphi
, where
v
satisfies condition (
1.1
) and
\varphi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
. Then, we obtain a quasilinear equation on
\varphi
with vanishing conditions. In this setting, it is not straightforward to show well-posedness in
v+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
,
using the latter results. This is due to the nonhomogenous nature of the equation on
\varphi
. However, more general quasilinear models have been considered in
[
35
]
. Their results provide local well-posedness in
v+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\cap H_{w}(\mathbb{R})
, for any
s\geq 1/2+11
, with some additional smoothness and decay assumptions on
v
, where
H_{w}(\mathbb{R})
is some weighted Sobolev space.

For our purposes, we will use the approach developed in
[
4
]
, where they proved that the Euler–Korteweg system is locally well-posed. As shown now, (
QGP
) can be written as an Euler–Korteweg system, with initial conditions given by
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
-perturbations of a dark soliton for
s\geq 1
.

Assume that
\kappa\leq 0
and
c>0
.
Let
\Psi_{0}=u_{c,\kappa}+\phi_{0}
satisfying
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\Psi_{0}|>0
, where
\phi_{0}\in{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
with
s\geq 1
and
u_{c,\kappa}
is the dark soliton given by Theorem
1.1
.
Since
\Psi_{0}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, then for any local solution
\Psi\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];u_{c,\kappa}+{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}))
, we deduce that
there exists
T>0
such that

\Psi(t)
also belongs to
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, for all
t\in[0,T]
, so that we can perform the Madelung transform
[
11
]
. Namely, writing

\Psi=\sqrt{\rho}e^{i\theta}
, with
\rho(t)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
and
\theta(t)\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
We conclude that
(\rho,\theta)
satisfies in
[0,T]
the system

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho-2\partial_{x}(\partial_{x}\theta\rho)=0,\\
&-\partial_{t}\theta+(\partial_{x}\theta)^{2}=\frac{1-2\kappa\rho}{2\rho}\partial_{xx}\rho-\frac{(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}}{4\rho^{2}}+1-\rho.\end{aligned}\right.

(7.1)

Therefore, setting

\tilde{\rho}(x,t)=\rho(x,-t/2),\ \ \tilde{v}(x,t)=\partial_{x}(\theta(x,-t/2)),\ \ K(y)=(1-2\kappa y)/4y,\ \ g_{0}(y)=(y-1)/2,

(7.2)

for all
y>0
, we deduce that

(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{v})
satisfies the Euler–Korteweg system in
[0,T]
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\tilde{\rho}+\partial_{x}(\tilde{v}\tilde{\rho})=0,\\
&\partial_{t}\tilde{v}+\tilde{v}\partial_{x}\tilde{v}=\partial_{x}\Big(K(\tilde{\rho})\partial_{xx}\tilde{\rho}+\frac{K^{\prime}(\tilde{\rho})}{2}(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}-g_{0}(\tilde{\rho})\Big).\end{aligned}\right.

(7.3)

The result established in
[
4
]
concerning the general Euler–Korteweg system (
7.3
) is as follows.

Theorem 7.1

(Theorem 5.1 in
[
4
]
)
.

Take
s>2+1/2
. Let
\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
be a function taking values in a compact subset of
(J_{-},J_{+})\subset(0,\infty)
.
Assume that
K,g_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, with
K>0
in
(J_{-},J_{+}).

If
v_{0}\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R})
, then there exists
T>0
such that (
7.3
) has a unique solution
(\rho,v)
on
\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]
, satisfying
\rho(\cdot,0)=\rho_{0}
,
v(\cdot,0)=v_{0}
, and

\begin{cases}(\rho-\rho_{0})\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R})),\\
(\partial_{x}\rho,v)\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R})),\\
\rho(\mathbb{R}\times[0,T])\subset\subset(J_{-},J_{+}).\end{cases}

(7.4)

Also, the flow map is continuous in a neighborhood of
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
in
(\rho_{0}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R})
.

For all
\kappa\leq 0
, it is clear that
K
given in (
7.2
) satisfies

K(y)>0,
for all
y>0
, so that we can apply Theorem
7.1
with
(J_{-},J_{+})=(0,\infty).

In the next result, we obtain the local well-posedness of (
QGP
) for small
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
-perturbations of
u_{c,\kappa}
using Theorem
7.1
and defining
\Psi=\rho e^{i\theta}\in u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
such that (
7.1
)–(
7.2
) hold. Also, we deduce the continuity of the flow map and the conservation of energy and momentum.

Corollary 7.2

(Well-posedness of (
QGP
))
.

Let
s>1/2+2
,
\kappa<0
and
c>0
. If
\Psi_{0}\in u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
belongs to

\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
,
then there exists
T_{\Psi_{0}}>0
, the maximal time of existence such that for every
T\in(0,T_{\Psi_{0}})
, (
QGP
) has a unique solution
\Psi
on
\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]
, satisfying
\Psi(\cdot,0)=\Psi_{0}
and

\begin{cases}\Psi\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})),\\
\inf_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]}|\Psi|>0.\end{cases}

(7.5)

Moreover, the flow map is continuous in a neighborhood of
\Psi_{0}
in
u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, and the energy and momentum are conserved, i.e.
E_{\kappa}(\Psi(\cdot,t))=E_{\kappa}(\Psi_{0})
and
p(\Psi(\cdot,t))=p(\Psi_{0})
,
for all
t\in[0,T].

Proof.

Existence.

We start by performing the Madelung transform to the initial condition:
\Psi_{0}=\sqrt{\rho_{0}}e^{i\theta_{0}}
. Then, setting
v_{0}=\partial_{x}\theta_{0}
,
we can apply Theorem
7.1
to obtain
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{v})
a solution to (
7.3
) satisfying (
7.4
), with the functions

K
and
g_{0}
defined in (
7.2
).
Setting
\rho(x,t)=\tilde{\rho}(x,-2t)
and
v=v(x,-2t)
, it remains to define
\theta\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,T])
so that
\Psi=\sqrt{\rho}e^{i\theta}
satisfies (
QGP
) and (
7.5
).
Indeed, taking

\theta(\cdot,t)=\theta_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}v^{2}-\frac{1-2\kappa\rho}{2\rho}\partial_{xx}\rho-\frac{(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}}{4\rho^{2}}+\rho-1,

yields
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, thus
\theta
satisfies the second equation in (
7.3
). Since
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}\rho>0
, we deduce that
\sqrt{\rho}e^{i\theta}
is a nonvanishing local solution to (
QGP
). We verify that
\Psi
is still a
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})-
perturbation of
u_{c,\kappa}
for
t>0
to ensure (
7.5
). Since
\Psi_{0}-u_{c,\kappa}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, we can conclude if we prove that
\Psi-\Psi_{0}\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))
. It is easy to check that
\partial_{x}(\Psi-\Psi_{0})
belongs in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}))
using (
7.4
), thus, we just need to verify that
\Psi-\Psi_{0}\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R})).

Since
(\rho,\theta)
satisfies (
7.1
), we deduce using (
7.4
) that
\partial_{t}\theta\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}))
, in particular
\theta-\theta_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and we see that
\Psi-\Psi_{0}\in\mathcal{C}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
follows from this observation and (
7.4
).

Uniqueness.
Assume that
\check{\rho}e^{i\check{\theta}}
is another solution to (
QGP
) defined on
[0,\check{T}]
with the same initial condition, then
\tilde{T}=\min\{\check{T},T\}
and relabeling
\tilde{T}
as
T
, we have
\check{\rho}=\rho
and
\partial_{x}\theta=\partial_{x}\check{\theta}
in
[0,T]
by Theorem
7.1
. Since
\check{\rho}e^{i\check{\theta}}
satisfies (
QGP
), we infer that
\check{\theta}
satisfies the second line in (
7.3
). Therefore, we deduce that
\partial_{t}\check{\theta}=\partial_{t}\theta
in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, so that, integrating the latter identity, we obtain
\check{\theta}=\theta
. We conclude using Theorem
7.1
that there exists
T
such that the flow map is well-defined in the vicinity of any initial condition
\Psi_{0}\in u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}|\Psi_{0}|>0
. Moreover, this map takes its value in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Continuity with respect to the initial data.
Let
\Psi_{0}\in u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}|\Psi_{0}|>0
, then the flow map is well-defined in a neighborhood
\mathcal{V}
of
\Psi_{0}
and valued in
\mathcal{C}([0,T],u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))
, for some
T>0
. By simplicity, we only show that this map is continuous at
\Psi_{0}
, the continuity in
\mathcal{V}
is similar. Let
(\Psi_{0}^{(n)})\subset\mathcal{V}
satisfying
||\Psi_{0}^{(n)}-\Psi_{0}||_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Let
\Psi^{(n)}=\rho^{(n)}e^{i\theta^{(n)}}
and
\Psi=\rho e^{i\theta}
be the unique solution of (
QGP
) in
\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]
satisfying (
7.5
) and
\Psi^{(n)}(\cdot,0)=\Psi_{0}^{(n)}
and
\Psi(\cdot,0)=\Psi_{0}
. By the continuity of the flow map in Theorem
7.1
we get

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}||(\rho^{(n)},\partial_{x}\theta^{(n)})-(\rho,\partial_{x}\theta)||_{\mathcal{C}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}))}=0.

(7.6)

Since
\theta^{(n)}
and
\theta
satisfies the second equation in (
7.1
), we deduce using (
7.6
) that
||\partial_{t}\theta^{(n)}-\partial_{t}\theta||_{\mathcal{C}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
, thus
||\theta^{(n)}-\theta||_{\mathcal{C}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
. We see that this relation and (
7.6
) are enough to ensure that
||\rho^{(n)}e^{i\theta^{(n)}}-\rho e^{i\theta}||_{\mathcal{C}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, hence the flow map is continuous in
\Psi_{0}
.

Conservation laws.
Assume that
\Psi_{0}
satisfies
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}|\Psi_{0}|>0
and is regular enough so that the solution
\Psi
of (
QGP
) belongs in
\mathcal{C}([0,T];u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))
, for some
s>4+1/2
. Then the energy and momentum of
\Psi
are conserved in time (one just needs to derive with respect to time in the integrals).
The conclusion follows for
s>2+1/2
, by
using a density argument and the continuity of the flow map.

As a conclusion, the following the maximal time of existence is well-defined:

T_{\Psi_{0}}=\sup\left\{T>0:\quad\begin{aligned} &\text{There exists a unique solution to \eqref{QGP} $\Psi$ defined on $[0,T]$}\\
&\text{satisfying \eqref{lwp} and $\Psi(\cdot,0)=\Psi_{0}$.}\end{aligned}\right\},

(7.7)

associated with an initial condition
\Psi_{0}\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})\cap u_{c,\kappa}+H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
.
∎

Finally, we can prove the stability of dark solitons, as stated in
Theorem
1.12
, by invoking Theorem
6.8
, and the Cazenave–Lions argument
[
12
]
.

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

Using that
\mathfrak{c}
is a bijection between
(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
and
(c_{\kappa}^{*},\sqrt{2})
, we just need to show the result for
u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}
, parametrized by
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
.
By contradiction, we
suppose that for some
\mathfrak{q}\in(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}),
the dark soliton
u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}
is not orbitally stable. Then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and a sequence
(v^{(n)}_{0})\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
,
s>5/2
,
such that
the solution
\Psi^{(n)}(x,t)=u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(x)+v^{(n)}(x,t)
to (
QGP
), with initial data
\Psi^{(n)}_{0}=u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}+v^{(n)}_{0}
, defined for
t\in[0,T_{\Psi_{0}^{(n)}})
, satisfies

\displaystyle d(\Psi^{(n)}_{0},u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa})<1/n,

(7.8)

\displaystyle\text{and }\quad\inf_{(y,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle d(\Psi^{(n)}(t_{n}),u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot-y)e^{i\theta})>\varepsilon_{0},

(7.9)

for some
t_{n}\in(0,T_{\Psi_{0}^{(n)}}).

Let us recall that Lemma 5.5 in
[
25
]
establishes that
if
v_{n},v\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

d(v_{n},v)\to 0
,
then,

\||v_{n}|-|v|\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\||v_{n}|^{2}-|v|^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0.

(7.10)

In particular, this implies the continuity of the energy
E_{\kappa}(v_{n})\to E_{\kappa}(v)
(with respect to
d
). In addition, if
v_{n},v\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, then we also have the continuity of the momentum, i.e. that
p(v_{n})\to p(v)
. By conservation of energy, we have

\displaystyle E_{\kappa}(\Psi^{(n)}(t))=E_{\kappa}(\Psi_{0}^{(n)}),\quad\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq t_{n}.

(7.11)

From (
7.11
), we infer that, up to a subsequence that we do not relabel, we have
\Psi^{(n)}(t)\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
for all
0\leq t\leq t_{n}
. Indeed, in view of (
7.8
) and the continuity of the energy with respect to
d
, we obtain,

\displaystyle E_{\kappa}(\Psi^{(n)}_{0})<E_{\kappa}(u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa})+\delta_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})+\delta_{n},

(7.12)

for some positive sequence
\delta_{n}\to 0
. Thus, for
n
large enough, using (
7.11
)–(
7.12
) and that
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}
is strictly increasing in
(0,\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
(see Theorem
6.8
), we deduce that there is
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}\in(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*})
such that

E_{\kappa}(\Psi^{(n)}(t))<\mathcal{E}(\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}),\quad\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq t_{n}.

(7.13)

By the definition of
\mathfrak{q}_{\kappa}^{*}
in (
1.55
), we deduce that
\Psi^{(n)}(t)\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R})
, for all
0\leq t\leq t_{n}
. We can now invoke the conservation of momentum and the continuity of the momentum (with respect to
d
), to conclude that
p(\Psi^{(n)}(t_{n}))\to\mathfrak{q}
. Again, using (
7.11
) and the continuity of the energy, we obtain
E_{\kappa}(\Psi^{(n)}(t_{n}))\to\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{q})
.
From Theorem
6.8
, we deduce that there exist
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and a sequence of points
(x_{n})
such that

\displaystyle 1-|\Psi^{(n)}(\cdot+x_{n},t_{n})|^{2}\to 1-|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}|^{2},\quad\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

(7.14)

\displaystyle(\Psi^{(n)})^{\prime}(\cdot+x_{n},t_{n})\to e^{i\theta}u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}^{\prime},\quad\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

(7.15)

Let us show that (
7.14
)–(
7.15
) imply that

d(\Psi^{(n)}(t_{n}),e^{i\theta}u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot-x_{n}))\to 0.

(7.16)

Indeed, it is immediate that (
7.15
) leads to
||(\Psi^{(n)})^{\prime}(t_{n})-e^{i\theta}u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}^{\prime}(\cdot-x_{n})||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
. In addition, we have the estimate:

\displaystyle\lVert|\Psi^{(n)}|-|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot-x_{n})|\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\frac{\lVert|\Psi^{(n)}|^{2}-|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot-x_{n})|^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{\inf_{\mathbb{R}}((|\Psi^{(n)}|+|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot-x_{n})|)^{2})}.

(7.17)

Since
0\leq 1-|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}|^{2}\leq 1-\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q})^{2}/2
(see Proposition
3.7
), we deduce that
|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}|\geq\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q})/\sqrt{2}
. Therefore, using (
7.17
) and (
7.14
), we conclude that
\lVert|\Psi^{(n)}|-|u_{\mathfrak{c}(\mathfrak{q}),\kappa}(\cdot-x_{n})|\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
, which establishes (
7.16
). This contradicts (
7.9
).
∎
